# C Programming Notes — Complete Chat Summary

## 1. Difference Between `float` and `double`

### Example 1: Using `double`

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    double a = 2187;
    printf("%lf", a);
    return 0;
}
```

**Output:**
```text
2187.000000
```

### Example 2: Using `float`

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    float a = 2187;
    printf("%f", a);
    return 0;
}
```

**Output:**
```text
2187.000000
```

### What is the difference?

- `float` usually occupies 4 bytes and provides about 6–7 significant decimal digits of precision.
- `double` usually occupies 8 bytes and provides about 15–16 significant decimal digits of precision.
- Both can represent the integer 2187 exactly.
- `%f` is used to print both `float` and `double` values with `printf`.
- `%lf` also works with `printf`, but the `l` has no effect in this case.

**Important:** In `scanf`, use `%f` for `float *` and `%lf` for `double *`.

---

## 2. Assigning 256 to a `char`

### Example

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    char a = 256;
    printf("%c", a);
    return 0;
}
```

### Will it compile and run?

It may compile and run, depending on the implementation. Compilation and execution do not guarantee that the result is portable or predictable.

- A typical `char` is 8 bits.
- A typical signed 8-bit `char` has a range of -128 to 127.
- An unsigned 8-bit `char` has a range of 0 to 255.

The value 256 does not fit in either of those ranges.

When an integer outside the range is converted to an unsigned 8-bit character type, the result is reduced modulo 256. Thus, 256 becomes 0.

When it is converted to a signed `char` that cannot represent the value, the result is implementation-defined, or the implementation may raise an implementation-defined signal.

On some common systems, the resulting value may be 0. Printing it with `%c` may produce no visible character because it is the null character.

**Remember:** A program running without an error does not mean every operation in it is portable or well-defined.

---

## 3. Assigning 129 to a `signed char`

### Example

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    signed char a = 129;
    printf("%c", a);
    return 0;
}
```

A typical 8-bit `signed char` has a range of -128 to 127.

The value 129 is outside that range, so converting it to `signed char` has an implementation-defined result or may raise an implementation-defined signal.

On many common systems using 8-bit two's-complement representation, the stored result is -127.

The `%c` format specifier prints a character corresponding to the argument after conversion to `unsigned char`. That character might not be printable.

**Important:** This is not necessarily a compilation error. The compiler can accept the code even though the conversion is implementation-defined.

---

## 4. Character Literal vs Numeric Value in a `char`

### Example 1: Assigning a character

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    char a = 'h';
    printf("%d", a);
    return 0;
}
```

**Output on an ASCII-compatible system:**
```text
104
```

Why?

- `'h'` is a character constant.
- Its value on an ASCII-compatible system is 104.
- That value is stored in `a`.
- `%d` prints the integer value of `a`.

### Example 2: Assigning a number

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    char a = 125;
    printf("%d", a);
    return 0;
}
```

**Output:**
```text
125
```

Why?

- The numeric value 125 is assigned directly to `a`.
- The value fits in a typical 8-bit `char`.
- `%d` prints the numeric value, which is 125.

### What is the main difference?

| Code | Meaning | Output |
|---|---|---|
| `char a = 'h';` | Assign the character `h` | `104` with `%d` on ASCII-compatible systems |
| `char a = 125;` | Assign the numeric value 125 | `125` with `%d` |

A `char` stores an integer value representing a character code or another small integer. It does not automatically store a special kind of data separate from numbers.

**Remember:**
- `%d` prints a decimal integer.
- `%c` prints the corresponding character.

---

## 5. Difference Between `char` and `int` with `%c`

### Example 1: `char`

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    char a = 65;
    printf("%c", a);
    return 0;
}
```

### Example 2: `int`

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int a = 65;
    printf("%c", a);
    return 0;
}
```

**Output for both on an ASCII-compatible system:**
```text
A
```

### Why is the output the same?

- In the first program, `a` is a `char` containing the value 65.
- In the second program, `a` is an `int` containing the value 65.
- `%c` tells `printf` to print a character corresponding to the argument.

On an ASCII-compatible system, character code 65 represents `A`.

### Main difference

| `char` | `int` |
|---|---|
| Usually occupies 1 byte | Commonly occupies 4 bytes |
| Typically used for characters and small integer values | Used for ordinary integer calculations |
| Has a smaller range | Has a larger range |

The output is the same here because both values are 65, not because the data types are identical.

---

## 6. Difference Between Bitwise AND (`&`) and Logical AND (`&&`)

### Bitwise AND (`&`)

It compares the individual bits of two integer operands.

Example:

```c
int a = 5;
int b = 3;

printf("%d", a & b);
```

Binary representation:

```text
a = 5 = 0101
b = 3 = 0011
        ----
a & b = 0001 = 1
```

**Output:**
```text
1
```

The result is 1 because only the rightmost bit is 1 in both operands.

### Logical AND (`&&`)

It checks whether both expressions are true.

Example:

```c
int x = 5;
int y = 3;

printf("%d", x > 0 && y > 0);
```

Both conditions are true, so the output is:

```text
1
```

In C:
- `0` represents false in a logical condition.
- Any nonzero value represents true.
- A logical operator produces either 0 or 1.
- `&&` uses short-circuit evaluation: if the left operand is false, the right operand is not evaluated.

### Main difference

| Bitwise AND (`&`) | Logical AND (`&&`) |
|---|---|
| Operates on individual bits | Checks logical truth |
| Produces a bitwise integer result | Produces 0 or 1 |
| Commonly used for bit manipulation | Commonly used in conditions |

---

## 7. Left-Shift (`<<`) and Right-Shift (`>>`)

### Program

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int x = 19;

    printf("x << 1 = %d\n", x << 1);
    printf("x >> 1 = %d\n", x >> 1);

    return 0;
}
```

**Output:**
```text
x << 1 = 38
x >> 1 = 9
```

### First, convert 19 to binary

```text
19 = 00010011
```

These are the 8 lowest bits for illustration. An actual `int` commonly has 32 bits.

### A. Left-shift: `x << 1`

Move all bits one position to the left. For this positive number, a zero enters from the right.

```text
Before: 00010011 = 19
After:  00100110 = 38
```

Therefore:

```text
19 << 1 = 38
```

For a positive integer such as 19, this is equivalent to multiplying by 2.

### B. Right-shift: `x >> 1`

Move all bits one position to the right.

```text
Before: 00010011 = 19
After:  00001001 = 9
```

Therefore:

```text
19 >> 1 = 9
```

For a nonnegative integer, right-shifting by one is equivalent to integer division by 2, discarding the remainder.

### Remember

- `<<` shifts bits to the left.
- `>>` shifts bits to the right.
- Shifting is not universally equivalent to multiplication or division for every integer value. In particular, signed negative values and shifts that exceed the permitted range require care.

---

## 8. Difference Between a Character Array and a String

In C, a string is represented by a character sequence terminated by the null character `'\0'`.

### Character array

```c
char a[] = {'H', 'e', 'l', 'l', 'o'};
```

This array contains five characters, but it does not have a terminating null character.

It is a character array, but it is not a valid null-terminated C string.

You can access its elements individually:

```c
printf("%c", a[0]);
```

Output:

```text
H
```

Do not pass this array to `%s` or ordinary string functions that expect a null-terminated string.

### String

```c
char a[] = "Hello";
```

The compiler automatically adds a null character.

The array contains:

```text
Index:  0   1   2   3   4    5
Value: 'H' 'e' 'l' 'l' 'o' '\0'
```

You can print it using:

```c
printf("%s", a);
```

Output:

```text
Hello
```

### Main difference

A character array is an array of characters. A C string is a character sequence that ends with `'\0'`.

---

## 9. Structure Members and Union Members

Structures and unions allow you to group different data types under one type name.

### A. Structure

```c
struct Student {
    int age;
    float marks;
    char grade;
};
```

Each member has its own storage within the structure.

Example:

```c
struct Student s;

s.age = 20;
s.marks = 85.5f;
s.grade = 'A';
```

All three values can exist at the same time.

A structure's size is at least large enough to hold all its members, and it may include additional padding for alignment.

### B. Union

```c
union Data {
    int i;
    float f;
    char c;
};
```

All members share the same storage.

Example:

```c
union Data d;

d.i = 10;
d.f = 3.5f;
```

After assigning `d.f`, the union's stored representation is the one used for the `float` member. Reading a different member may produce a representation-dependent result and must be handled carefully.

### Main difference

| Structure | Union |
|---|---|
| Each member has separate storage | Members share storage |
| Members can hold independent values simultaneously | Normally, only one member's value is intended to be active at a time |
| Size accounts for all members and padding | Size is sufficient for the largest member and alignment requirements |

### When should you use them?

Use a structure when you need to store several related values simultaneously.

Use a union when different values can occupy the same storage at different times.

---

## 10. How an Integer Is Stored in Memory

Consider:

```c
int number = 42;
```

On a system where `int` is 4 bytes, the integer occupies four bytes of memory.

### Step 1: Convert 42 to binary

```text
42 = 00101010
```

In a typical 32-bit `int`, the full representation is:

```text
00000000 00000000 00000000 00101010
```

### Step 2: Store the bytes

The four bytes are:

```text
00 00 00 2A
```

Here, `2A` is hexadecimal for decimal 42.

### Little-endian system

The least significant byte is stored at the lowest address.

```text
Address     Stored byte
0x1000      2A
0x1001      00
0x1002      00
0x1003      00
```

### Big-endian system

The most significant byte is stored at the lowest address.

```text
Address     Stored byte
0x1000      00
0x1001      00
0x1002      00
0x1003      2A
```

The addresses above are illustrative.

### Important points

- One byte commonly consists of 8 bits.
- The size of `int` is implementation-dependent; it is commonly 4 bytes.
- Endianness describes the order in which a multibyte value's bytes are stored.
- Memory addresses are managed within a program's address space.
- Alignment can affect which addresses are suitable for storing a particular type.

---

## 11. How Pointers Are Stored in Memory

### Program

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int x = 42;
    int *ptr = &x;

    printf("Address of x: %p\n", (void *)&x);
    printf("Value of ptr: %p\n", (void *)ptr);
    printf("Value pointed to by ptr: %d\n", *ptr);

    return 0;
}
```

### Example memory layout

Imagine that `x` is located at address `0x1000`.

```text
Variable x
Address: 0x1000
Value:   42
```

The pointer `ptr` stores the address of `x`.

```text
Variable ptr
Address: 0x2000
Value:   0x1000
```

The addresses are examples, not guaranteed real addresses.

### Understand the operators

- `x` gives the value stored in `x`, which is 42.
- `&x` gives the address of `x`.
- `ptr` gives the address stored in the pointer, which is the address of `x`.
- `*ptr` accesses the value at the address stored in `ptr`, which is 42.

### Pointer size

Pointer size depends on the implementation. Commonly:

- A 32-bit environment uses 4-byte pointers.
- A 64-bit environment uses 8-byte pointers.

These are common conventions, not universal rules for every platform.

### Remember

A pointer is a variable that stores a pointer value, commonly a memory address. It does not store the pointed-to integer itself.

---

## 12. What Is the Use of `x & 1`?

The expression:

```c
x & 1
```

uses bitwise AND to check the least significant bit (LSB), which is the rightmost bit.

The binary representation of 1 is:

```text
00000001
```

All the other bits are zero, so ANDing with 1 preserves only the rightmost bit.

### Example 1: Odd number

```c
int x = 5;
printf("%d", x & 1);
```

Binary calculation:

```text
x = 5:    00000101
1:        00000001
          --------
x & 1:    00000001
```

Output:

```text
1
```

The rightmost bit of 5 is 1, so the result is 1.

### Example 2: Even number

```c
int x = 8;
printf("%d", x & 1);
```

Binary calculation:

```text
x = 8:    00001000
1:        00000001
          --------
x & 1:    00000000
```

Output:

```text
0
```

The rightmost bit of 8 is 0, so the result is 0.

### Why does this work?

In binary:

- An even integer has a least significant bit of 0.
- An odd integer has a least significant bit of 1.

Therefore, `x & 1` is commonly used to test whether an integer is odd or even.

### Complete program

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int x = 7;

    if (x & 1) {
        printf("%d is odd\n", x);
    } else {
        printf("%d is even\n", x);
    }

    return 0;
}
```

Output:

```text
7 is odd
```

---

# Quick Revision Table

| Topic | Key concept |
|---|---|
| `float` vs `double` | Difference in precision and commonly in size |
| `char` | Stores a small integer value, often used for character codes |
| `%d` | Prints a decimal integer |
| `%c` | Prints a character |
| `%f` | Prints a floating-point value with `printf` |
| `&` | Bitwise AND |
| `&&` | Logical AND |
| `<<` | Left-shift operator |
| `>>` | Right-shift operator |
| Character array | An array containing characters |
| C string | A character sequence terminated by `'\0'` |
| Structure | Members have separate storage |
| Union | Members share storage |
| Pointer | Stores a pointer value, commonly a memory address |
| `&x` | Gets the address of `x` |
| `*ptr` | Dereferences a pointer |
| `x & 1` | Checks the least significant bit; commonly used for odd/even checks |

## Final Tip

When learning C, always distinguish between:

1. **The value stored in a variable** — for example, `65`.
2. **The character printed from that value** — for example, `A` with `%c` on an ASCII-compatible system.
3. **The binary representation** — for example, `01000001` for 65.
4. **The memory address** — the location where an object is stored.

These concepts are related, but they are not the same thing.

# C Programming Notes: Characters, Strings, and Bits

## 1. Storing a string in C

### Original code
```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    char a = "vaishnavi";
    printf("%c", a);

    return 0;
}
```

### What is the error?

- `char` stores a single character, not an entire string.
- `"vaishnavi"` is a string literal because it uses double quotes.
- `%c` is used to print a single character.
- `%s` is used to print a string.

### Correct code
```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    char a[] = "vaishnavi";
    printf("%s", a);

    return 0;
}
```

Output:
```text
vaishnavi
```

Here, `char a[]` creates a character array that stores the string and its terminating null character (`'\0'`).

---

## 2. How does a character store 8 bits?

In C:

- `char` always occupies exactly **1 byte**.
- A byte contains at least 8 bits; on virtually all modern computers, 1 byte is 8 bits.
- Each bit can contain either `0` or `1`.
- Eight bits can represent \(2^8 = 256\) different bit patterns.

A `char` can therefore represent one byte of data.

Depending on the implementation, plain `char` behaves like either `signed char` or `unsigned char`.

For an 8-bit character type:
- `unsigned char`: 0 to 255
- `signed char`: typically -128 to 127

Note: A C `char` stores a numeric code, not a complete human-readable character by itself. The character encoding determines how the code corresponds to a character.

---

## 3. What is the binary value of 65?

The decimal number 65 in binary is:

```text
65 = 1000001 (binary)
```

Written using 8 bits:

```text
01000001
```

Each bit has a place value:

| Bit position | Value |
|---|---:|
| 7 | 128 |
| 6 | 64 |
| 5 | 32 |
| 4 | 16 |
| 3 | 8 |
| 2 | 4 |
| 1 | 2 |
| 0 | 1 |

For `01000001`:

```text
64 + 1 = 65
```

In ASCII, decimal 65 represents the uppercase letter `A`.

Example:
```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    char a = 65;

    printf("%c\n", a);
    printf("%d\n", a);

    return 0;
}
```

Typical output:
```text
A
65
```

- `%c` prints the character corresponding to the value.
- `%d` prints the integer value after integer promotion.

---

## 4. What happens when we write `char a[] = '129';`?

### Incorrect code
```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    char a[] = '129';
    printf("%c", a);

    return 0;
}
```

### Why is it incorrect?

Single quotes are used for character constants, not strings.

- `'1'` represents the character `1`.
- `"129"` represents a string containing the characters `1`, `2`, and `9`, followed by `'\0'`.
- `'129'` is a multi-character character constant in C. Its value is implementation-defined, and it is not a string.

To store the string `129`, write:

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    char a[] = "129";

    printf("%s", a);

    return 0;
}
```

Output:
```text
129
```

To store the individual character `1`, write:

```c
char a = '1';
printf("%c", a);
```

Output:
```text
1
```

---

## 5. Is `char a = '129';` correct?

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    char a = '129';

    printf("%c", a);

    return 0;
}
```

This is **not the correct way to store the number 129 or the string "129".**

`'129'` is a multi-character character constant. C permits such constants, but their value is implementation-defined, and the value may not fit in a `char`.

If you want to store the numeric value 129, use:

```c
char a = 129;
```

However, whether a plain `char` can represent 129 depends on whether it is signed or unsigned and on the implementation.

If you want to store the string `"129"`, use:

```c
char a[] = "129";
```

---

## 6. Is `char a = 129;` correct?

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    char a = 129;

    printf("%c", a);

    return 0;
}
```

This is different from using `'129'`.

- `129` is an integer constant.
- `'129'` is a multi-character character constant.

When assigning `129` to a plain `char`:

- If `char` is unsigned and has 8 bits, 129 can be represented.
- If `char` is signed with the usual range of -128 to 127, 129 is outside its range. The conversion result is implementation-defined or an implementation-defined signal may be raised.

Also, `%c` prints a character corresponding to the converted value. It does not print the number `129` as digits.

To print the number 129, use:

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int a = 129;

    printf("%d", a);

    return 0;
}
```

Output:
```text
129
```

To store the value 129 in an unsigned 8-bit character type, use:

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    unsigned char a = 129;

    printf("%u", (unsigned int)a);

    return 0;
}
```

Output:
```text
129
```

---

## 7. Which is correct: `'129'` or `129`?

### Example A
```c
char a = '129';
printf("%c", a);
```

`'129'` is a multi-character character constant. It is not a valid way to write the number 129 or the string `"129"`. Its value is implementation-defined, and converting it to `char` may also lose information.

### Example B
```c
char a = 129;
printf("%c", a);
```

`129` is an integer constant. Assigning it to `char` converts the integer to the range and representation of that type. The result depends on the implementation if `char` cannot represent 129.

### Conclusion

Neither version is the best portable way to print the numeric value 129.

Use this to print the number:

```c
int a = 129;
printf("%d", a);
```

Use this to store and print the string:

```c
char a[] = "129";
printf("%s", a);
```

Use this to store a single character:

```c
char a = '1';
printf("%c", a);
```

## Quick revision table

| Code | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `char a = 'A';` | Stores one character |
| `char a = 65;` | Stores the integer value converted to `char`; commonly represents `A` in ASCII |
| `char a[] = "ABC";` | Stores a string and its terminating null character |
| `printf("%c", a);` | Prints one character |
| `printf("%s", a);` | Prints a null-terminated string |
| `printf("%d", a);` | Prints an integer after the required argument conversion |
| `'1'` | Character constant for the digit `1` |
| `"1"` | String containing `1` and `'\0'` |
| `'129'` | Multi-character character constant; not a string |
| `129` | Integer constant with value 129 |

**Remember:** Single quotes represent character constants, double quotes represent strings, and a numeric value such as `129` is not the same thing as the character or string `"129"`.

# C Programming – Basic Notes

## 1. Example of a C Program

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int a = 2, b = 3;
    int c;

    c = a + b;

    printf("%d", c);

    return 0;
}
```

**Output:**
```
5
```

**Explanation:**
- `int a = 2, b = 3;` declares two integer variables.
- `int c;` declares an integer variable named `c`.
- `c = a + b;` adds `a` and `b` and stores the result in `c`.
- `printf("%d", c);` prints the value of `c`.
- `return 0;` indicates successful program termination.

**Note:** The statement `c = 100 = a + b;` is invalid because `100` is a constant and cannot be assigned a value. If you want to add 100 to the sum, write `c = 100 + a + b;`. The output will be `105`.

---

## 2. Variable Declaration Examples

A variable declaration specifies the data type and name of a variable.

```c
int number;                  // Integer
float pi;                    // Floating-point number
double price;                // Double-precision number
char grade;                  // Character
int scores[5];               // Array of integers
char name[20];               // Character array (string)
int *ptr;                    // Pointer to an integer
const float PI = 3.14;       // Constant
```

**Boolean example:**
```c
#include <stdbool.h>

bool isTrue = true;
```

---

## 3. Examples of Variable Names

Variable names identify variables in a program.

```c
int age;
float averageTemperature;
char userGrade;
double totalPrice;
int studentScores[5];
int *ptrNumber;
const float PI = 3.14;
```

Good variable names should be meaningful and easy to understand.

---

## 4. Valid and Invalid Variable Names

### Valid Variable Names

```c
int age;
float temperature_celsius;
char initial;
double averageScore;
int studentScores[5];
int *ptrNumber;
int Vai_12;
```

### Invalid Variable Names

```c
int 123abc;              // Starts with a digit
float average temperature; // Contains a space
char my-grade;           // Contains a hyphen
double total$;            // Contains a special character
int void;                // Uses a reserved keyword
int break;               // Uses a reserved keyword
```

### Rules for Variable Names in C

1. A variable name must begin with a letter or an underscore (`_`).
2. It can contain letters, digits, and underscores.
3. It cannot contain spaces.
4. It cannot contain special characters such as `@`, `$`, or `-`.
5. It cannot be a C keyword such as `int`, `void`, or `break`.
6. Variable names are case-sensitive. For example, `age` and `Age` are different names.

**Example:** `Vai_12` is a valid variable name because it starts with a letter and contains only letters, an underscore, and digits.

---

## 5. Operator Precedence in C

Operator precedence determines which operator is evaluated first in an expression.

### Common Operators (Highest to Lowest Precedence)

| Priority | Operators | Description |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | `()`, `[]`, `.` , `->`, postfix `++`, `--` | Parentheses, array access, member access, postfix increment/decrement |
| 2 | `++`, `--`, unary `+`, unary `-`, `!`, `~`, `*`, `&`, `sizeof`, type cast | Unary operators |
| 3 | `*`, `/`, `%` | Multiplication, division, modulus |
| 4 | `+`, `-` | Addition, subtraction |
| 5 | `<<`, `>>` | Bitwise shift |
| 6 | `<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=` | Relational operators |
| 7 | `==`, `!=` | Equality operators |
| 8 | `&` | Bitwise AND |
| 9 | `^` | Bitwise XOR |
| 10 | `|` | Bitwise OR |
| 11 | `&&` | Logical AND |
| 12 | `||` | Logical OR |
| 13 | `?:` | Conditional operator |
| 14 | `=`, `+=`, `-=`, `*=`, `/=`, `%=` and other assignments | Assignment operators |
| 15 | `,` | Comma operator |

### Example 1: Multiplication Before Addition

```c
int result = 2 + 3 * 4;
printf("%d", result);
```

**Output:**
```
14
```

**Explanation:** Multiplication has higher precedence than addition.

First, `3 * 4 = 12`, then `2 + 12 = 14`.

### Example 2: Using Parentheses

```c
int result = (2 + 3) * 4;
printf("%d", result);
```

**Output:**
```
20
```

**Explanation:** Parentheses have higher precedence, so `2 + 3` is evaluated first. Then `5 * 4 = 20`.

### Example 3: Division and Multiplication

```c
int result = 12 / 3 * 2;
printf("%d", result);
```

**Output:**
```
8
```

**Explanation:** Multiplication and division have equal precedence and are evaluated from left to right. Therefore, `12 / 3 = 4`, then `4 * 2 = 8`.

**Remember:** Operator precedence determines priority, while associativity determines the evaluation direction when operators have equal precedence. Parentheses can be used to make expressions clearer.



C PROGRAMMING — COMBINED CHAT NOTES

These notes combine the C programming topics discussed in the chat. A
few earlier explanations are corrected where necessary.

1. CHARACTER, INTEGER, AND STRING LITERALS

Example: char a = ‘Y’; printf(“%d”, a);

In ASCII-compatible systems, ‘Y’ has the integer code 89, so printing it
with %d typically prints 89.

    char a = 89;
    printf("%d\n", a);  // prints 89 on typical systems
    printf("%c\n", a);  // prints Y on ASCII-compatible systems

Single quotes represent a character constant; double quotes represent a
string literal: char ch = ‘2’; char text[] = “278”;

Important correction: char a = '278'; is not a string. In C, a
multi-character constant such as ‘278’ has an implementation-defined
value (commonly a multi-character integer constant), and assigning it to
char can lose information. Do not rely on it. Use char text[] = "278";
for a string.

2. sprintf() VS sscanf()

sprintf formats values and writes the result into a character array.
sscanf reads/parses values from a string.

Example: char buffer[50]; int n = 42; sprintf(buffer, “Number: %d”, n);
// writes “Number: 42” into buffer

    int extracted;
    sscanf(buffer, "Number: %d", &extracted); // extracted becomes 42

Always ensure the destination array is large enough when using sprintf.
In modern C, snprintf is generally safer because it limits the number of
characters written.

3. NUMBER 4,294,967,295

Read as: “four billion, two hundred ninety-four million, nine hundred
sixty-seven thousand, two hundred ninety-five.”

This is 2^32 - 1, the maximum value of a 32-bit unsigned integer.
Whether unsigned int is 32 bits depends on the implementation.

4. short int AND int ON A 16-BIT SYSTEM

On many traditional 16-bit C implementations: - signed short int:
-32,768 to 32,767 - signed int: -32,768 to 32,767

Both are often 16 bits on such systems. C only guarantees minimum
ranges, so check the implementation. sizeof can be used to inspect type
sizes: printf(“%zu”, sizeof(short int)); printf(“%zu”, sizeof(int));

5. THE COMMA OPERATOR

With parentheses: int c = (100, 5, 4);

The comma operator evaluates expressions left to right; the value of the
whole expression is the last expression, 4. Thus c becomes 4.

Without parentheses, this is NOT a valid declaration: int c = 100, 5, 4;

In a declaration, commas separate declarators, so after int c = 100, the
token 5 is not a valid variable name. This causes a syntax error. The
earlier claim that it prints 100 was incorrect.

Compare: int c = 100, d = 5, e = 4; // valid declaration of three
variables c = (100, 5, 4); // valid comma expression; c becomes 4

6. scanf/printf VS getchar/putchar

scanf reads formatted input; printf prints formatted output. getchar
reads one character from stdin; putchar writes one character to stdout.

Example: int ch = getchar(); putchar(ch);

Use int for the result of getchar because it must represent every
unsigned-char value plus EOF.

7. VALID AND INVALID IDENTIFIER NAMES

Valid examples: count _totalAmount average_123 first_name MAX_SIZE
isValid calculateSum PI tempValue total_count

Invalid examples: 2ndNumber // starts with a digit total amount //
contains a space average# // contains # user-name // contains hyphen if
// keyword double // keyword struct // keyword my variable // contains a
space 123total // starts with a digit sum@value // contains @

Identifiers can contain letters, digits, and underscores, but cannot
start with a digit. C keywords cannot be used as identifiers. Names
beginning with underscores can be reserved in some contexts, so ordinary
descriptive names are usually preferable.

8. IMPLICIT AND EXPLICIT TYPE CONVERSION

Implicit conversion happens automatically: int a = 5; float b = 3.14f;
float result = a + b; // a is converted to float for the operation

Explicit conversion is requested using a cast: int a = 10; double b =
(double)a;

A conversion from a lower-precision/range type to a
higher-precision/range type is often called promotion (for example, int
to double), but “low to high” is only a rough teaching rule. C
conversion rules depend on the types and operation.

A conversion from floating point to integer discards the fractional part
toward zero: float f = 7.89f; int n = (int)f; // n is 7

9. INTEGER DIVISION AND printf FORMAT SPECIFIERS

Given: int z; int x = 20, y = 3; z = x / y; printf(“Sum %f”, z);

x / y is integer division, so z becomes 6. But %f expects a double
argument in printf, whereas z is an int. This format mismatch causes
undefined behavior; the printed output cannot be predicted reliably.

Correct integer output: printf(“Result: %d”, z); // 6

Floating-point division: double z = (double)x / y; printf(“Result: %f”,
z); // approximately 6.666667

10. BITWISE NOT (~)

The bitwise NOT operator flips every bit of its operand. For ordinary
signed integers on modern two’s-complement systems: ~x == -x - 1

Therefore: ~4 == -5 ~(-6) == 5

Why is ~4 equal to -5? - 4 in a simplified 8-bit view: 00000100 - Flip
every bit: 11111011 - This is a negative two’s-complement number. - To
find its magnitude, flip the bits: 00000100 - Add 1: 00000101 = 5 -
Because the original pattern was negative, its value is -5.

The “flip bits and add one” method finds the magnitude of a negative
two’s-complement number; the sign must still be applied. The final 5 is
the magnitude, not the signed value. In C, int is usually wider than 8
bits, but the same rule applies on modern two’s-complement systems.

For -6 in 8 bits: -6 = 11111010 ~(-6) = 00000101 = 5

Example: int num = 4; int result = ~num; printf(“%d”, result); // -5 on
modern two’s-complement systems

11. VALID AND INVALID EXPRESSIONS

8++ is invalid: a constant is not a modifiable lvalue. ++x++ is invalid:
the result of postfix increment is not a modifiable lvalue for prefix
increment. (x+y)- - is incomplete/invalid: the second minus has no
operand.

More examples: - x ** y is invalid in C; C does not have a ** exponent
operator. Use pow(x, y) from math.h (and link the math library if
required by the platform). - int 123abc = 42; is invalid because an
identifier cannot start with a digit. - if x > y { ... } is invalid
syntax; write if (x > y) { ... }.

Correction: x + +y is valid C. It means x + (+y), i.e. x plus positive
y. Similarly, x + + +y can be parsed as x plus unary-positive
unary-positive y, although it is unusual and confusing. x =+ y is also
syntactically valid and means x = (+y), NOT x += y. The compound
assignment is written x += y.

12. INVALID FOR-LOOP AND IF SYNTAX

Invalid: for i = 0; i < 10; i++) { }

Valid: for (i = 0; i < 10; i++) { }

Invalid (missing closing parenthesis): for (int i = 0; i < 10; i++ { }

Valid: for (int i = 0; i < 10; i++) { }

Invalid: if x > y { }

Valid: if (x > y) { }

An if/else statement can have single statements without braces, but
braces are recommended for clarity. Multiple else clauses attached to
the same if chain are invalid.

13. CONTINUE IN A WHILE LOOP

Code: int i = 0; while (i < 5) { if (i == 2) { continue; } printf(“%d”,
i); i++; }

This prints: 0 1

Then it loops forever. When i becomes 2, continue skips printf and i++;
i stays 2, so the condition remains true forever. The program appears to
stop after printing 0 and 1 because it is stuck in an infinite loop.

Fix: int i = 0; while (i < 5) { if (i == 2) { i++; continue; }
printf(“%d”, i); i++; }

This prints 0, 1, 3, 4.

14. FUNCTIONS: PARAMETERS AND ARGUMENTS

Parameters (also called formal parameters) are listed in the function
definition. Arguments (actual arguments) are the values supplied in a
function call.

Example: int sum(int x, int y) { return x + y; }

    int result = sum(3, 2); // 3 and 2 are arguments; x and y are parameters

For a function with a visible prototype/definition, the number of
arguments must match the parameters, except for variadic functions
(functions declared with ...). Passing too few or too many arguments to
a prototyped function requires a diagnostic and is not a valid call.

Example: int sum(int x, int y, int h) { return x + y + h; }

    int c = sum(3, 2, 9); // valid
    // int c = sum(3, 2); // invalid: too few arguments
    // int c = sum(3, 2, 9, 10); // invalid: too many arguments

Older C material sometimes says extra arguments are ignored or missing
arguments receive garbage values. Do not rely on this. With a proper
prototype, a mismatched argument count is a constraint violation and a
conforming compiler must issue a diagnostic. Without a prototype in
older C dialects, behavior could be different and unsafe; modern C
requires function prototypes in normal practice.

A corrected two-argument sum: int sum(int x, int y) { return x + y; }

    int main(void) {
        int a = 3, b = 2;
        int c = sum(a, b);
        printf("Sum of %d and %d: %d\n", a, b, c);
        return 0;
    }

For a variable number of arguments, C supports variadic functions using
<stdarg.h>. One argument commonly indicates how many values follow:
#include <stdarg.h> int sum(int count, …) { int result = 0; va_list
args; va_start(args, count); for (int i = 0; i < count; i++) result +=
va_arg(args, int); va_end(args); return result; } Call: sum(3, 3, 2, 9)
returns 14.

15. ARRAYS, DECREMENT, AND THE FOR LOOP

Code: int i, arr[8] = {1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8}; for (i = 7; i >= 0; i–) {
printf(“%d, –arr[–i]); }

Inside the loop, --i happens in the array index and the loop update i--
happens too. Starting from i=7: - First body: –i makes i=6; arr[6] is 7,
and –arr[6] makes it 6; prints 6. Loop update makes i=5. - Second body:
–i makes i=4; arr[4] is 5, and –arr[4] makes it 4; prints 4. Loop update
makes i=3. - Third body: –i makes i=2; arr[2] is 3, and –arr[2] makes it
2; prints 2. Loop update makes i=1. - Fourth body: –i makes i=0; arr[0]
is 1, and –arr[0] makes it 0; prints 0. Loop update makes i=-1, and the
loop stops.

Output: 6 4 2 0

The code modifies the array elements at indices 6, 4, 2, and 0. It is
legal here because i is modified once in the body expression and once in
the loop update, in separate full expressions.

16. POINTER INCREMENT AND DEREFERENCE

Suppose: int arr[] = {3, 6, 8, 4}; int *ptr = arr; int x;

A. x = *ptr++; Means x = *(ptr++);: use the current pointed-to value,
then advance the pointer. - x becomes 3 - ptr then points to arr[1],
value 6 - array unchanged

B. x = *++ptr; Means x = *(++ptr);: advance pointer first, then read the
new pointed-to value. - ptr moves to arr[1] - x becomes 6 - array
unchanged

C. x = ++*ptr; Means x = ++(*ptr);: increment the value being pointed
to, then assign it to x. - arr[0] changes from 3 to 4 - x becomes 4 -
ptr still points to arr[0]

D. x = (*ptr)++; Means post-increment the pointed-to value. - x gets the
old value 3 - arr[0] becomes 4 afterward - ptr still points to arr[0]

These examples should be run separately or reset ptr and the array
between examples if you want each to start from the original values.

17. FILE OPEN MODE “r” VS fread()

"r" is a mode passed to fopen; it opens a file for reading as text: FILE
*fp = fopen(“example.txt”, “r”);

fread is a function that reads a number of bytes/elements from an
already-open stream: size_t n = fread(buffer, 1, sizeof buffer, fp);

They are not alternatives: - fopen(..., "r") opens the file. -
fread(...) reads from the open file. - "rb" is commonly used for binary
reading.

Check that fopen did not return NULL, check how many elements fread
returned, and close the file with fclose. If using fread to read text
into a buffer, add a terminating ‘\0’ yourself if you intend to treat
the data as a C string and have space available.

18. extern AND SCOPE / LINKAGE

extern declares an object or function whose definition exists elsewhere
(or later, in some cases). It does not mean you should #include the .c
source file.

Typical multi-file pattern:

main.c: #include <stdio.h> extern int globalVar;

    int main(void) {
        printf("%d\n", globalVar);
        return 0;
    }

other.c: int globalVar = 42;

Compile and link both source files together, for example: cc main.c
other.c -o program

A more maintainable approach is to put the declaration in a header:

other.h: extern int globalVar;

main.c and other.c can both include #include "other.h", while other.c
contains the one definition: int globalVar = 42;

Do not directly #include "other.c" in main.c as a normal multi-file
technique; that can create duplicate definitions if both files are
compiled and linked separately. A header is for declarations; a .c file
holds definitions and is compiled separately.

19. QUICK SUMMARY

-   %d prints an int; %f in printf expects a double.
-   sprintf writes formatted output to a string; sscanf parses values
    from a string.
-   (100, 5, 4) evaluates to 4 because the comma operator returns the
    last expression.
-   ~4 is -5 on modern two’s-complement C systems; ~(-6) is 5.
-   continue skips the rest of the current loop iteration; make sure
    loop control variables still change.
-   *ptr++ advances the pointer; (*ptr)++ increments the pointed-to
    value.
-   A function call should match the function prototype’s parameter
    count and compatible types.
-   fopen opens a file; fread reads from it.
-   extern declares a name defined elsewhere; compile/link source files
    together rather than including .c files.


# C Programming – Data Types, Overflow, and Uninitialized Variables

## 1. Why is it printing 2?

**Code:**

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    unsigned short int a = 65538;
    printf("%d", a);

    return 0;
}
```

**Question:** Why is it printing 2?

**Answer:**

The variable `a` is declared as an `unsigned short int`.

Typically, an `unsigned short int` occupies 2 bytes (16 bits) and has a range of:

- Minimum: 0
- Maximum: 65535

The value assigned is 65538, which exceeds the maximum range.

Unsigned integers wrap around using modulo \(2^{16}\), assuming a 16-bit `unsigned short int`.

Calculation:

65538 % 65536 = 2

Therefore, the value stored in `a` is 2, and the output is:

```text
2
```

**Note:** Use `%hu` rather than `%d` to correctly print an `unsigned short int` with `printf`.

---

## 2. If the range is 65535, does exceeding it cause an error?

**Question:** The range is 65535, right? If the value is out of range, does it mean an error occurs?

**Answer:**

No, an error is not automatically generated when an unsigned integer receives a value outside its range.

In C, when an integer value is converted to an unsigned integer type, the result is reduced modulo one more than the maximum representable value.

For a 16-bit `unsigned short int`:

- Range: 0 to 65535
- Value assigned: 65538
- Value stored: 2

This behavior is defined by the C standard.

However, this rule applies to conversion to unsigned integer types. Signed integer overflow during arithmetic is a different situation and can cause undefined behavior.

---

## 3. What is the purpose of using `unsigned short int` instead of `int`?

**Question:** Why use `unsigned short int` if it has a smaller range?

**Answer:**

Different integer data types are useful for different requirements.

| Data type | Typical size | Typical range |
|---|---|---|
| `short int` | 2 bytes | -32768 to 32767 |
| `unsigned short int` | 2 bytes | 0 to 65535 |
| `int` | 4 bytes | -2147483648 to 2147483647 |
| `unsigned int` | 4 bytes | 0 to 4294967295 |

These sizes and ranges are typical on modern systems; the C standard does not require these exact sizes on every platform.

**Reasons to use `unsigned short int`:**

1. It can use less memory than a larger integer type on some systems.
2. It can represent only non-negative values.
3. It can represent values up to 65535 when using 16 bits.
4. It may be useful for arrays containing many small numbers or for specific data formats.

For ordinary calculations, `int` is often the more convenient choice.

---

## 4. If a value exceeds the range, why doesn't C automatically choose a larger data type?

**Question:** If the value goes out of range, why doesn't C automatically use a larger data type to store it?

**Answer:**

C does not automatically change a variable's declared data type when a value exceeds its range.

Consider:

```c
unsigned short int a = 65538;
```

The variable `a` is declared to store values using the `unsigned short int` type. C converts the assigned value to that type, producing 2 if the type has 16 bits.

C does not automatically change `a` into an `int` or `unsigned int`.

If you need to store 65538 without losing its value, choose a sufficiently large type from the beginning.

For example:

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    unsigned int a = 65538;
    printf("%u", a);

    return 0;
}
```

**Output:**

```text
65538
```

The important point is that you, the programmer, must select the appropriate data type according to the range of values your program needs.

---

## 5. In C, are variables initialized to garbage values or zero by default?

**Question:** Does C assign garbage values or zero by default?

**Answer:**

It depends on the variable's storage duration.

### A. Local variable without `static`

```c
int main() {
    int a;
    return 0;
}
```

The variable `a` has an indeterminate value because it has automatic storage duration and was not initialized.

It is commonly described as containing a garbage value, but that is an informal term. Its value is not guaranteed to be any particular number.

### B. Global variable

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int a;

int main() {
    printf("%d", a);
    return 0;
}
```

**Output:**

```text
0
```

A global variable that is not explicitly initialized is automatically initialized to zero.

### C. Local variable declared with `static`

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    static int a;
    printf("%d", a);
    return 0;
}
```

**Output:**

```text
0
```

A local variable declared with `static` is also automatically initialized to zero.

**Summary:**

- Uninitialized local automatic variable: indeterminate value.
- Uninitialized global variable: zero.
- Uninitialized local `static` variable: zero.

---

## 6. Why does `int a; printf("%d", a);` sometimes print 0 instead of a garbage value?

**Code:**

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int a;
    printf("%d", a);
    return 0;
}
```

**Question:** If a local variable is not initialized, why does it sometimes print 0?

**Answer:**

A local variable such as `int a;` is not automatically initialized to zero.

Its value is indeterminate. You might observe 0 when running a program on a particular system, but that does not mean C initialized the variable to zero.

Possible reasons include the memory previously containing zero or the compiler and execution environment affecting what you observe.

However, there is an important technical detail: reading an uninitialized automatic `int` can result in undefined behavior under the C standard. You must not rely on the program printing 0 or any other value.

**Correct practice:**

Always initialize a variable before using its value.

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int a = 0;
    printf("%d", a);
    return 0;
}
```

**Output:**

```text
0
```

Now the output is guaranteed to be 0 because the variable was explicitly initialized.

---

# Final Revision Summary

1. A 16-bit `unsigned short int` has a range of 0 to 65535.
2. Assigning 65538 to that type produces 2 because the value is converted modulo 65536.
3. C does not automatically change a variable's data type when its value exceeds the range.
4. Use a larger data type when the required value exceeds the current type's range.
5. Uninitialized local automatic variables have indeterminate values; they are not guaranteed to contain garbage or zero.
6. Global variables and variables declared with `static` are automatically initialized to zero if no initializer is provided.
7. Reading an uninitialized local variable is not safe; initialize variables before using them.
8. Use the correct `printf` format specifier: `%d` for `int`, `%u` for `unsigned int`, and `%hu` for `unsigned short int`.

# Understanding `x = ++x + ++x` in C

## 1. Original C Program

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int x = 4;
    x = ++x + ++x;
    printf("O: %d\n", x);

    return 0;
}
```

## 2. First Question: Explain How It Prints 14

Initially, `x = 4`.

The expression is:

```c
x = ++x + ++x;
```

The `++x` operator is called **pre-increment**. It increments the variable by 1 and produces the incremented value.

If we imagine evaluating the increments one after another:

- Initially, `x = 4`.
- The first `++x` increments `x` to 5.
- The second `++x` increments `x` to 6.
- The values produced by these operations would be 5 and 6.
- Their sum would be `5 + 6 = 11`.

Therefore, this simple step-by-step explanation does **not** explain an output of 14.

## 3. Second Question: But Why Is It Printing 14?

In C, the expression

```c
x = ++x + ++x;
```

has **undefined behavior**.

This happens because the variable `x` is modified multiple times within the same expression without the sequencing required by the C language standard.

When a program has undefined behavior, the C standard does not guarantee any particular result. Depending on the compiler, compiler version, optimization settings, or environment, the program might print different values, including 14.

However, 14 is not a guaranteed or standard result, and we cannot reliably explain it using a valid sequence of C operations.

## 4. How to Write the Code Correctly

If you want to increment `x` twice and add the resulting values, write:

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int x = 4;

    int a = ++x;  // x becomes 5, a = 5
    int b = ++x;  // x becomes 6, b = 6

    x = a + b;    // x = 5 + 6 = 11

    printf("O: %d\n", x);

    return 0;
}
```

Output:

```text
O: 11
```

## 5. Key Takeaway

- `++x` means increment first, then use the new value.
- `x++` means use the current value first, then increment.
- Modifying the same variable multiple times in an unsequenced expression can cause undefined behavior in C.
- Never rely on the output of an expression with undefined behavior, even if it consistently prints 14 on your computer.

# C PROGRAMMING — COMPLETE CHAT NOTES

## 1. Does every variable need a data type and storage class?

**Question:** Every variable must have a data type, and is a storage class compulsory? If yes, why?

**Answer:**

### Data Type
A data type specifies the kind of value a variable can store, such as an integer, character, or decimal number.

Example:
```c
int age = 20;
float price = 3.4;
char grade = 'A';
double number = 12.345;
```

The data type helps the compiler determine how to interpret the value and which operations are valid.

### Storage Class
A storage class determines properties such as a variable's storage duration, scope, and linkage.

Common C storage-class specifiers include:
- `auto`
- `register`
- `static`
- `extern`

Example:
```c
static int count = 0;
```

**Is a storage class compulsory?**

No. You do not usually need to specify a storage-class specifier explicitly. C applies default rules depending on where and how a variable is declared.

For example:
```c
int main(void)
{
    int x = 10;
    return 0;
}
```

Here, `x` has automatic storage duration even though `auto` is not written.

**Remember:** A data type is required for ordinary C variable declarations, but explicitly writing a storage-class specifier is generally optional.

---

## 2. Why does `%d` not work for a float?

**Question:**
```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main(void)
{
    float var = 3.4;

    printf("%d", var);
    return 0;
}
```

Why does this not work correctly?

**Answer:**

`%d` is used to print an `int`, whereas `%f` is used to print a floating-point value passed to `printf`.

The variable `var` is a `float`, but `printf` receives it as a `double` because of the default argument promotions applied to variadic function arguments.

Using `%d` tells `printf` to retrieve an `int`, which does not match the actual argument type. This causes **undefined behavior**.

The output is not guaranteed to be any particular value, and a runtime error is not guaranteed to occur.

**Correct code:**
```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main(void)
{
    float var = 3.4f;

    printf("%f", var);
    return 0;
}
```

Output:
```text
3.400000
```

---

## 3. Why does `%f` work for a double?

**Question:**
```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main(void)
{
    double var = 3;

    printf("Hello Boss. %f", var);
    return 0;
}
```

Why does this work?

**Answer:**

In `printf`, `%f` is used to print a `double`.

Although the variable is declared as `double`, `%f` is the correct conversion specifier for it.

Output:
```text
Hello Boss. 3.000000
```

### Is `%lf` required for double?

For `printf`:
- `%f` prints a `double`.
- `%lf` also prints a `double` in standard C; the `l` has no effect for the floating-point conversion in `printf`.

For `scanf`:
- `%f` expects a pointer to `float`.
- `%lf` expects a pointer to `double`.

Example:
```c
float a;
double b;

scanf("%f", &a);
scanf("%lf", &b);
```

**Important distinction:** `%lf` is necessary for reading a `double` using `scanf`, but `%f` is sufficient for printing a `double` using `printf`.

---

## 4. Why might the output be zero?

Consider:
```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main(void)
{
    float var = 3;

    printf("Hello Boss. %d", var);
    return 0;
}
```

**Answer:**

The problem is the mismatch between `%d` and `var`.

- `%d` expects an `int`.
- `var` is a `float`.
- A `float` argument is promoted to `double` when passed to `printf`.
- `%d` attempts to retrieve an `int`, causing undefined behavior.

You might see `0`, another unexpected number, or other behavior depending on the compiler and execution environment.

**The C language does not guarantee that the output will be zero.**

Correct code:
```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main(void)
{
    float var = 3;

    printf("Hello Boss. %f", var);
    return 0;
}
```

Output:
```text
Hello Boss. 3.000000
```

### Why doesn't the compiler report an error?

Some compilers issue a warning when the format specifier does not match the argument type. Others may not report it, depending on the compiler and settings.

The compiler is not required to diagnose every case of undefined behavior.

Remember: **No error does not mean the code is correct.** Always match the format specifier to the expected argument type.

---

## 5. What is the difference between `TRUE` and `true` in C?

Code:
```c
int main(void)
{
    if (TRUE)
    {
        printf("India\n");
    }

    if (true)
    {
        printf("Honey\n");
    }

    printf("ZING");

    return 0;
}
```

**Answer:**

In standard C:
- `TRUE` is not a predefined keyword or standard Boolean constant.
- `true` is available through `<stdbool.h>` in C99 through C17.
- In C23, `bool`, `true`, and `false` are built into the language.

If `TRUE` has not been defined, the program will normally fail to compile because `TRUE` is undeclared. If `true` is also unavailable because the appropriate header is missing or the language version does not support it, that may cause another compilation error.

### Correct code
```c
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdbool.h>

int main(void)
{
    if (true)
    {
        printf("India\n");
    }

    if (true)
    {
        printf("Honey\n");
    }

    printf("ZING");

    return 0;
}
```

Output:
```text
India
Honey
ZING
```

Both conditions are true, so both `if` blocks execute.

---

## 6. What is the output when a semicolon follows a while loop?

Code:
```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main(void)
{
    int i = 0;

    while (i < 5);
        printf("%d\n", i);

    i++;

    return 0;
}
```

**Answer:**

The semicolon immediately after `while (i < 5)` is important.

```c
while (i < 5);
```

The semicolon represents an empty loop body.

Since `i` starts at `0` and never changes inside the loop, the condition `i < 5` remains true forever.

Therefore, the program enters an **infinite loop** and never reaches the `printf` statement. It produces no output from that statement.

### Correct code
```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main(void)
{
    int i = 0;

    while (i < 5)
    {
        printf("%d\n", i);
        i++;
    }

    return 0;
}
```

Output:
```text
0
1
2
3
4
```

### Key lesson

A semicolon after a `while` statement creates an empty loop body. If the condition never becomes false, the loop never ends.

---

## 7. What happens when a semicolon follows an if statement?

Code:
```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main(void)
{
    int i = 0;

    if (i < 5);
    {
        printf("%d\n", i);
        i++;
    }

    return 0;
}
```

**Answer:**

The semicolon after the `if` statement ends the `if` statement.

```c
if (i < 5);
```

The `if` statement has an empty body. The block that follows is a separate block and executes regardless of the condition.

Since `i` is initialized to `0`, the separate block prints `0` and then increments `i` to `1`.

Output:
```text
0
```

### Correct code
```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main(void)
{
    int i = 0;

    if (i < 5)
    {
        printf("%d\n", i);
        i++;
    }

    return 0;
}
```

Output:
```text
0
```

In this corrected version, the block executes because `i < 5` is true.

**Remember:**
- `if (condition);` has an empty body.
- `if (condition) { ... }` executes the block only when the condition is true.

---

## 8. Explain the switch statement with duplicate cases

Code:
```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main(void)
{
    char code = 64;

    switch (code)
    {
        case 64:
            printf("SHIP ");
            break;

        case 8 * 8:
            printf("BOAT ");
            break;

        default:
            printf("PETROL");
    }

    printf("CHILLY");

    return 0;
}
```

**Answer:**

This program produces a compilation error because the `case` labels have duplicate values.

Consider these two cases:
```c
case 64:
```

and
```c
case 8 * 8:
```

Since `8 * 8` equals `64`, both cases represent the same value.

In a C `switch` statement, each `case` value must be unique. Therefore, these two cases cannot appear together in the same `switch`.

### What if the second case is removed?

Corrected code:
```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main(void)
{
    char code = 64;

    switch (code)
    {
        case 64:
            printf("SHIP ");
            break;

        default:
            printf("PETROL");
    }

    printf("CHILLY");

    return 0;
}
```

Output:
```text
SHIP CHILLY
```

### Why?

1. `code` is initialized to `64`.
2. `switch (code)` looks for a matching case.
3. `case 64` matches, so `SHIP ` is printed.
4. `break` exits the `switch` statement.
5. `printf("CHILLY")` is outside the `switch`, so it executes afterward.

Output:
```text
SHIP CHILLY
```

**Important:** A `break` exits the `switch`; it does not end the entire program.

---

# QUICK REVISION SUMMARY

| Topic | Key rule |
|---|---|
| Data type | Specifies what kind of value a variable holds |
| Storage class | Controls properties such as storage duration, scope, and linkage |
| `printf("%d", x)` | Expects an `int` |
| `printf("%f", x)` | Expects a `double` argument |
| `scanf("%f", &x)` | Expects a `float *` |
| `scanf("%lf", &x)` | Expects a `double *` |
| `TRUE` | Not predefined by standard C |
| `true` | Available via `<stdbool.h>` in C99–C17 |
| `while (condition);` | Creates an empty loop body |
| `if (condition);` | Ends the `if` with an empty body |
| `switch` cases | Must have unique constant values |
| `break` | Exits the nearest enclosing loop or `switch` |
| Missing braces | Can change which statements belong to a control structure |
| No compiler error | Does not guarantee correct or defined behavior |

## Final advice

When learning C, pay close attention to:
1. Data types and format specifiers.
2. The difference between `printf` and `scanf`.
3. Semicolons after `if`, `while`, and `for`.
4. Braces and statement boundaries.
5. Unique `case` values in a `switch`.
6. Compiler warnings, especially format warnings.

These small details are essential for understanding C programming and solving output-based questions.

# C Programming – Combined Notes

## 1. Maximum Length of a Variable Name (Identifier)

In C, an identifier is the name given to a variable, function, array, etc.

There is no universal maximum identifier length specified by the C language itself. The compiler determines the practical limit.

The C standard guarantees support for at least 31 significant initial characters for certain identifiers with external linkage in C99 and later standards. Modern compilers generally support much longer names.

Example:

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int thisIsAnExtremelyLongVariableNameWithManyCharacters = 42;

    printf("Value = %d\n", thisIsAnExtremelyLongVariableNameWithManyCharacters);

    return 0;
}
```

Output:
```text
Value = 42
```

## 2. Valid and Invalid Identifier Names

### Rules for naming identifiers

1. An identifier must begin with a letter (`A–Z`, `a–z`) or an underscore (`_`).
2. After the first character, it can contain letters, digits (`0–9`), and underscores.
3. Spaces and special characters such as `@`, `$`, and `-` are not allowed.
4. C keywords cannot be used as identifiers.
5. Identifiers are case-sensitive.

### Valid names

```c
int age;
int student_name;
int count123;
int _value;
int myVariable;
int MyVariable;
int total_marks;
```

### Invalid names

```c
int 123count;       // Starts with a digit
int student-name;   // Contains a hyphen
int student name;   // Contains a space
int price@value;    // Contains a special character
int if;             // Uses a C keyword
```

Note: `myVariable` and `MyVariable` are different identifiers because C is case-sensitive.

## 3. Unary Operators in C

A unary operator operates on only one operand.

Common unary operators include:

- `+` — Unary plus
- `-` — Unary minus
- `++` — Increment
- `--` — Decrement
- `!` — Logical NOT
- `~` — Bitwise NOT
- `&` — Address-of
- `*` — Pointer dereference
- `sizeof` — Determines the size of an object or type

### A. Unary Plus (`+`)

The unary plus operator indicates a positive value. It does not change the value.

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int a = 25;
    int b = +a;

    printf("a = %d\n", a);
    printf("b = %d\n", b);

    return 0;
}
```

Output:
```text
a = 25
b = 25
```

### B. Unary Minus (`-`)

The unary minus operator reverses the sign of a numeric value.

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int a = 25;
    int b = -a;

    printf("a = %d\n", a);
    printf("b = %d\n", b);

    return 0;
}
```

Output:
```text
a = 25
b = -25
```

### C. Increment (`++`)

Increases a variable's value by one.

```c
int a = 5;
a++;
printf("%d", a);
```

Output:
```text
6
```

### D. Decrement (`--`)

Decreases a variable's value by one.

```c
int a = 5;
a--;
printf("%d", a);
```

Output:
```text
4
```

### E. Logical NOT (`!`)

Reverses a logical condition. In C, zero is false and nonzero is true.

```c
int a = 0;
printf("%d", !a);
```

Output:
```text
1
```

### F. Bitwise NOT (`~`)

Flips the bits of an integer.

```c
unsigned int a = 12;
printf("%u\n", ~a);
```

The exact output depends on the width of `unsigned int`. For a 32-bit `unsigned int`, the output is `4294967283`.

### G. Address-of (`&`)

Gets the memory address of a variable.

```c
int a = 10;
int *p = &a;
```

### H. Pointer Dereference (`*`)

Accesses the value stored at the address held by a pointer.

```c
int a = 10;
int *p = &a;

printf("%d", *p);
```

Output:
```text
10
```

### I. `sizeof`

Returns the size of a type or object in bytes.

```c
int a = 10;
printf("%zu", sizeof(a));
```

The output depends on the implementation; `4` is common.

## 4. Arithmetic Operators in C

Arithmetic operators perform mathematical calculations.

| Operator | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|
| `+` | Addition | `a + b` |
| `-` | Subtraction | `a - b` |
| `*` | Multiplication | `a * b` |
| `/` | Division | `a / b` |
| `%` | Remainder | `a % b` |

Example:

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int a = 25;
    int b = 5;

    printf("a + b = %d\n", a + b);
    printf("a - b = %d\n", a - b);
    printf("a * b = %d\n", a * b);
    printf("a / b = %d\n", a / b);
    printf("a %% b = %d\n", a % b);

    return 0;
}
```

Output:
```text
a + b = 30
a - b = 20
a * b = 125
a / b = 5
a % b = 0
```

Note: To display the `%` symbol in a `printf()` format string, use `%%`.

## 5. Arithmetic with `int` and `float`

Consider:

```c
int a = 25;
float b = 5.9f;

printf("a + b = %f\n", a + b);
printf("a - b = %f\n", a - b);
```

Output:
```text
a + b = 30.900000
a - b = 19.100000
```

When an integer and a floating-point value are used together in arithmetic, the integer is converted to a floating-point type for the calculation.

### Division with a negative integer and a float

```c
int a = -25;
float b = 5.3f;

printf("a / b = %f\n", a / b);
```

Output (approximately):
```text
a / b = -4.716981
```

The result is a floating-point value because one operand is a `float`.

## 6. Format Specifiers in C

Format specifiers tell `printf()` how to display a value.

| Data type or value | Common `printf()` specifier | Example |
|---|---|---|
| `int` | `%d` or `%i` | `printf("%d", a);` |
| `short int` | `%hd` | `printf("%hd", a);` |
| `long int` | `%ld` | `printf("%ld", a);` |
| `long long int` | `%lld` | `printf("%lld", a);` |
| `unsigned int` | `%u` | `printf("%u", a);` |
| `char` | `%c` | `printf("%c", ch);` |
| Character's integer value | `%d` | `printf("%d", ch);` |
| `float` | `%f` | `printf("%f", f);` |
| `double` | `%f` | `printf("%f", d);` |
| `long double` | `%Lf` | `printf("%Lf", ld);` |
| String (`char` array) | `%s` | `printf("%s", str);` |
| Pointer address | `%p` | `printf("%p", (void *)p);` |
| `size_t` | `%zu` | `printf("%zu", sizeof(a));` |

### Can we use `%d` for a `char`?

Yes, with `printf()`, a `char` is promoted to `int` in the usual case. `%d` prints its integer value, while `%c` prints the character.

```c
char ch = 'A';

printf("%c\n", ch);
printf("%d\n", ch);
```

Output on an ASCII-compatible system:
```text
A
65
```

### Can we use `%d` for a `short int`?

Yes. A `short int` is promoted to `int` (or, in some circumstances, `unsigned int`) when passed to a variadic function such as `printf()`. For ordinary `short int` values, `%d` is valid. `%hd` is also valid and expresses the intended type clearly.

```c
short int a = 100;

printf("%d\n", a);
printf("%hd\n", a);
```

Both print:
```text
100
100
```

### Can we use `%d` for a `float` or `double`?

No. This is a format mismatch and causes undefined behavior.

```c
float a = 5.9f;
double b = 3.14;

printf("%f\n", a);
printf("%f\n", b);
```

Both `%f` conversions are correct for `printf()`. A `float` is promoted to `double` when passed to `printf()`.

### Can we use `%f` for an `int`?

No. `%f` expects a `double` argument in `printf()`, not an `int`. Using the wrong format specifier can cause undefined behavior.

```c
int a = 25;

printf("%d\n", a);   // Correct
```

Remember: Do not rely on an incorrect format specifier producing a particular output. It is not guaranteed to print zero or simply convert the value.

## 7. Compound Assignment Operators

Compound assignment operators perform an operation and assign the result back to the left operand.

There are ten compound assignment operators in C.

| Operator | Meaning | Equivalent form for a simple variable |
|---|---|---|
| `+=` | Add and assign | `a = a + b;` |
| `-=` | Subtract and assign | `a = a - b;` |
| `*=` | Multiply and assign | `a = a * b;` |
| `/=` | Divide and assign | `a = a / b;` |
| `%=` | Remainder and assign | `a = a % b;` |
| `&=` | Bitwise AND and assign | `a = a & b;` |
| `|=` | Bitwise OR and assign | `a = a | b;` |
| `^=` | Bitwise XOR and assign | `a = a ^ b;` |
| `<<=` | Left shift and assign | `a = a << b;` |
| `>>=` | Right shift and assign | `a = a >> b;` |

Note: These equivalent forms are conceptual. Compound assignment evaluates its left operand only once, unlike some manually expanded expressions. Integer division, overflow, and shift rules still apply.

### Example of all ten compound assignment operators

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int a;

    a = 10;
    a += 5;
    printf("+= : %d\n", a);

    a = 10;
    a -= 5;
    printf("-= : %d\n", a);

    a = 10;
    a *= 5;
    printf("*= : %d\n", a);

    a = 10;
    a /= 5;
    printf("/= : %d\n", a);

    a = 17;
    a %= 5;
    printf("%%= : %d\n", a);

    a = 12;
    a &= 10;
    printf("&= : %d\n", a);

    a = 12;
    a |= 10;
    printf("|= : %d\n", a);

    a = 12;
    a ^= 10;
    printf("^= : %d\n", a);

    a = 5;
    a <<= 1;
    printf("<<= : %d\n", a);

    a = 10;
    a >>= 1;
    printf(">>= : %d\n", a);

    return 0;
}
```

Output:
```text
+= : 15
-= : 5
*= : 50
/= : 2
%= : 2
&= : 8
|= : 14
^= : 6
<<= : 10
>>= : 5
```

### Bitwise compound assignment explanation

For the example `a = 12` and `b = 10`:

```text
12 = 1100 (binary)
10 = 1010 (binary)
```

- `a &= b` gives `1000` in binary, which is `8`.
- `a |= b` gives `1110` in binary, which is `14`.
- `a ^= b` gives `0110` in binary, which is `6`.

For shifts:

- `a <<= 1` shifts the bits of `a` one position to the left. For `a = 5`, the result is `10`.
- `a >>= 1` shifts the bits of `a` one position to the right. For `a = 10`, the result is `5`.

## 8. Important Points to Remember

1. `%d` is used for `int`.
2. `%c` prints a character; `%d` can print a character's integer value after promotion.
3. `%f` is used for `float` and `double` arguments in `printf()`.
4. `%hd` is the explicit format for `short int`, although `%d` also works for ordinary `short int` values because of integer promotion.
5. Never use `%d` to print a `float` or `double`.
6. `+` and `-` can be unary operators when they act on one operand.
7. `++` increments a variable, and `--` decrements it.
8. C has ten compound assignment operators.
9. Identifiers cannot begin with a digit or be C keywords.
10. Use meaningful variable names and the correct format specifiers for reliable programs.

# C Program to Check Prime Numbers

## 1. C Program

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int n, i, count = 0;

    printf("Enter a number: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);

    for (i = 2; i <= n / 2; i++) {
        if (n % i == 0) {
            count++;
            break;
        }
    }

    if (count == 0) {
        printf("%d is a prime number.\n", n);
    } else {
        printf("%d is not a prime number.\n", n);
    }

    return 0;
}
```

## 2. Why is the loop condition `i <= n / 2` instead of `i <= n`?

The condition `i <= n / 2` is used because we do not need to check every number up to `n` to find a divisor.

For example, if `n = 12`, we only need to check possible divisors from `2` to `6` (`n / 2`).

If a number greater than `n / 2` divides `n`, the only possibility is the number itself. We do not need to check `n` because every number is divisible by itself.

Therefore, checking up to `n / 2` is sufficient, but it is not the most efficient method.

## 3. Why is `sqrt(n)` enough?

When checking whether a number `n` is prime, we only need to check possible divisors up to the square root of `n`.

This is because factors occur in pairs.

For example:

`36 = 2 × 18`

`36 = 3 × 12`

`36 = 4 × 9`

`36 = 6 × 6`

Notice that the factor pairs meet at `sqrt(36) = 6`.

If a number has a factor greater than its square root, it must also have a corresponding factor smaller than its square root.

For example:

`36 = 4 × 9`

Here, `9` is greater than `sqrt(36) = 6`, but its corresponding factor, `4`, is smaller than `6`.

Therefore, if we have checked every possible divisor from `2` to `sqrt(n)` and none divides `n` exactly, there cannot be another factor greater than the square root either.

Hence, checking up to `sqrt(n)` is sufficient to determine whether a number is prime.

## 4. More efficient version of the program

```c
#include <stdio.h>
#include <math.h>

int main() {
    int n, i, count = 0;

    printf("Enter a number: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);

    if (n < 2) {
        printf("%d is not a prime number.\n", n);
        return 0;
    }

    for (i = 2; i <= sqrt(n); i++) {
        if (n % i == 0) {
            count++;
            break;
        }
    }

    if (count == 0) {
        printf("%d is a prime number.\n", n);
    } else {
        printf("%d is not a prime number.\n", n);
    }

    return 0;
}
```

**Important:** The `math.h` header provides the `sqrt()` function. Some C compilers require linking the math library, for example, by compiling with `gcc program.c -lm`.

The check `n < 2` is necessary because `0`, `1`, and negative integers are not prime numbers.

## 5. Main difference

- `i <= n`: Checks too many numbers.
- `i <= n / 2`: Checks enough numbers, but performs unnecessary iterations.
- `i <= sqrt(n)`: Checks enough numbers with fewer iterations.

**Remember:** If a number is composite, it must have at least one factor greater than `1` and less than or equal to its square root. That is why checking up to the square root is enough.

# C Programming: Loops, Increment Operators, and Undefined Behavior

## 1. Optimize Loop Condition

### Question
Why is checking divisors only up to the square root of a number enough to determine whether it is prime?

### Explanation
Consider this C program:

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int n, i, count = 0;

    printf("Enter a number: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);

    for (i = 2; i <= n / 2; i++) {
        if (n % i == 0) {
            count++;
            break;
        }
    }

    if (count == 0)
        printf("Prime number");
    else
        printf("Not a prime number");

    return 0;
}
```

We can optimize the loop by checking divisors only up to the square root of `n`.

**Why?**

If a number `n` is composite, it can be expressed as:

`n = a * b`

If both `a` and `b` were greater than the square root of `n`, their product would be greater than `n`.

Therefore, at least one factor must be less than or equal to the square root of `n`.

We can write:

```c
for (i = 2; i * i <= n; i++)
```

This reduces the number of iterations.

---

## 2. Infinite Loop Using a for Loop

### Question
When will this program stop?

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main()
{
    int i = 0;

    for (i++; ++i; i++)
    {
        printf("GeeksforGeeks to Infinite");
    }

    return 0;
}
```

### Explanation

The syntax of a `for` loop is:

```c
for (initialization; condition; update)
```

In this program:

- `i++` is the initialization expression and executes once.
- `++i` is the condition expression and executes before every iteration.
- `i++` is the update expression and executes after every iteration.

Initially, `i = 0`.

1. Initialization: `i++` changes `i` to `1`.
2. Condition: `++i` changes `i` to `2`. Since `2` is nonzero, the condition is true.
3. The body prints the message.
4. Update: `i++` increases `i`.
5. The process repeats.

In C, a nonzero integer is considered true.

However, when a signed `int` overflows, the behavior is undefined. Therefore, this is not a guaranteed infinite loop under the C standard.

---

## 3. Indefinite Loop Example

An indefinite loop is a loop whose number of iterations is not known beforehand.

Example:

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int userInput;

    printf("Enter a number (0 to stop): ");
    scanf("%d", &userInput);

    while (userInput != 0) {
        printf("You entered: %d\n", userInput);

        printf("Enter a number (0 to stop): ");
        scanf("%d", &userInput);
    }

    printf("Loop stopped.\n");

    return 0;
}
```

The loop continues until the user enters `0`.

Unlike an unconditional infinite loop, this loop has a termination condition controlled by user input.

---

## 4. Infinite Loop Using while

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    while (1) {
        printf("This is an infinite loop.\n");
    }

    return 0;
}
```

**Explanation:** `1` is always true, so the loop continues indefinitely unless the program is terminated externally.

---

## 5. Infinite Loop Using for

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    for (;;) {
        printf("This is another infinite loop.\n");
    }

    return 0;
}
```

**Explanation:** All three expressions in the `for` loop are omitted. An omitted condition is treated as true, so the loop does not terminate naturally.

---

## 6. Infinite Loop Using do-while

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    do {
        printf("This is yet another infinite loop.\n");
    } while (1);

    return 0;
}
```

**Explanation:** A `do-while` loop executes its body at least once. Because its condition is always true, it continues indefinitely.

---

## 7. Loop with a break Statement

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    while (1) {
        printf("This loop executes once.\n");
        break;
    }

    return 0;
}
```

**Explanation:** Although the condition is always true, `break` immediately terminates the loop. Therefore, the loop executes only once.

This is not an infinite loop in actual execution.

---

## 8. Loop with a Condition That Never Becomes False

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int i = 0;

    while (i >= 0) {
        printf("This loop continues.\n");
    }

    return 0;
}
```

**Explanation:** `i` remains `0` because it is never modified. The condition `i >= 0` therefore remains true, and the loop continues indefinitely.

---

## 9. Infinite Loop Using continue

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    for (int i = 1; i > 0; i++) {
        if (i % 2 == 0) {
            continue;
        }

        printf("%d\n", i);
    }

    return 0;
}
```

**Explanation:** The `continue` statement skips the remaining body statements for even values of `i`.

The loop attempts to print odd numbers indefinitely. However, because `i` is a signed `int`, it eventually reaches a point where incrementing it causes signed overflow, which is undefined behavior in C. Thus, this is not a strictly guaranteed infinite loop.

---

## 10. Conditions in for and while Loops

### for Loop Syntax

```c
for (initialization; condition; update) {
    // Loop body
}
```

- **Initialization:** Executes once at the beginning.
- **Condition:** Checked before each iteration. If true, the body executes.
- **Update:** Executes after each iteration of the body.

Example:

```c
for (int i = 1; i <= 5; i++) {
    printf("%d\n", i);
}
```

Output:

```text
1
2
3
4
5
```

### while Loop Syntax

```c
while (condition) {
    // Loop body
}
```

The condition is checked before every iteration. If true, the loop body executes; if false, the loop terminates.

Example:

```c
int i = 1;

while (i <= 5) {
    printf("%d\n", i);
    i++;
}
```

Output:

```text
1
2
3
4
5
```
