### Main Difference

A `for` loop is commonly used when the loop structure includes initialization, a condition, and an update.

A `while` loop is commonly used when repetition depends on a condition and the update may occur inside the body.

Both loops can perform the same tasks.

---

## 11. Understanding x = x++ + x++

### Question

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int x = 2;

    x = x++ + x++;

    printf("%d\n", x);

    return 0;
}
```

The program appears to print `5`. Why isn't the final value `6`?

### Important Explanation

**The expression `x = x++ + x++;` has undefined behavior in C.**

The variable `x` is modified multiple times without the required sequencing between those modifications.

Therefore, the C standard does not guarantee that the output will be `5`, `6`, or any other particular value.

It is incorrect to assume that the compiler must evaluate the expression as:

```c
x = 2 + 3;
```

The behavior is undefined, so the output cannot be reliably predicted.

### What Does x++ Mean?

The post-increment operator `x++` produces the old value of `x` as its expression result and schedules an increment of `x`.

For example:

```c
int x = 2;
int y = x++;

printf("x = %d\n", x);
printf("y = %d\n", y);
```

Output:

```text
x = 3
y = 2
```

Here, `y` receives the old value, `2`, while `x` becomes `3`.

---

## 12. Why Doesn't the Second x++ Determine the Final Value?

Consider:

```c
x = x++ + x++;
```

It may seem that:

1. The first `x++` uses `2`.
2. The second `x++` uses `3`.
3. The sum becomes `5`.
4. The assignment stores `5` in `x`.

But this explanation is not valid for C because the expression has undefined behavior.

The key point is that **the post-increment operator does not guarantee a simple left-to-right sequence for the entire expression**.

The final assignment is not guaranteed to preserve the effects you might expect from both increments. Because the program has undefined behavior, there is no standard-defined final value.

To understand post-increment safely, use separate statements.

For example:

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int x = 2;

    int a = x++;
    int b = x++;

    printf("a = %d\n", a);
    printf("b = %d\n", b);
    printf("x = %d\n", x);

    return 0;
}
```

Output:

```text
a = 2
b = 3
x = 4
```

Each increment occurs in a separate full expression, so the behavior is well-defined.

---

## 13. Pre-Increment and Post-Increment

### Post-Increment: x++

The current value is produced, and then the variable is incremented.

```c
int x = 2;
int y = x++;

printf("x = %d, y = %d\n", x, y);
```

Output:

```text
x = 3, y = 2
```

### Pre-Increment: ++x

The variable is incremented first, and the new value is produced.

```c
int x = 2;
int y = ++x;

printf("x = %d, y = %d\n", x, y);
```

Output:

```text
x = 3, y = 3
```

### Important Warning

Do not assume that expressions such as these have predictable results in C:

```c
x = ++x + ++x;
x = x++ + ++x;
x = x++ + x++;
```

These expressions modify the same scalar object multiple times without the required sequencing. Their behavior is undefined in C.

Instead, use separate statements:

```c
int x = 2;

int a = x++;
int b = ++x;

printf("a = %d\n", a);
printf("b = %d\n", b);
printf("x = %d\n", x);
```

Output:

```text
a = 2
b = 4
x = 4
```

Explanation:

1. `a = x++` stores `2` in `a`, then `x` becomes `3`.
2. `b = ++x` increments `x` to `4`, then stores `4` in `b`.
3. The final value of `x` is `4`.

---

# Key Takeaways

1. In C, zero represents false, and nonzero values represent true.
2. A `for` loop can omit its condition, as in `for (;;)`, to create an unconditional loop.
3. A `while (1)` loop continues until it is terminated or reaches a `break` statement.
4. `break` exits the nearest enclosing loop.
5. `continue` skips the rest of the current iteration and proceeds to the next iteration.
6. `x++` produces the old value, while `++x` produces the incremented value.
7. Avoid modifying the same variable multiple times in one expression without proper sequencing.
8. **Undefined behavior means the C standard imposes no requirements on what the program does.** Never rely on a particular output from an expression with undefined behavior.

# C Programming Questions and Explanations — Complete Combined Notes

## 1. Replace Every Digit 2 with 8 in an Array

**Question:**

Given the array:

```c
arr[] = {21, 34, 22, 521, 23};
```

Replace every digit `2` with `8`.

**Expected output:**

```text
{81, 34, 88, 581, 83}
```

### C Program

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int arr[] = {21, 34, 22, 521, 23};
    int n = sizeof(arr) / sizeof(arr[0]);

    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        int num = arr[i];
        int updatedNum = 0;
        int multiplier = 1;

        while (num != 0) {
            int digit = num % 10;

            if (digit == 2) {
                digit = 8;
            }

            updatedNum += digit * multiplier;
            num /= 10;
            multiplier *= 10;
        }

        arr[i] = updatedNum;
    }

    printf("{");
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        printf("%d", arr[i]);

        if (i < n - 1) {
            printf(", ");
        }
    }
    printf("}\n");

    return 0;
}
```

**Output:**

```text
{81, 34, 88, 581, 83}
```

### Explanation of the important lines

```c
updatedNum += digit * multiplier;
num /= 10;
multiplier *= 10;
```

- `updatedNum += digit * multiplier;` adds the modified digit to its correct place in the new number.
- `num /= 10;` removes the last digit from the original number.
- `multiplier *= 10;` moves the place value from ones to tens, hundreds, thousands, and so on.

### Example: `num = 624`

Initially:

```c
int num = 624;
int updatedNum = 0;
int multiplier = 1;
```

| Iteration | Digit | Modified digit | Multiplier | Updated number | Remaining num |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 1 | 4 | 4 | 1 | 4 | 62 |
| 2 | 2 | 8 | 10 | 84 | 6 |
| 3 | 6 | 6 | 100 | 684 | 0 |

The resulting number is `684`.

### Example: `num = 524`

| Iteration | Digit | Modified digit | Multiplier | Calculation | Updated number |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---|---:|
| 1 | 4 | 4 | 1 | 0 + 4 × 1 | 4 |
| 2 | 2 | 8 | 10 | 4 + 8 × 10 | 84 |
| 3 | 5 | 5 | 100 | 84 + 5 × 100 | 584 |

Final result: `584`.

---

## 2. Right Shift Operator in a `for` Loop

### Program

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int i = 1024;

    for (; i; i >>= 1)
        printf("GeeksQuiz");

    return 0;
}
```

### Explanation

The loop has three parts:

```c
for (; i; i >>= 1)
```

- Initialization is omitted because `i` is initialized before the loop.
- Condition: `i` — the loop continues while `i` is nonzero.
- Update: `i >>= 1` shifts the bits of `i` one position to the right.

For this positive integer, right-shifting by one bit is equivalent to integer division by 2.

Initially, `i = 1024`.

| Iteration | Value of `i` before printing | Value after right shift |
|---|---:|---:|
| 1 | 1024 | 512 |
| 2 | 512 | 256 |
| 3 | 256 | 128 |
| 4 | 128 | 64 |
| 5 | 64 | 32 |
| 6 | 32 | 16 |
| 7 | 16 | 8 |
| 8 | 8 | 4 |
| 9 | 4 | 2 |
| 10 | 2 | 1 |
| 11 | 1 | 0 |

When `i` becomes zero, the loop stops.

**Output:** `GeeksQuiz` is printed 11 times, without spaces or newlines.

---

## 3. Using `switch` to Check Even or Odd

### Program

```c
#include <stdio.h>

#define EVEN 0
#define ODD 1

int main() {
    int i = 3;

    switch (i % 2) {
        case EVEN:
            printf("Even");
            break;

        case ODD:
            printf("Odd");
            break;

        default:
            printf("Default");
    }

    return 0;
}
```

### Explanation

```c
#define EVEN 0
#define ODD 1
```

These macros replace `EVEN` with `0` and `ODD` with `1`.

The expression:

```c
i % 2
```

calculates the remainder when `i` is divided by 2.

For `i = 3`:

```text
3 % 2 = 1
```

Therefore, `case ODD` executes.

**Output:**

```text
Odd
```

The `break` statement exits the `switch` block.

---

## 4. Understanding a `do-while` Loop

### Program

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int c = 5, no = 10;

    do {
        no /= c;
    } while (c--);

    printf("%d\n", no);

    return 0;
}
```

### Important concept

A `do-while` loop executes its body first and checks its condition afterward.

The expression:

```c
c--
```

is post-decrement. It uses the current value of `c` for the condition and then decreases `c` by one.

### Execution

| Step | `c` used in body | Calculation | `no` after calculation | Condition |
|---|---:|---|---:|---|
| 1 | 5 | 10 / 5 | 2 | `c--` evaluates to 5 |
| 2 | 4 | 2 / 4 | 0 | `c--` evaluates to 4 |
| 3 | 3 | 0 / 3 | 0 | `c--` evaluates to 3 |
| 4 | 2 | 0 / 2 | 0 | `c--` evaluates to 2 |
| 5 | 1 | 0 / 1 | 0 | `c--` evaluates to 1 |
| 6 | 0 | 0 / 0 | Undefined behavior | Not reached safely |

**Important correction:** This program has undefined behavior because it attempts to divide by zero during the sixth iteration. Therefore, its final output cannot be reliably predicted.

The loop condition does not prevent this division: the condition is checked only after the body executes.

---

## 5. Why Array Elements Cannot Be Used Directly in `case` Labels

### Original program

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int check = 20, arr[] = {10, 20, 30};

    switch (check) {
        case arr[0]:
            printf("Geeks ");

        case arr[1]:
            printf("Quiz ");

        case arr[2]:
            printf("GeeksQuiz");
    }

    return 0;
}
```

### Why does this produce a compilation error?

In C, each `case` label must be an integer constant expression.

These expressions are not valid `case` labels:

```c
case arr[0]:
case arr[1]:
case arr[2]:
```

Although the array contains fixed initial values, `arr[0]`, `arr[1]`, and `arr[2]` are array-element accesses, not integer constant expressions in C.

### Correct version using constant values

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int check = 20;

    switch (check) {
        case 10:
            printf("Geeks ");
            break;

        case 20:
            printf("Quiz ");
            break;

        case 30:
            printf("GeeksQuiz");
            break;

        default:
            break;
    }

    return 0;
}
```

**Output:**

```text
Quiz
```

### Equivalent `if-else` version using the array

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int check = 20;
    int arr[] = {10, 20, 30};

    if (check == arr[0])
        printf("Geeks ");
    else if (check == arr[1])
        printf("Quiz ");
    else if (check == arr[2])
        printf("GeeksQuiz");

    return 0;
}
```

This version works because ordinary `if` conditions can compare variables and array elements.

---

## 6. Can Logical OR (`||`) Be Used in a `case` Label?

Consider:

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    char check = 'a';

    switch (check) {
        case 'a' || 1:
            printf("Geeks ");

        case 'b' || 2:
            printf("Quiz ");
            break;

        default:
            printf("GeeksQuiz");
    }

    return 0;
}
```

### What does `'a' || 1` mean?

The logical OR operator `||` returns `1` if at least one operand is nonzero. Otherwise, it returns `0`.

Since `'a'` is a nonzero character value:

```c
'a' || 1
```

evaluates to:

```c
1
```

Similarly:

```c
'b' || 2
```

also evaluates to:

```c
1
```

Therefore, both labels become:

```c
case 1:
```

This creates duplicate `case` values, which violates the C language requirements and causes a compilation error in a conforming implementation.

**Important:** Logical OR is allowed in a `case` expression when the complete expression is an integer constant expression and the resulting value is unique among the labels. The problem is not the operator itself; it is the duplicate result.

### Example of a valid logical OR expression

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int check = 1;

    switch (check) {
        case (0 || 1):
            printf("One");
            break;

        case (2 || 3):
            printf("Also one");
            break;

        default:
            printf("Default");
    }

    return 0;
}
```

This example is **invalid** because both expressions evaluate to `1`. It illustrates why the result matters, not merely whether the operator is allowed.

A valid version is:

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int check = 1;

    switch (check) {
        case (0 || 1):
            printf("One");
            break;

        case (0 || 0):
            printf("Zero");
            break;

        default:
            printf("Default");
    }

    return 0;
}
```

**Output:**

```text
One
```

Here, `(0 || 1)` evaluates to `1`, and `(0 || 0)` evaluates to `0`, so the labels are distinct.

### Using multiple values for the same action

If you want both `'a'` and `1` to trigger the same action, write:

```c
switch (check) {
    case 'a':
    case 1:
        printf("Geeks ");
        break;

    case 'b':
    case 2:
        printf("Quiz ");
        break;

    default:
        printf("GeeksQuiz");
}
```

These are separate labels that share the same block of code. They do not use logical OR.

---

## 7. Which Operators Are Valid in `switch` and `case` Expressions?

A `switch` statement in C has this structure:

```c
switch (expression) {
    case constant_expression:
        statements;
        break;

    default:
        statements;
}
```

The `switch` expression must have an appropriate integer or enumeration type in standard C. Each `case` label must be an integer constant expression, and its value must be unique within that `switch`.

### Operators that can be used in constant expressions

Examples include:

- Arithmetic: `+`, `-`, `*`, `/`, `%`
- Bitwise: `&`, `|`, `^`, `~`, `<<`, `>>`
- Logical: `&&`, `||`, `!`
- Relational: `<`, `>`, `<=`, `>=`
- Equality: `==`, `!=`
- Conditional: `?:`

The operands must satisfy the rules for integer constant expressions. For example:

```c
case 3 + 4:
```

is valid because the expression evaluates to the constant `7`.

```c
case 'a' | 1:
```

can also be valid if its value is unique among the other labels.

However, this is not valid:

```c
case arr[0]:
```

because an array-element access is not an integer constant expression.

### Important distinction

The operators above are not restricted to `case` labels alone. The key rule is that the complete `case` expression must be a valid integer constant expression.

You cannot use a normal variable comparison to make a `case` label depend on runtime data.

---

## 8. `break` and `continue` Inside a `while` Loop

### Program

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int i = -5;

    while (i <= 5) {
        if (i >= 0)
            break;
        else {
            i++;
            continue;
        }

        printf("GeeksQuiz");
    }

    return 0;
}
```

### What does `break` do?

The `break` statement immediately exits the nearest enclosing loop or `switch` statement.

Here, it exits the entire `while` loop, not just the `if` statement.

### What does `continue` do?

The `continue` statement skips the remaining statements in the current loop iteration and proceeds to the next iteration.

### Execution step by step

Initially:

```c
i = -5;
```

| Iteration | `i` at start | Condition `i >= 0` | Action | `i` afterward |
|---|---:|---|---|---:|
| 1 | -5 | False | Increment and continue | -4 |
| 2 | -4 | False | Increment and continue | -3 |
| 3 | -3 | False | Increment and continue | -2 |
| 4 | -2 | False | Increment and continue | -1 |
| 5 | -1 | False | Increment and continue | 0 |
| 6 | 0 | True | Execute `break` | 0 |

When `i` becomes `0`, the `if` condition is true, so `break` exits the `while` loop.

The statement:

```c
printf("GeeksQuiz");
```

is never executed because every negative-value iteration encounters `continue`, and the nonnegative-value iteration encounters `break`.

**Output:** Nothing is printed.

---

# Quick Revision

1. `num % 10` extracts the last digit of an integer.
2. `num /= 10` removes the last digit using integer division.
3. `multiplier *= 10` advances the place value.
4. `i >>= 1` shifts the bits of a positive integer one position to the right.
5. `switch` matches its expression against constant `case` values.
6. `case` labels must be integer constant expressions with unique values.
7. Logical operators such as `||` can appear in valid constant expressions, but duplicate results cause a problem.
8. `break` exits the nearest enclosing loop or `switch`.
9. `continue` skips the rest of the current loop iteration.
10. A `do-while` loop executes its body before checking its condition.
11. Integer division truncates fractional parts, so `2 / 4` evaluates to `0` when both operands are integers.
12. Division by zero causes undefined behavior in C.

# C Programming: Understanding `%s`, `%c`, Pointers, and Array Indexing

## 1. C Program

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main()
{
    char str[] = "GeeksQuiz";

    printf("%s %s %s ", &str[5], &5[str], str + 5);

    printf("%c %c %c ", *(str + 6), str[6], 6[str]);

    return 0;
}
```

## 2. Output

```text
Quiz Quiz Quiz s s s
```

## 3. Explanation of the First `printf()`

```c
printf("%s %s %s ", &str[5], &5[str], str + 5);
```

The string `"GeeksQuiz"` is stored as an array:

| Index | Character |
|---:|:---|
| 0 | G |
| 1 | e |
| 2 | e |
| 3 | k |
| 4 | s |
| 5 | Q |
| 6 | u |
| 7 | i |
| 8 | z |
| 9 | `\0` |

The null character `\0` marks the end of the string.

### A. `&str[5]`

- `str[5]` gives the character `'Q'`.
- `&str[5]` gives the address of that character.
- `%s` starts printing from that address until it encounters `\0`.

Therefore, the output is:

```text
Quiz
```

### B. `&5[str]`

In C, the expression `str[5]` is equivalent to `5[str]`.

This is because array indexing is defined as:

```c
str[5] == *(str + 5)
5[str] == *(5 + str)
```

Since addition is commutative:

```c
str + 5 == 5 + str
```

Therefore:

```c
&5[str] == &str[5]
```

Both expressions point to the character `'Q'`, so `%s` prints:

```text
Quiz
```

### C. `str + 5`

Here, `str` points to the first character of the string. Adding `5` moves the pointer to the character at index `5`, which is `'Q'`.

Thus, `str + 5` points to the substring `"Quiz"`.

The output is:

```text
Quiz
```

All three expressions print the same substring:

```text
Quiz Quiz Quiz
```

## 4. Explanation of the Second `printf()`

```c
printf("%c %c %c ", *(str + 6), str[6], 6[str]);
```

Here, `%c` is used to print individual characters.

### A. `*(str + 6)`

- `str + 6` points to the character at index `6`.
- `*(str + 6)` accesses that character.
- The character is `'u'`.

### B. `str[6]`

This directly accesses the character at index `6`.

The character is `'u'`.

### C. `6[str]`

This is equivalent to `str[6]`, so it also gives `'u'`.

Therefore, the second `printf()` prints:

```text
u u u
```

The complete output is:

```text
Quiz Quiz Quiz u u u
```

## 5. Why Can't We Use `%s` with `*str`?

Consider this example:

```c
char str[] = "GeeksQuiz";

printf("%s", *str);
```

This is incorrect because `%s` expects a pointer to a null-terminated character string, but `*str` gives a single character.

### What is the difference?

**`str`**

```c
str
```

Represents the address of the first character of the array. It can be passed to `%s` because it points to a null-terminated string.

**`*str`**

```c
*str
```

Dereferences the pointer and gives the first character:

```c
'G'
```

It is a character, not a pointer to a string.

### Correct usage

To print the entire string:

```c
printf("%s", str);
```

Output:

```text
GeeksQuiz
```

To print the first character:

```c
printf("%c", *str);
```

Output:

```text
G
```

To print the string starting from the sixth character:

```c
printf("%s", str + 5);
```

Output:

```text
Quiz
```

### Important distinction

| Expression | What it represents | Correct format specifier |
|---|---|---|
| `str` | Pointer to the beginning of the string | `%s` |
| `*str` | First character, `'G'` | `%c` |
| `str[5]` | Character `'Q'` | `%c` |
| `&str[5]` | Address of `'Q'`, pointing to `"Quiz"` | `%s` |
| `str + 5` | Pointer to the character `'Q'` | `%s` |
| `*(str + 6)` | Character `'u'` | `%c` |

**Important:** Using `%s` with `*str` causes undefined behavior because `%s` expects a pointer, whereas `*str` is a character. The program might print unexpected output or crash.

## 6. Key Concepts to Remember

1. `%s` prints a null-terminated string starting at a given address.
2. `%c` prints one character.
3. `str[i]` is equivalent to `*(str + i)`.
4. `i[str]` is also equivalent to `str[i]`.
5. `&str[i]` gives the address of the character at index `i`.
6. `str + i` points to the character at index `i`.
7. `*str` gives the first character, while `str` points to the first character.

The easiest way to remember the main difference is:

```c
printf("%s", str);   // Prints the string
printf("%c", *str);  // Prints the first character
```

# C Programming – Main Function, Nested Functions, and Simple Functions

## 1. Can the return type of `main()` be `float`?

**Question:** Can the return type of `main()` be `float`?

**Answer:**

In standard C, the return type of `main()` should be `int`, not `float`.

Example:

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main()
{
    printf("Hello World");
    return 0;
}
```

The return value indicates the program's exit status. Usually, `return 0;` indicates successful execution.

Using `float` as the return type of `main()` is not standard C, even if a particular compiler accepts the program.

## 2. But `float` is also running!

**Question:** But `float` is also running.

**Answer:**

Some compilers may accept a program that uses `float main()`, but this does not make it valid standard C.

Example:

```c
#include <stdio.h>

float main()
{
    printf("Hello World");
    return 0.0;
}
```

A compiler might compile this program, depending on its settings. However, the program does not follow the standard C requirements for `main()`.

**Remember:** A program compiling and running successfully does not necessarily mean that it follows the C standard.

Use `int main()` for standard C programs.

---

## 3. Nested Functions in C

**Question:** What are nested functions in C?

**Answer:**

A nested function is a function defined inside another function.

Standard C does not support defining a function inside another function. However, some compilers, such as GCC, support nested functions as a compiler-specific extension.

Example using the GCC extension:

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main()
{
    int outerFunction(int a)
    {
        int innerFunction(int b)
        {
            return a + b;
        }

        return innerFunction(5);
    }

    printf("%d\n", outerFunction(3));

    return 0;
}
```

**Output:**

```text
8
```

**Explanation:**

1. `outerFunction(3)` is called with `a = 3`.
2. Inside `outerFunction()`, the nested function `innerFunction()` is defined.
3. `innerFunction(5)` adds `a` and `b`, giving `3 + 5 = 8`.
4. The result `8` is returned and printed.

**Important:** This example uses a GCC extension. It is not valid standard C and may not compile with other compilers.

---

## 4. Simple Function Example in C

**Question:** Give a simple function example.

**Answer:**

Here is a simple example of a function that adds two numbers.

```c
#include <stdio.h>

// Function declaration
int addNumbers(int a, int b);

int main()
{
    // Function call
    int result = addNumbers(3, 5);

    printf("Sum: %d\n", result);

    return 0;
}

// Function definition
int addNumbers(int a, int b)
{
    int sum = a + b;
    return sum;
}
```

**Output:**

```text
Sum: 8
```

### Explanation

**1. Function declaration (prototype)**

```c
int addNumbers(int a, int b);
```

This tells the compiler about the function's name, return type, and parameters.

**2. Function call**

```c
int result = addNumbers(3, 5);
```

This calls the function with the arguments `3` and `5`.

**3. Function definition**

```c
int addNumbers(int a, int b)
{
    int sum = a + b;
    return sum;
}
```

The function adds the two numbers and returns the result.

**4. Printing the result**

```c
printf("Sum: %d\n", result);
```

This displays `Sum: 8`.

---

## 5. Difference Between a Simple Function and a Nested Function

| Feature | Simple function | Nested function |
|---|---|---|
| Definition | Defined outside another function | Defined inside another function |
| Standard C support | Yes | No |
| Portability | Portable when standard features are used | Depends on compiler extensions |
| Example | `addNumbers()` | `innerFunction()` inside `outerFunction()` |

### Final points to remember

- In standard C, use `int main()` as the return type of `main()`.
- Standard C does not allow defining a function inside another function.
- A function can call another function in standard C.
- Function declarations, calls, definitions, parameters, and return values are important concepts to understand.

# Reverse Each Number in an Array in C

## 1. Question

Given an array such as:

```c
int arr[] = {28, 20, 82, 20};
```

Reverse each number in the array.

For example:
- 28 → 82
- 20 → 02
- 82 → 28
- 230 → 032

**Expected output:**

```text
82, 02, 28, 02
```

Note: Since `020` is an octal constant in C, use `20` if you mean the decimal number twenty. Also, integers cannot store leading zeros as part of their numeric value. To display leading zeros, use formatting or strings.

## 2. Reverse a Number in C

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int reverseNumber(int number)
{
    int reversed = 0;

    while (number > 0)
    {
        reversed = reversed * 10 + number % 10;
        number = number / 10;
    }

    return reversed;
}

int main()
{
    int number = 230;

    printf("%d", reverseNumber(number));

    return 0;
}
```

Output:

```text
32
```

### Explanation

For the number `230`:

1. `230 % 10` gives `0`.
2. `23 % 10` gives `3`.
3. `2 % 10` gives `2`.

The reversed numeric value is `32`. The leading zero cannot be stored in an integer.

## 3. Reverse Every Number in an Array

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int reverseNumber(int number)
{
    int reversed = 0;

    while (number > 0)
    {
        reversed = reversed * 10 + number % 10;
        number = number / 10;
    }

    return reversed;
}

int main()
{
    int arr[] = {28, 20, 82, 20};
    int size = sizeof(arr) / sizeof(arr[0]);

    for (int i = 0; i < size; i++)
    {
        arr[i] = reverseNumber(arr[i]);
    }

    for (int i = 0; i < size; i++)
    {
        printf("%d ", arr[i]);
    }

    return 0;
}
```

Output:

```text
82 2 28 2
```

## 4. Display Leading Zeros

If you want `230` to display as `032`, use `%03d` with the reversed value.

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int reverseNumber(int number)
{
    int reversed = 0;

    while (number > 0)
    {
        reversed = reversed * 10 + number % 10;
        number = number / 10;
    }

    return reversed;
}

int main()
{
    int arr[] = {28, 20, 82, 20};
    int size = sizeof(arr) / sizeof(arr[0]);

    for (int i = 0; i < size; i++)
    {
        printf("%03d ", reverseNumber(arr[i]));
    }

    return 0;
}
```

Output:

```text
082 002 028 002
```

Here, `%03d` prints every number using at least three digits, adding leading zeros when necessary.

## 5. Important Concept

- `% 10` extracts the last digit.
- `/ 10` removes the last digit when working with positive integers.
- `reversed * 10 + number % 10` appends the extracted digit to the reversed number.
- An integer does not preserve leading zeros.
- `%03d` displays an integer using at least three digits, but it does not preserve the original number's digit count automatically.

If you want exactly the reversed digits, including leading zeros, for numbers with different lengths, use strings instead of integers.
C PROGRAMMING — COMBINED QUESTIONS AND ANSWERS
================================================

This document combines the distinct C programming questions from the conversation, removes repeated explanations, and organizes the concepts into one study reference.

1. BITWISE OPERATORS
--------------------

Q1. How does bitwise AND check whether a number is odd or even?

Example:
    int x = 19;
    (x & 1) ? printf("Odd") : printf("Even");

Output: Odd

19 in binary is 10011. The last bit is 1, so x & 1 is nonzero.
Rule:
- (n & 1) == 1 means odd.
- (n & 1) == 0 means even.

Q2. What is the difference between & and &&?

Example:
    int x = 2, y = 5;
    (x & y) ? printf("True ") : printf("False ");
    (x && y) ? printf("True ") : printf("False ");

Output: False True

Bitwise AND (&) compares individual bits:
    2 = 010
    5 = 101
        ---
    AND 000 = 0

Logical AND (&&) checks whether both values are nonzero. Since 2 and 5 are nonzero, the second condition is true.

For x = 3 and y = 5:
    3 = 011
    5 = 101
        ---
    AND 001 = 1
Both conditions are true.

For x = -4 and y = 5, on a typical two's-complement system, the bitwise AND is nonzero, so both conditions are true.

Q3. What does a | 1 do?

Example:
    int a = 10;
    printf("%d", a | 1);

Output: 11

The bitwise OR operator (|) sets the rightmost bit to 1.
Examples:
    5 | 1  = 5
    10 | 1 = 11
    8 | 1  = 9
    7 | 1  = 7

Q4. How do you access or modify a particular bit?

Bit positions are counted from the right, starting at position 0. Use (1 << position) to create a mask.

Check a bit:
    int x = 12;  /* binary 1100 */
    if (x & (1 << 2))
        printf("Bit is 1");
    else
        printf("Bit is 0");

Output: Bit is 1

Set a bit to 1:
    int x = 5;  /* binary 0101 */
    x |= (1 << 3);
    printf("%d", x);

Output: 13

Clear a bit to 0:
    int x = 15; /* binary 1111 */
    x &= ~(1 << 2);
    printf("%d", x);

Output: 11

Toggle a bit:
    int x = 12; /* binary 1100 */
    x ^= (1 << 2);
    printf("%d", x);

Output: 8

Remember:
- Check: x & (1 << p)
- Set: x |= (1 << p)
- Clear: x &= ~(1 << p)
- Toggle: x ^= (1 << p)

Q5. Is %b a valid binary format specifier in C?

Standard C's printf does not provide a portable %b binary conversion specifier. A helper function can print a number in binary:

    #include <stdio.h>

    void printBinary(unsigned int n) {
        if (n > 1)
            printBinary(n / 2);
        printf("%u", n % 2);
    }

    int main(void) {
        printBinary(15);
        return 0;
    }

Output: 1111

Q6. How can binary digits be stored in an array?

    #include <stdio.h>

    void toBinary(unsigned int n, int bits[], int size) {
        for (int i = size - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
            bits[i] = n % 2;
            n /= 2;
        }
    }

    int main(void) {
        int bits[8];
        toBinary(15, bits, 8);

        for (int i = 0; i < 8; i++)
            printf("%d", bits[i]);

        return 0;
    }

Output: 00001111

Q7. What is the result of -1 << 1?

In standard C, left-shifting a negative signed integer has undefined behavior. Some systems may produce -2, but portable code must not rely on that result.


2. DATA TYPES AND sizeof
------------------------

Q8. What is sizeof(a + d) when a is int and d is double?

    #include <stdio.h>

    int main(void) {
        int a = 0;
        double d = 10.21;
        printf("%zu", sizeof(a + d));
        return 0;
    }

On a typical system, output: 8

In a + d, a is converted to double, so the expression has type double. Use %zu to print the size_t value returned by sizeof.

Q9. What is wrong with Float a = 5?

C is case-sensitive. The correct type name is lowercase float:
    float a = 5;
    double d = 10.21;
    printf("%zu", sizeof(a + d));

The expression a + d has type double; its size is typically 8 bytes.

Q10. What happens if you store a nine-bit value in char?

A char is one C byte, and a byte has at least eight bits. On common systems, a byte is eight bits, so an eight-bit char cannot represent every possible nine-bit value.

Example on an eight-bit-byte system:
    unsigned char x = 511;
    printf("%u", (unsigned int)x);

Output: 255

The conversion to unsigned char wraps modulo 256 on such a system. Use a suitable wider integer type for larger values.


3. STRINGS AND POINTERS
-----------------------

Q11. Why does == not compare the contents of two strings?

    char str1[] = "Hello";
    char str2[] = "Hello";

    if (str1 == str2)
        printf("Equal");
    else
        printf("Not equal");

Output: Not equal

The arrays are separate objects. Comparing str1 and str2 compares their addresses, not their characters.

Q12. How does strcmp() work?

    #include <stdio.h>
    #include <string.h>

    int main(void) {
        char str1[] = "Hello";
        char str2[] = "Hello";

        if (strcmp(str1, str2) == 0)
            printf("Strings are equal");
        else
            printf("Strings are not equal");

        return 0;
    }

Output: Strings are equal

strcmp():
- Returns 0 when strings are equal.
- Returns a negative value if the first string is lexicographically smaller.
- Returns a positive value if the first string is lexicographically greater.
The exact nonzero value is not guaranteed to be -1 or 1.

Q13. Do identical string literals have the same address?

    char *str1 = "Hello";
    char *str2 = "Hello";

A compiler may store identical string literals at the same address, but this is not guaranteed. str1 == str2 compares addresses, not contents. Use strcmp(str1, str2) == 0 to compare contents.

Q14. Can == compare a float and an integer?

Yes.

    float f = 10.1f;
    int i = 10;
    printf("%s", f == i ? "Equal" : "Not equal");

Output: Not equal

The integer 10 is converted to a floating-point value for the comparison. 10.1f is not equal to 10.0f.

Q15. Why does assigning 10.1 to int make a comparison false?

    float floatValue = 10.1f;
    int intValue = 10.1;

    if (floatValue == intValue)
        printf("Equal");
    else
        printf("Not equal");

Output: Not equal

When 10.1 is converted to int, its fractional part is discarded and intValue becomes 10. The comparison is approximately 10.1 against 10.0.

Q16. Why can 0.1 + 0.2 == 0.3 be false?

Many decimal fractions cannot be represented exactly in binary floating point, so the computed result may differ slightly from 0.3. For approximate comparisons, use a suitable tolerance:

    #include <math.h>

    if (fabs(x - y) < 1e-9)
        printf("Approximately equal");

Choose a tolerance appropriate to the values and precision required.


4. INCREMENT AND ASSIGNMENT OPERATORS
-------------------------------------

Q17. What happens with printf("%d%d%d", a+=2, a+=3, a+=2)?

    int a = 10;
    printf("%d%d%d", a += 2, a += 3, a += 2);

This expression has undefined behavior in C: multiple unsequenced modifications of a occur while evaluating the printf arguments. A compiler may print something, but there is no guaranteed output.

Write the operations separately:

    int a = 10;
    a += 2;
    printf("%d ", a);
    a += 3;
    printf("%d ", a);
    a += 2;
    printf("%d", a);

Output: 12 15 17

Q18. What happens with a = ++a + ++a + ++a?

    int a = 10;
    a = ++a + ++a + ++a;

This has undefined behavior in C because a is modified multiple times without the required sequencing. Do not rely on any particular output.

Q19. What is the difference between i++ and ++i?

- i++ (post-increment) yields the old value and then increments i.
- ++i (pre-increment) increments i first and yields the new value.

Example:
    int i = 5;
    printf("%d ", i++);
    printf("%d", i);

Output: 5 6

Example:
    int i = 5;
    printf("%d ", ++i);
    printf("%d", i);

Output: 6 6


5. RECURSION AND STATIC VARIABLES
---------------------------------

Q20. How does a recursive function with a static variable work?

    #include <stdio.h>

    int main(void) {
        static int i = 5;

        if (--i) {
            main();
            printf("%d ", i);
        }

        return 0;
    }

On implementations that allow this recursion, the observed output is commonly:
    0 0 0 0

The static variable is initialized once and shared by the recursive calls. Each call decrements i. When i reaches 0, the condition becomes false. As the earlier calls return, they all print the same shared value, 0.

Important: The C standard does not permit calling main() recursively. Use a separate function for portable recursion examples.


6. CONDITIONAL STATEMENTS AND LOOPS
-----------------------------------

Q21. Is if (1); valid?

Yes.

    if (1);
    printf("Hello");

Output: Hello

The semicolon is an empty statement. The if controls only that empty statement, not the printf. Correct form:

    if (1) {
        printf("Hello");
    }

Q22. What happens in this while loop?

    signed int i = 10;
    while (i-- >= 0)
        printf("%u ", i);

On a typical system with 32-bit int and unsigned int, people may observe:
    9 8 7 6 5 4 3 2 1 0 4294967295

However, %u expects unsigned int, while i is int. This format mismatch causes undefined behavior, so the output is not guaranteed.

Use %d for a signed int:
    while (i-- >= 0)
        printf("%d ", i);

This prints 9 down to -1 before the condition becomes false.

Q23. What are the categories of control-flow statements?

- Selection: if, if-else, switch
- Iteration: for, while, do-while
- Jump: break, continue, goto, return

A while loop is an iteration statement. An if statement selects a path. goto transfers control to a labeled statement.

Q24. Give an example of goto.

    #include <stdio.h>

    int main(void) {
        int count = 0;

    start:
        if (count < 5) {
            printf("%d ", count);
            count++;
            goto start;
        }

        return 0;
    }

Output: 0 1 2 3 4

goto start transfers execution back to the label start. Use goto carefully because it can make code harder to follow.


7. SWITCH STATEMENTS
--------------------

Q25. Give an example of a valid switch.

    int choice = 2;

    switch (choice) {
        case 1:
            printf("One");
            break;
        case 2:
            printf("Two");
            break;
        default:
            printf("Invalid");
    }

Output: Two

Q26. What makes a switch statement invalid?

A. Duplicate case values:
    switch (x) {
        case 1: printf("One"); break;
        case 1: printf("Again"); break;
    }
Invalid because case values must be unique.

B. Floating-point case value:
    switch (x) {
        case 1.5:
            printf("Hello");
            break;
    }
Invalid because case labels must be integer constant expressions.

C. String or pointer as switch expression:
    char *s = "Hello";
    switch (s) {
        case 1: printf("Hello"); break;
    }
Invalid because the switch expression must have an integer type.

D. Variable as a case label:
    int n = 2;
    switch (x) {
        case n:
            printf("Two");
            break;
    }
Invalid because n is a variable, not an integer constant expression.

E. Missing break:
    switch (x) {
        case 1:
            printf("One");
        case 2:
            printf("Two");
            break;
    }
This is valid C. If x is 1, it prints OneTwo due to fall-through.

Q27. Is short int allowed in switch?

Yes. The switch expression can use integer types such as char, short int, int, and enum types.

Example:
    short int x = 2;
    switch (x) {
        case 1: printf("One"); break;
        case 2: printf("Two"); break;
    }

Output: Two


8. FOR LOOPS
------------

Q28. Give examples of invalid for loops.

A. Missing semicolons:
    for (int i = 0 i < 5 i++) {
        printf("%d", i);
    }
Invalid syntax. Correct:
    for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++) {
        printf("%d ", i);
    }

B. Missing closing parenthesis:
    for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++ {
        printf("%d", i);
    }
Invalid syntax.

C. Undeclared variable:
    for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++, j++) {
        printf("%d", i);
    }
Invalid if j has not been declared.

D. Infinite loop:
    for (int i = 0; i < 5; ) {
        printf("%d ", i);
    }
This is valid C, but it loops forever because i never changes.

for (;;) is also valid C and creates an infinite loop. Omitting initialization or iteration expressions does not automatically make a for loop invalid.


9. NESTED LOOPS AND STAR PATTERNS
---------------------------------

Q29. Explain this nested-loop star pattern.

    #include <stdio.h>

    int main(void) {
        int rows = 5;

        for (int i = 0; i < rows; i++) {
            for (int j = 0; j < 2 * i; j++) {
                printf(" ");
            }

            for (int k = 0; k < rows - i; k++) {
                printf("* ");
            }

            printf("\\n");
        }

        return 0;
    }

Output:
* * * * *
  * * * *
    * * *
      * *
        *

Explanation:
- rows = 5 sets the number of rows.
- The outer loop controls the rows.
- The first inner loop prints 2 * i spaces.
- The second inner loop prints rows - i stars.
- printf("\\n") moves to the next line.

Spaces and stars by row:
Row 0: 0 spaces, 5 stars
Row 1: 2 spaces, 4 stars
Row 2: 4 spaces, 3 stars
Row 3: 6 spaces, 2 stars
Row 4: 8 spaces, 1 star


10. REVERSING NUMBERS AND PRESERVING ZEROS
------------------------------------------

Q30. How do you reverse digits?

Examples:
- 123 becomes 321.
- 1290 becomes 0921 if displayed with four digits.
- 0450 becomes 0540 if the leading zero is preserved as part of the input.

Q31. How do you reverse an integer using arithmetic?

    #include <stdio.h>

    int main(void) {
        int n = 123;
        int rev = 0;

        while (n > 0) {
            int digit = n % 10;
            rev = rev * 10 + digit;
            n /= 10;
        }

        printf("%d", rev);
        return 0;
    }

Output: 321

n % 10 extracts the last digit. rev * 10 + digit appends it to the reversed number. n /= 10 removes the last digit.

Q32. How do you preserve leading zeros when displaying a reversed number?

Integer values do not retain leading zeros. To display 3 as 003:
    printf("%03d", 3);

Output: 003

To display 921 as 0921:
    printf("%04d", 921);

Output: 0921

Q33. How do you reverse input such as 0320 into 0230?

Read the input as a string to preserve leading zeros:

    #include <stdio.h>
    #include <string.h>

    int main(void) {
        char n[] = "0320";
        int len = (int)strlen(n);

        for (int i = len - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
            printf("%c", n[i]);
        }

        return 0;
    }

Output: 0230

Examples:
Input "1290" -> "0921"
Input "0450" -> "0540"
Input "123"  -> "321"
Input "300"  -> "003"
Input "0320" -> "0230"

Use a string whenever leading zeros must be preserved.


11. QUICK REVISION
------------------

- Odd/even: n & 1 checks the last bit.
- Bitwise OR: n | 1 sets the last bit.
- Bitwise AND (&) compares bits.
- Logical AND (&&) checks truth values.
- Set a bit: x |= (1 << p).
- Clear a bit: x &= ~(1 << p).
- Toggle a bit: x ^= (1 << p).
- Check a bit: x & (1 << p).
- Standard printf has no portable %b binary specifier.
- Use %zu to print sizeof results.
- Use strcmp() to compare string contents.
- == on pointers compares addresses.
- Floating-point comparisons may need a tolerance.
- i++ yields the old value; ++i yields the new value.
- A static local variable retains its value between calls.
- if (condition); has an empty controlled statement.
- switch needs an integer expression and unique case values.
- for (;;) is a valid infinite loop.
- while is an iteration statement.
- goto transfers control to a label.
- Integer reversal cannot preserve leading zeros.
- String reversal preserves every character, including zeros.

END OF DOCUMENT
C AND C++ PROGRAMMING — COMBINED NOTES

1. VARIABLE-SIZE ARRAYS
In older Microsoft Visual C++ compilers, int arr[iSize] is invalid when iSize is determined at runtime. C++ requires a compile-time array size for ordinary arrays. Use a fixed-size array, malloc in C, or vector in modern C++.

2. ARRAY SIZE ERROR
Errors C2057, C2466, C2133 occur because the compiler does not accept the runtime size as a constant expression. const int iSize = 0 is also not a usable runtime array size; it is explicitly zero. Read iSize first, validate it, then allocate memory dynamically or use a fixed-size array.

3. INPUT AND ARRAY INDEXING
Use scanf("%d", &arriArray[iIndex]); not &arriArray[iSize]. Valid indices are 0 through iSize-1. Loop while iLoop < iSize, not iLoop < arriArray[iSize].

4. CHANGE ONE DIGIT INTO ANOTHER IN AN ARRAY
#include <stdio.h>
#define MAX_SIZE 100
int main(void) {
    int arr[MAX_SIZE], size, before, after, i;
    printf("Size: ");
    scanf("%d", &size);
    if (size < 1 || size > MAX_SIZE) return 1;
    for (i = 0; i < size; i++) scanf("%d", &arr[i]);
    printf("Digit to change: "); scanf("%d", &before);
    printf("Replacement digit: "); scanf("%d", &after);
    for (i = 0; i < size; i++) {
        int n = arr[i], result = 0, place = 1;
        if (n == 0 && before == 0) result = after;
        while (n > 0) {
            int digit = n % 10;
            if (digit == before) digit = after;
            result += digit * place;
            place *= 10;
            n /= 10;
        }
        printf("%d ", result);
    }
    return 0;
}
Example: input 1231, 080, 822, 088; change 8 to 4 gives 1231, 040, 422, 044 when output formatting preserves the original width. The integer version cannot preserve leading zeros by itself.

5. WHY THE REPLACEMENT CODE CAN PRINT WRONG RESULTS
Initialize result to 0 and place to 1. For every digit, use result += digit * place; then place *= 10. If place starts at 0, the result remains 0. Process n == 0 separately if zero itself must be replaced.

6. WHY INTEGER INPUT LOSES LEADING ZEROS
scanf("%d", &number) stores a numeric value, not its original text. For example, 020 becomes integer 20 and 003 becomes integer 3. Use strings when leading zeros must be retained.

7. REVERSE NUMBERS WHILE PRESERVING ZEROS
If the input is 0230 and the expected reversed output is 0320, store the input as a string. Reverse its characters. Integer arithmetic alone cannot distinguish 0230 from 230.

8. REVERSE A STRING WITHOUT A STRING FUNCTION
#include <stdio.h>
int main(void) {
    char str[100], temp;
    int length = 0, i;
    scanf("%99s", str);
    while (str[length] != '\0') length++;
    for (i = 0; i < length / 2; i++) {
        temp = str[i];
        str[i] = str[length - i - 1];
        str[length - i - 1] = temp;
    }
    printf("%s\n", str);
    return 0;
}
Example: 0230 becomes 0320; 012 becomes 210; 0340 becomes 0430.

9. ARRAY OF STRINGS
A two-dimensional character array stores multiple strings: char strings[100][100]; Each row stores one string and each column stores a character. For example, strings[0] = "Hello" and strings[1] = "World".

10. REVERSE EACH STRING IN AN ARRAY OF STRINGS
#include <stdio.h>
#define MAX_STRINGS 100
#define MAX_LENGTH 100
int main(void) {
    char strings[MAX_STRINGS][MAX_LENGTH], temp;
    int count, i, j, len;
    scanf("%d", &count);
    if (count < 1 || count > MAX_STRINGS) return 1;
    for (i = 0; i < count; i++) scanf("%99s", strings[i]);
    for (i = 0; i < count; i++) {
        len = 0;
        while (strings[i][len] != '\0') len++;
        for (j = 0; j < len / 2; j++) {
            temp = strings[i][j];
            strings[i][j] = strings[i][len - j - 1];
            strings[i][len - j - 1] = temp;
        }
    }
    for (i = 0; i < count; i++) printf("%s\n", strings[i]);
    return 0;
}

11. CHANGE A PARTICULAR CHARACTER IN AN ARRAY OF STRINGS
#include <stdio.h>
#define MAX_STRINGS 100
#define MAX_LENGTH 100
int main(void) {
    char strings[MAX_STRINGS][MAX_LENGTH], target, replacement;
    int count, i, j;
    scanf("%d", &count);
    if (count < 1 || count > MAX_STRINGS) return 1;
    for (i = 0; i < count; i++) scanf("%99s", strings[i]);
    printf("Character to change: "); scanf(" %c", &target);
    printf("Replacement character: "); scanf(" %c", &replacement);
    for (i = 0; i < count; i++)
        for (j = 0; strings[i][j] != '\0'; j++)
            if (strings[i][j] == target) strings[i][j] = replacement;
    for (i = 0; i < count; i++) printf("%s\n", strings[i]);
    return 0;
}
Example: strings Hello, World, C++; change l to x gives Hexxo, Worxd, C++.
For numeric-looking strings, enter 0234 and change 2 to 8; output is 0834.

12. EXPLANATION OF THE TWO-DIMENSIONAL ARRAY
char arr_cStrings[MAX_STRINGS][MAX_LENGTH] = {0}; declares space for up to 100 strings, each with room for up to 99 characters plus the terminating '\0'. arr_cStrings[iIndex] refers to one complete string; arr_cStrings[iIndex][iLoop] refers to one character within that string.

13. EXPLANATION OF STRING LENGTH
while (arr_cStrings[iIndex][iLength] != '\0') iLength++; counts characters until the null terminator. The null terminator marks the end of a C string and is not included in its visible length.

14. WHY scanf(" %c", &character) HAS A SPACE
The leading space tells scanf to skip whitespace, including the newline left behind by a previous input. Without it, %c may read that newline instead of the intended character.

15. POSITION-BASED DIGIT REPLACEMENT VS VALUE-BASED REPLACEMENT
Position-based: change a digit at a chosen position, such as the tens or hundreds place. Value-based: replace every occurrence of a specified digit wherever it appears. For value-based replacement, use if (digit == before) digit = after;.

16. WHY A DIGIT POSITION MUST NOT BE CONFUSED WITH A DIGIT VALUE
For 4320, changing the digit 2 to 5 produces 4350. Changing the digit at zero-based position 2 from the right produces 4520. These are different operations.

17. DYNAMIC MEMORY ALLOCATION WITH malloc
#include <stdlib.h>
int *arr = (int *)malloc(size * sizeof(int));
if (arr == NULL) return 1;
/* use arr */
free(arr);
Include <stdlib.h> to declare malloc and free in C. Check that allocation succeeds and that size is positive. In C++, prefer std::vector<int> for runtime-sized arrays.

18. WHY malloc WAS UNDECLARED
The compiler reports malloc as undeclared when its declaration is missing. Add #include <stdlib.h> for C or use the appropriate C++ headers and standard library facilities when compiling C++.

19. PRINT FIXED-WIDTH NUMBERS WITH LEADING ZEROS
printf("%03d", number); prints an integer using at least three digits, padding with zeros when needed. Example: 8 prints as 008. This only controls output width; it does not restore the original text or original width automatically.

20. INPUTS WITH LEADING ZEROS IN AN ARRAY
For inputs 020, 210, an integer array stores 20 and 210. If the desired result must retain three characters per element, use strings or remember each element's original width. Printing every value with %03d works only when every value should have width three.

21. COMMON LOOP ERRORS
Use for (i = 0; i < size; i++) to visit every array element. Accessing arr[size] is out of bounds. Initialize all variables before using them, and use the correct loop limit for the array rather than an element's value.

22. HANDLING ZERO IN DIGIT LOOPS
A loop such as while (number > 0) never runs when number is zero. Handle zero explicitly if the algorithm must process it. A loop using while (number >= 0) can become infinite because integer division eventually leaves number at zero.

23. C VS C++ VARIABLE-LENGTH ARRAYS
C99 supports variable-length arrays in certain contexts, but older Microsoft Visual C++ compilers do not support them, and standard C++ does not support them. int arr[size] may therefore produce C2057, C2466 or C2133. Use a compile-time size, malloc in C, or std::vector<int> in modern C++.

24. VALIDATING ARRAY SIZE
After reading the size, check that it is greater than zero and does not exceed the array capacity before reading elements or allocating memory.

25. GENERAL RULES
- Use single quotes for characters: '2', '8'.
- Use double quotes for strings: "0234".
- Use %d for integers and %s for null-terminated strings.
- Use %c for a single character.
- Use &variable with scanf for ordinary numeric and character variables; for a character array used as a string, pass the array name without &.
- Free memory allocated with malloc when finished.
- Use strings whenever leading zeros or the original digit count must be preserved.
# C Programming: Replace a Specific Digit in Every Array Element

## Question 1: Replace a particular digit in an array

**Question:** Given an array such as `240, 020, 892, 2000, 422`, replace every occurrence of a user-specified digit. For example, replace `2` with `8`.

**Expected output:**
```text
840, 080, 898, 8000, 488
```

## Question 2: Take the array and digits as user input

**Question:** Write a C program that accepts an array, the digit to replace, and the replacement digit from the user.

**Answer:**

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main()
{
    int n, i, target, replace;

    scanf("%d", &n);
    int a[n];

    for (i = 0; i < n; i++)
        scanf("%d", &a[i]);

    scanf("%d%d", &target, &replace);

    for (i = 0; i < n; i++)
    {
        int num = a[i], result = 0, place = 1;

        while (num != 0)
        {
            int digit = num % 10;

            if (digit == target)
                digit = replace;

            result += digit * place;
            place *= 10;
            num /= 10;
        }

        printf("%d ", result);
    }

    return 0;
}
```

**Note:** This version uses only `main()` but does not preserve leading zeros because integers do not store them.

## Question 3: Can the program be written without a separate function?

**Answer:** Yes. Put the input, digit-replacement logic, and output directly inside `main()`. A separate `changeDigit()` or `replaceDigit()` function is unnecessary.

## Question 4: Can the program be shorter and simpler?

**Answer:** The essential digit-replacement logic is:

```c
while (num != 0)
{
    int digit = num % 10;

    if (digit == target)
        digit = replace;

    result += digit * place;
    place *= 10;
    num /= 10;
}
```

- `num % 10` extracts the last digit.
- `num / 10` removes the last digit.
- `place` maintains the digit's position.
- The `if` statement replaces the target digit.

A loop is needed to process a number's digits iteratively. Recursion can avoid loops, but it requires a separate function or another more complicated approach.

## Question 5: Why are leading zeros not preserved?

**Question:** When the input is `0200`, why does the program not preserve the leading zero?

**Answer:** Because `scanf("%d", &a[i])` reads the input as an integer. The integer `0200` is stored as `200`; its original leading zero is lost. Similarly, `0002` is stored as `2`.

To preserve leading zeros, read each array element as a string using `%s` instead of `%d`.

## Question 6: Preserve leading zeros while replacing digits

**Question:** Input:

```text
Enter the size of the array: 4
Enter 4 elements for the array:
0200
302
1421
0002
Enter the digit to replace: 2
Enter the replacement digit: 3
```

Expected output:

```text
0300, 303, 1431, 0003
```

**Answer:** Use character arrays so that the original digits and leading zeros are preserved.

```c
#include <stdio.h>

int main()
{
    int n, i, j;
    char a[100][100], target, replace;

    printf("Enter the size of the array: ");
    scanf("%d", &n);

    printf("Enter the array elements: ");
    for (i = 0; i < n; i++)
        scanf("%99s", a[i]);

    printf("Enter the digit to replace: ");
    scanf(" %c", &target);

    printf("Enter the replacement digit: ");
    scanf(" %c", &replace);

    printf("Output array: ");

    for (i = 0; i < n; i++)
    {
        for (j = 0; a[i][j] != '\0'; j++)
        {
            if (a[i][j] == target)
                a[i][j] = replace;
        }

        printf("%s", a[i]);

        if (i < n - 1)
            printf(", ");
    }

    return 0;
}
```

**Output:**
```text
0300, 303, 1431, 0003
```

This program uses only `main()`, accepts user input, replaces every occurrence of the chosen digit, and preserves leading zeros.

## Question 7: What happens when the input contains zero?

**Question:** Input:

```text
Array elements:
0220
2000
0

Digit to replace: 2
Replacement digit: 4
```

**Expected output:**

```text
0440, 4000, 0
```

**Answer:** The string-based program handles this correctly because it processes every character, including zeros. The integer-based program cannot preserve the original leading zeros.

## Important points

1. Use `%d` for ordinary integer arrays when leading zeros do not matter.
2. Use `%s` with character arrays when leading zeros must be preserved.
3. The program replaces every occurrence of the selected digit, not just the first occurrence.
4. The replacement digit and target digit can be supplied by the user.
5. The string-based solution is the simplest option for the required output format.

C PROGRAMMING – DIGIT REPLACEMENT AND NUMBER REVERSAL

1. QUESTION: Explain the following C program.

#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int size, targetDigit, replacementDigit;

    printf("Enter the size of the array: ");
    scanf("%d", &size);

    int inputArray[size];

    printf("Enter %d elements for the array: ", size);
    for (int i = 0; i < size; ++i) {
        scanf("%d", &inputArray[i]);
    }

    printf("Enter the digit to replace: ");
    scanf("%d", &targetDigit);

    printf("Enter the replacement digit: ");
    scanf("%d", &replacementDigit);

    printf("Output array: ");

    for (int i = 0; i < size; ++i) {
        int num = inputArray[i];
        int result = 0;
        int multiplier = 1;

        while (num != 0) {
            int digit = num % 10;
            num /= 10;

            if (digit == targetDigit) {
                result += replacementDigit * multiplier;
            } else {
                result += digit * multiplier;
            }

            multiplier *= 10;
        }

        printf("%d ", result);
    }

    return 0;
}

ANSWER:

This program replaces every occurrence of a specified digit in each array element with another digit.

Important variables:
- size: Number of array elements.
- targetDigit: Digit to be replaced.
- replacementDigit: New digit.
- num: Current number being processed.
- result: Reconstructed number after replacement.
- multiplier: Maintains the digit's original place value (1, 10, 100, etc.).

The expression num % 10 extracts the last digit.
The expression num /= 10 removes the last digit.
The multiplier preserves the original digit positions while rebuilding the number.

2. QUESTION: Explain how the program works for the number 300 when replacing 0 with 5.

INPUT:
Number = 300
targetDigit = 0
replacementDigit = 5

ANSWER:

Initially:
num = 300
result = 0
multiplier = 1

Iteration 1:
digit = 300 % 10 = 0
num = 300 / 10 = 30

Since digit == targetDigit:
result = 0 + (5 * 1) = 5
multiplier = 10

Iteration 2:
digit = 30 % 10 = 0
num = 30 / 10 = 3

Since digit == targetDigit:
result = 5 + (5 * 10) = 55
multiplier = 100

Iteration 3:
digit = 3 % 10 = 3
num = 3 / 10 = 0

Since digit != targetDigit:
result = 55 + (3 * 100) = 355
multiplier = 1000

OUTPUT:
355

Explanation:
The program processes digits from right to left but uses multiplier to retain their original positions. Both zeros are replaced with 5, so 300 becomes 355.

3. QUESTION: How can we print the reverse of a number when the reversed number starts with zero?

Example:
Input: 230
Expected reverse: 032

ANSWER:

An integer cannot preserve leading zeros when printed using %d. For example, the integer 032 is printed as 32.

If you need to display leading zeros, print the reversed digits directly or store the number as a string.

Method 1: Print reversed digits directly.

#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int num, digit;

    printf("Enter a number: ");
    scanf("%d", &num);

    printf("Reverse: ");

    if (num == 0) {
        printf("0");
    }

    while (num != 0) {
        digit = num % 10;
        printf("%d", digit);
        num /= 10;
    }

    return 0;
}

Example:
Input: 230
Output: 032

Input: 300
Output: 003

Explanation:
- num % 10 extracts the last digit.
- printf("%d", digit) prints the digit immediately, including zeros.
- num /= 10 removes the last digit.
- The loop continues until num becomes zero.

Unlike storing the reversed result in an integer, printing each digit immediately preserves leading zeros.

NOTE:
The program above assumes non-negative input. For negative numbers, handle the sign separately.
C PROGRAMMING: READING OCTAL INPUT USING SCANF AND FIXING SEGMENTATION FAULTS

Q1. Why is 002 not accepted correctly in scanf() in C?

Answer:
If you want to read 002 as a decimal number while preserving leading zeros, use a string instead of an integer.

Using an integer:
    int num;
    scanf("%d", &num);

Input: 002
Stored value: 2

The integer does not preserve leading zeros.

Using a string:
    char num[20];
    scanf("%19s", num);

Input: 002
Stored value: "002"

If you need to preserve leading zeros, use a string.

If you want to interpret the input as an octal number, use %o.

--------------------------------------------------

Q2. Why does this code cause a segmentation fault?

Code:
    for (int i = 0; i < size; ++i) {
        scanf("%o", inputArray[i]);
        printf("number");
        printf("%o", inputArray[i]);
    }

Answer:
The problem is that scanf() requires the address of the variable where the input should be stored.

Incorrect:
    scanf("%o", inputArray[i]);

Correct:
    scanf("%o", &inputArray[i]);

The & operator provides the address of the array element.

--------------------------------------------------

Q3. How do I read and print octal numbers in an array correctly?

Answer:

#include <stdio.h>

int main(void)
{
    int size;

    printf("Enter array size: ");
    if (scanf("%d", &size) != 1 || size <= 0)
        return 1;

    int inputArray[size];

    for (int i = 0; i < size; i++) {
        printf("Enter octal number: ");

        if (scanf("%o", &inputArray[i]) != 1) {
            printf("Invalid input\n");
            return 1;
        }

        printf("Number: %o\n", inputArray[i]);
    }

    return 0;
}

Explanation:
- %o reads an octal integer.
- &inputArray[i] provides the address of the array element.
- %o prints the stored value in octal.
- scanf() returns the number of successfully read items.

Note: This example uses a variable-length array, which is supported by some C compilers but not by all C implementations.

--------------------------------------------------

Q4. I used & with scanf(), but the segmentation fault still occurs. What should I check?

Answer:

1. Ensure the array has been declared or allocated correctly.
2. Ensure size is positive and within a reasonable limit.
3. Ensure the loop condition is i < size.
4. Check that scanf() receives the correct address.
5. Check that dynamic memory allocation succeeds if using malloc().
6. Compile with warnings enabled to identify errors.

Example using dynamic memory allocation:

#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>

int main(void)
{
    int size;

    printf("Enter array size: ");

    if (scanf("%d", &size) != 1 || size <= 0)
        return 1;

    int *inputArray = malloc((size_t)size * sizeof *inputArray);

    if (inputArray == NULL) {
        printf("Memory allocation failed\n");
        return 1;
    }

    for (int i = 0; i < size; i++) {
        printf("Enter octal number: ");

        if (scanf("%o", &inputArray[i]) != 1) {
            printf("Invalid input\n");
            free(inputArray);
            return 1;
        }

        printf("Number: %o\n", inputArray[i]);
    }

    free(inputArray);
    return 0;
}

Compile using GCC:
    gcc -Wall -Wextra program.c -o program

IMPORTANT:
If the original program still crashes after adding &, the cause may be elsewhere in the code. The complete program is needed to identify the exact cause.

--------------------------------------------------

Q5. What is the difference between %d and %o in scanf()?

Answer:

%d — Reads an integer in decimal notation.
%o — Reads an integer in octal notation (base 8).

Example:

int a, b;

scanf("%d", &a);  // Input 10 means decimal 10
scanf("%o", &b);  // Input 10 means octal 10, which is decimal 8

To print:
    printf("%d", b);  // Prints 8
    printf("%o", b);  // Prints 10

Note: Octal digits range from 0 to 7. An input containing 8 or 9 is not a valid octal number.

--------------------------------------------------

Q6. How can I preserve leading zeros in a number?

Answer:

Integers do not preserve leading zeros. For example, if you read 002 using %d, the stored value is 2.

Option 1: Store the input as a string.

    char num[20];
    scanf("%19s", num);
    printf("%s\n", num);

Input: 002
Output: 002

Option 2: Print an integer with a fixed width.

    int num = 2;
    printf("%03d\n", num);

Output:
002

Use a string if the original digits must be preserved. Use formatted integer output if you only need a fixed number of digits when printing.
# C Programming: Accepting "00" as an Array Element

## Question
How can I modify this code to accept `00` as an initial value and preserve the zeros?

```c
char inputArray[size];

printf("Enter %d elements for the array: ", size);

for (int i = 0; i < size; ++i) {
    scanf("%s", &inputArray[i]);
    printf("numver");
    printf("%s", inputArray[i]);
}
```

## Answer
The problem is that `char inputArray[size]` stores individual characters, not strings. To preserve values such as `00`, declare a two-dimensional character array and use `%2s` to read up to two characters per element.

### Corrected Code

```c
#include <stdio.h>

#define SIZE 5

int main(void)
{
    char inputArray[SIZE][3];

    printf("Enter %d elements for the array:\n", SIZE);

    for (int i = 0; i < SIZE; ++i) {
        scanf("%2s", inputArray[i]);
        printf("Number: %s\n", inputArray[i]);
    }

    return 0;
}
```

### Example Input
```text
00 12 05 08 30
```

### Output
```text
Number: 00
Number: 12
Number: 05
Number: 08
Number: 30
```

### Explanation
- `char inputArray[SIZE][3]` stores strings of up to two characters plus the null terminator (`'\0'`).
- `%2s` reads a maximum of two non-whitespace characters.
- `inputArray[i]` is the correct argument for `%s`; do not use `&inputArray[i]`.
- Store numbers as strings when leading zeros must be preserved. Integer variables cannot retain leading zeros.

**Note:** This code assumes each input element contains at most two characters. For longer inputs, increase the row size and adjust the `scanf` width accordingly.
C PROGRAMMING – STRINGS, CHARACTERS, AND TYPE CONVERSION

Q1. Why does this program display a segmentation fault?

Code:
int main() {
    char test;
    printf("Enter the value");
    scanf("%s", &test);
    printf("%s", test);
}

Answer:
- `char test` stores only one character.
- `%s` expects a character array (string), not a single character.
- Using `%s` with `test` in `printf` passes a character value instead of a valid string address, causing undefined behavior.

Q2. How to read and display only a single character in C?

Answer:
Use `%c` with `scanf()` and `printf()`.

Code:
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    char test;

    printf("Enter a character: ");
    scanf(" %c", &test);

    printf("You entered: %c\n", test);

    return 0;
}

Explanation:
- `%c` reads and displays a single character.
- The space before `%c` skips leading whitespace, including leftover newline characters.

Q3. How to accept a whole string and display it?

Answer:
Use a character array and `%s`.

Code:
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    char test[100];

    printf("Enter a string: ");
    scanf("%99s", test);

    printf("You entered: %s\n", test);

    return 0;
}

Explanation:
- `char test[100]` stores a string of up to 99 characters plus the null terminator `'\0'`.
- `%s` reads a sequence of non-whitespace characters.
- `%99s` prevents writing more than 99 characters into the array.
- To read a string containing spaces, use `fgets()` instead.

Q4. How to convert a string to an integer in C?

Answer:
Use `atoi()` from `<stdlib.h>` for basic conversion.

Code:
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>

int main() {
    char str[100];

    printf("Enter a number as a string: ");
    scanf("%99s", str);

    int number = atoi(str);

    printf("Integer value: %d\n", number);

    return 0;
}

Example:
Input: "123"
Output: 123

Explanation:
- `atoi(str)` converts a numeric string into an integer.
- `atoi()` does not provide reliable error detection for invalid input.
- For robust error handling, use `strtol()` from `<stdlib.h>` with `<errno.h>` and check whether the entire input was valid.
Q1. In C, `%d` is not taking input correctly when entering `006`. How can I solve this?

Answer:
`%d` reads input as a decimal integer. When you enter `006` using `scanf("%d", &num)`, it reads the value as decimal 6. Leading zeros are not preserved because integers store numeric values, not their original formatting.

If you need to preserve leading zeros, use a character array with `%s`.

Example:
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    char num[20];

    printf("Enter a number: ");
    scanf("%19s", num);

    printf("Number: %s\n", num);

    return 0;
}

Input: 006
Output: 006


Q2. How do I declare a variable when using `%o` for octal or `%x` for hexadecimal?

Answer:
Declare an integer variable using `int`. Use `%o` to print its octal representation and `%x` to print its hexadecimal representation.

Example:
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int octalNumber = 012;
    int hexadecimalNumber = 0x1A;

    printf("Octal: %o\n", octalNumber);
    printf("Hexadecimal: %x\n", hexadecimalNumber);

    return 0;
}

Output:
Octal: 12
Hexadecimal: 1a

Explanation:
- `int octalNumber = 012;` declares an integer initialized with an octal literal. Its decimal value is 10.
- `int hexadecimalNumber = 0x1A;` declares an integer initialized with a hexadecimal literal. Its decimal value is 26.
- `%o` prints an integer in octal (base 8).
- `%x` prints an integer in hexadecimal (base 16), using lowercase letters.
- `%d` prints an integer in decimal (base 10).

Important:
If you want to preserve input such as `006`, `020`, or `003`, use a character array (`char`) instead of an integer (`int`), because integers do not retain leading zeros.
Q1. How to convert a string to an integer in C without changing leading zeros, such as "002"?

Answer:
In C, an integer cannot preserve leading zeros. Converting "002" to an integer produces the value 2.

Example:
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>

int main() {
    char str[] = "002";
    int result = atoi(str);

    printf("%d\n", result);  // Output: 2

    return 0;
}

If you need to preserve "002" exactly, keep the value as a string or use formatted output to display the integer with leading zeros.


Q2. How can I print an integer with leading zeros, such as 002?

Answer:
Use the printf format specifier %03d, where 3 represents the minimum output width.

Example:
#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int result = 2;

    printf("%03d\n", result);  // Output: 002

    return 0;
}


Q3. How can the width in %03d be dynamic based on user input?

Answer:
Use %0*d, where the width is supplied as an additional argument to printf.

Example:
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
#include <string.h>

int main() {
    char str[100];

    printf("Enter a number: ");
    scanf("%99s", str);

    int result = atoi(str);
    int width = strlen(str);

    printf("Converted integer with leading zeros: %0*d\n",
           width, result);

    return 0;
}

Input:
002

Output:
Converted integer with leading zeros: 002

Explanation:
- atoi(str) converts the string to an integer.
- strlen(str) calculates the original string's length.
- %0*d dynamically sets the output width using the width variable.
- The 0 flag pads the output with leading zeros when necessary.

Important:
This example assumes a non-negative, valid integer that fits in int. If the original input contains leading zeros, using its string length preserves the displayed width. However, the integer itself does not store those zeros. If you need to preserve the exact original digits, keep the input as a string.
Q1. Write a C program to reverse a number.

Answer:

#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int number, reversedNumber = 0, remainder;

    printf("Enter an integer: ");
    scanf("%d", &number);

    while (number != 0) {
        remainder = number % 10;
        reversedNumber = reversedNumber * 10 + remainder;
        number /= 10;
    }

    printf("Reversed number: %d\n", reversedNumber);

    return 0;
}

Explanation:
- number % 10 extracts the last digit.
- reversedNumber * 10 + remainder adds the extracted digit to the reversed number.
- number /= 10 removes the last digit.
- The loop continues until number becomes 0.

Note: Reading input into an int does not preserve leading zeros. For example, input 0320 is interpreted as decimal 320, so the original digit count is lost.


Q2. How can I reverse 0320 to get 0230 and 2450 to get 0542?

Answer:

If leading zeros must be preserved, read the input as a string instead of an integer.

#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>

int main() {
    char number[100];

    printf("Enter a number: ");
    scanf("%99s", number);

    int len = strlen(number);

    printf("Reversed number: ");

    for (int i = len - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
        printf("%c", number[i]);
    }

    printf("\n");

    return 0;
}

Example output:

Input: 0320
Output: 0230

Input: 2450
Output: 0542

Explanation:
- char number[100] stores the input as a string.
- strlen(number) returns the number of characters.
- The for loop starts at the last character and prints each character backward.
- Since the input is stored as a string, zeros are preserved.

Important: If the input is 032, its reverse is 230. If the input is 0320, its reverse is 0230.


Q3. Explain the following C program that counts the number of characters scanned while reading an integer.

Code:

#include <stdio.h>

int main() {
    int begin;
    int after = 0;
    int number;

    printf("Enter number\n");
    fflush(stdout);

    // Consume leading whitespace.
    // Record the number of characters scanned.
    scanf(" %n%d%n", &begin, &number, &after);

    if (after > 0) {
        int n = after - begin;

        printf("No of digits: %d\n", n);
        printf("Value read  : %d\n", number);
    } else {
        puts("Invalid input");
    }

    return 0;
}

Explanation, line by line:

1. #include <stdio.h>
   Includes the standard input/output library for functions such as printf(), scanf(), fflush(), and puts().

2. int main()
   Defines the main function, where program execution begins.

3. int begin;
   Stores the number of characters consumed before the integer is read.

4. int after = 0;
   Stores the number of characters consumed after reading the integer. Initializing it to 0 helps detect when the second %n has not executed.

5. int number;
   Stores the integer read from the input.

6. printf("Enter number\n");
   Displays the input prompt.

7. fflush(stdout);
   Flushes the output buffer so the prompt is displayed immediately.

8. scanf(" %n%d%n", &begin, &number, &after);

   Format string breakdown:

   - The leading space consumes any leading whitespace, such as spaces, tabs, and newlines.
   - The first %n stores the number of characters consumed so far in begin. It does not read input or count as a conversion.
   - %d reads an integer and stores it in number.
   - The second %n stores the total number of characters consumed so far in after. It also does not read input.

   The %n conversion requires an int pointer, so &begin and &after are used.

9. if (after > 0)
   Checks whether the second %n has executed and recorded a positive character count. This alone is not a reliable test for successful integer conversion in every possible input scenario.

10. int n = after - begin;
    Calculates the number of characters consumed while reading the integer.

11. printf("No of digits: %d\n", n);
    Prints the calculated character count.

12. printf("Value read  : %d\n", number);
    Prints the integer value read.

13. else
    Executes when the condition after > 0 is false.

14. puts("Invalid input");
    Prints "Invalid input" followed by a newline.

15. return 0;
    Indicates that the program has completed successfully.

16. }
    Marks the end of the main function.

Example:

Input:
12345

Output:
No of digits: 5
Value read  : 12345

Important notes:
- The character count includes the optional sign of a signed integer. For example, -123 has 4 characters but only 3 digits.
