Therefore, `[^\n]` means any character except newline.

Example:

Input:
```text
abc123
```

The call consumes `abc123` but leaves the newline in the input stream.

### Q12. What does `scanf("%*c");` do?

**Answer:**

```c
scanf("%*c");
```

It reads one character and discards it.

If the remaining input is a newline, it consumes that newline.

### Q13. Why are two `scanf()` calls sometimes used?

**Answer:**

```c
scanf("%*[^\n]");
scanf("%*c");
```

The first discards the rest of the current line. The second consumes the newline.

Example:

Input:
```text
abc123\n
```

After the first call:
```text
\n remains in the input stream
```

After the second call:
```text
The newline is consumed
```

A simpler alternative for clearing a line is:

```c
int ch;

while ((ch = getchar()) != '\n' && ch != EOF)
{
    /* Discard remaining characters */
}
```

This also handles end-of-file safely.

### Q14. What happens if `scanf("%*[^\n]")` is used without `*`?

**Answer:**

The `*` suppresses assignment. Without it, a scanset conversion needs a destination character array.

Example:

```c
char buffer[100];

scanf("%99[^\n]", buffer);
```

This stores characters up to the newline in `buffer`, leaving the newline unread. The width limit prevents writing beyond the array.

---

## 4. QUEUE IMPLEMENTATION

### Q15. Why was the rear item printing a strange number such as `3711` or `-27819`?

**Answer:**

The main problem was declaring the return value as `short`:

```c
short sReturnVal = 0;

sReturnVal = in_SQueue->m_arri_Array[in_SQueue->m_iRear];
printf("Rear item is %d\n", sReturnVal);
```

A `short` is commonly 16 bits, while the array elements are `int`, commonly 32 bits. Assigning an `int` to a `short` can truncate the value.

Correct code:

```c
int DP_Rear(DP_SQueue *in_SQueue)
{
    int iRearValue;

    if (DP_IsEmpty(in_SQueue))
        return FAILURE;

    iRearValue =
        in_SQueue->m_arri_Array[in_SQueue->m_iRear];

    printf("Rear item is %d\n", iRearValue);

    return SUCCESS;
}
```

### Q16. What is the difference between a normal queue and a circular queue?

**Answer:**

A normal linear array queue does not automatically reuse indexes freed at the beginning. A circular queue wraps indexes around using the modulo operator.

Circular queue:

```c
rear = (rear + 1) % MAX_SIZE;
front = (front + 1) % MAX_SIZE;
```

For a circular queue that tracks the number of elements, the empty and full conditions can be:

```c
empty: size == 0
full:  size == MAX_SIZE
```

The rear index refers to the most recently inserted element.

### Q17. What was wrong with the original dequeue implementation?

**Answer:**

The original code incorrectly changed `rear` rather than removing the front item and advancing `front`.

Correct circular queue dequeue logic:

```c
int DP_Dequeue(DP_SQueue *in_SQueue)
{
    int iItem;

    if (in_SQueue->m_iSize == 0)
        return FAILURE;

    iItem =
        in_SQueue->m_arri_Array[in_SQueue->m_iFront];

    printf("Dequeued element is %d\n", iItem);

    in_SQueue->m_iFront =
        (in_SQueue->m_iFront + 1) % MAX_SIZE;

    in_SQueue->m_iSize--;

    if (in_SQueue->m_iSize == 0)
    {
        in_SQueue->m_iFront = DP_EMPTY;
        in_SQueue->m_iRear = DP_EMPTY;
    }

    return SUCCESS;
}
```

This assumes `DP_EMPTY` is `-1` and the queue uses `m_iSize` to distinguish empty from non-empty.

### Q18. How does queue display work?

**Answer:**

For a circular queue, the display loop must wrap around the array and print exactly `m_iSize` elements.

```c
int displayQueue(DP_SQueue *in_SQueue)
{
    int i;
    int index;

    if (in_SQueue->m_iSize == 0)
        return FAILURE;

    printf("Queue elements: ");

    index = in_SQueue->m_iFront;

    for (i = 0; i < in_SQueue->m_iSize; i++)
    {
        printf("%d ",
               in_SQueue->m_arri_Array[index]);

        index = (index + 1) % MAX_SIZE;
    }

    printf("\n");

    return SUCCESS;
}
```

Using `m_iSize` avoids relying on a loop that stops at `rear`, which can fail when the queue wraps around.

### Q19. What is the difference between `DP_Front()` and `DP_Rear()`?

**Answer:**

- `DP_Front()` returns or displays the element at the front index.
- `DP_Rear()` returns or displays the element at the rear index.

For a queue containing:

```text
10 20 30
```

Front is `10`, and rear is `30`.

The variables holding these values should have the same suitable type as the array elements.

---

## 5. LINKED LIST FUNDAMENTALS

### Q20. Why is linked-list data stored in a `struct`?

**Answer:**

A linked-list node stores both the data and the address of the next node.

```c
struct Node
{
    int data;
    struct Node *next;
};
```

Example:

```text
+------+--------+    +------+--------+    +------+------+
|  10  |   o----+--->|  20  |   o----+--->|  30  | NULL |
+------+--------+    +------+--------+    +------+------+
```

Each node contains:
- `data`: the value.
- `next`: the pointer to the next node.

### Q21. Explain `struct Node *next;`

**Answer:**

```c
struct Node *next;
```

This declares a pointer that can store the address of another `struct Node`.

It does not create another node by itself.

Example:

```c
struct Node first;
struct Node second;

first.data = 10;
second.data = 20;

first.next = &second;
second.next = NULL;
```

The first node points to the second node.

### Q22. Why is `malloc()` used for each new linked-list node?

**Answer:**

Each new node requires its own memory.

```c
struct Node *newNode =
    malloc(sizeof(struct Node));
```

For three nodes, three node-sized memory blocks are required.

Calling `malloc()` once only allocates memory for one node. A linked list with a variable number of nodes needs additional allocations as nodes are added.

Always check for allocation failure:

```c
if (newNode == NULL)
{
    printf("Memory allocation failed.\n");
    return FAILURE;
}
```

Free a node when it is deleted to avoid memory leaks.

### Q23. What is the difference between `struct Node *ptr` and `struct Node** ptr`?

**Answer:**

```c
struct Node *ptr;
```

A pointer to a node. It can access or modify that node.

```c
struct Node **ptr;
```

A pointer to a node pointer. It can access or modify the pointer that identifies the node, such as the list's head pointer.

The placement of spaces does not change the type:

```c
struct Node *ptr;
struct Node* ptr;
struct Node    * ptr;
```

All three declarations have the same meaning.

### Q24. Why does `insertAtBeginning()` use a double pointer?

**Answer:**

```c
void insertAtBeginning(struct Node **head,
                       int newData)
```

The function needs to update the caller's head pointer.

Example:

```c
struct Node *head = NULL;

insertAtBeginning(&head, 10);
```

Inside the function:

```c
newNode->next = *head;
*head = newNode;
```

Here:
- `head` points to the caller's head pointer.
- `*head` is the actual head pointer.
- `*head = newNode` updates the caller's head pointer.

If the function received only `struct Node *head`, changing the local pointer would not update the caller's pointer.

### Q25. Should the NULL check use `pSHead` or `*pSHead`?

**Answer:**

Both checks are meaningful, but they test different things.

```c
if (pSHead == NULL)
```

Checks whether the pointer-to-pointer itself is NULL. If it is NULL, the function cannot access the caller's head pointer.

```c
if (*pSHead == NULL)
```

Checks whether the linked list is empty.

Example:

```c
int DP_InsertBeginning(struct Node **pSHead)
{
    struct Node *newNode;

    if (pSHead == NULL)
    {
        printf("Head pointer argument is NULL.\n");
        return FAILURE;
    }

    newNode = malloc(sizeof(struct Node));

    if (newNode == NULL)
    {
        printf("Memory allocation failed.\n");
        return FAILURE;
    }

    newNode->data = 10;
    newNode->next = *pSHead;
    *pSHead = newNode;

    return SUCCESS;
}
```

This function accepts an empty list because `*pSHead == NULL` is a valid initial state.

### Q26. Why are these three lines used when inserting at the beginning?

```c
newNode->data = newData;
newNode->next = *pSHead;
*pSHead = newNode;
```

**Answer:**

They perform three different operations:

1. Store the data in the new node.
2. Make the new node point to the old first node.
3. Make the new node the first node.

Example:

Before:

```text
head -> 20 -> 30 -> NULL
```

Insert 10:

```text
newNode -> 10
```

After linking:

```text
head -> 10 -> 20 -> 30 -> NULL
```

### Q27. How do you insert a node at the end?

**Answer:**

```c
int insertAtEnd(struct Node **head, int data)
{
    struct Node *newNode;
    struct Node *last;

    if (head == NULL)
        return FAILURE;

    newNode = malloc(sizeof(struct Node));

    if (newNode == NULL)
        return FAILURE;

    newNode->data = data;
    newNode->next = NULL;

    if (*head == NULL)
    {
        *head = newNode;
        return SUCCESS;
    }

    last = *head;

    while (last->next != NULL)
        last = last->next;

    last->next = newNode;

    return SUCCESS;
}
```

If the list is empty, the new node becomes the head. Otherwise, the function traverses to the last node and links the new node there.

### Q28. What should `return` do in an `int` function?

**Answer:**

An `int` function should return an integer value.

```c
return SUCCESS;
```

or:

```c
return FAILURE;
```

If `SUCCESS` is defined as 1 and `FAILURE` as 0, the caller can test the result:

```c
if (insertAtEnd(&head, 20) == SUCCESS)
{
    printf("Insertion successful.\n");
}
```

Do not use a bare `return;` in a function declared to return `int`.

---

## 6. LINKED-LIST INSERTION, DELETION AND DISPLAY

### Q29. How does insertion at a specified position work?

**Answer:**

Suppose the list is:

```text
10 -> 20 -> 30 -> NULL
```

Insert `15` at position 2, using one-based positions:

```text
10 -> 15 -> 20 -> 30 -> NULL
```

The new node must point to the node currently at position 2, and the previous node must point to the new node.

```c
int insertAtPosition(struct Node **head,
                     int position,
                     int data)
{
    struct Node *newNode;
    struct Node *current;
    int i;

    if (head == NULL || position < 1)
        return FAILURE;

    if (position == 1)
    {
        newNode = malloc(sizeof(struct Node));

        if (newNode == NULL)
            return FAILURE;

        newNode->data = data;
        newNode->next = *head;
        *head = newNode;

        return SUCCESS;
    }

    current = *head;

    for (i = 1; i < position - 1 && current != NULL; i++)
        current = current->next;

    if (current == NULL)
        return FAILURE;

    newNode = malloc(sizeof(struct Node));

    if (newNode == NULL)
        return FAILURE;

    newNode->data = data;
    newNode->next = current->next;
    current->next = newNode;

    return SUCCESS;
}
```

### Q30. How do you delete the first node?

**Answer:**

```c
int deleteBeginning(struct Node **head)
{
    struct Node *temp;

    if (head == NULL || *head == NULL)
        return FAILURE;

    temp = *head;
    *head = temp->next;

    free(temp);

    return SUCCESS;
}
```

Example:

```text
Before: 10 -> 20 -> 30 -> NULL
After:  20 -> 30 -> NULL
```

### Q31. How do you delete the last node?

**Answer:**

```c
int deleteEnd(struct Node **head)
{
    struct Node *current;
    struct Node *previous = NULL;

    if (head == NULL || *head == NULL)
        return FAILURE;

    current = *head;

    if (current->next == NULL)
    {
        free(current);
        *head = NULL;
        return SUCCESS;
    }

    while (current->next != NULL)
    {
        previous = current;
        current = current->next;
    }

    previous->next = NULL;
    free(current);

    return SUCCESS;
}
```

Example:

```text
Before: 10 -> 20 -> 30 -> NULL
After:  10 -> 20 -> NULL
```

For a one-node list, deleting the last node also makes the head NULL.

### Q32. How do you delete a node at a specified position?

**Answer:**

```c
int deleteAtPosition(struct Node **head, int position)
{
    struct Node *current;
    struct Node *temp;
    int i;

    if (head == NULL || *head == NULL || position < 1)
        return FAILURE;

    if (position == 1)
        return deleteBeginning(head);

    current = *head;

    for (i = 1; i < position - 1 && current != NULL; i++)
        current = current->next;

    if (current == NULL || current->next == NULL)
        return FAILURE;

    temp = current->next;
    current->next = temp->next;

    free(temp);

    return SUCCESS;
}
```

Example:

```text
Before: 10 -> 20 -> 30 -> 40 -> NULL
Delete position 3
After:  10 -> 20 -> 40 -> NULL
```

The position is counted starting from 1.

### Q33. How do you display a linked list?

**Answer:**

```c
int display(struct Node *head)
{
    struct Node *current = head;

    if (current == NULL)
    {
        printf("List is empty.\n");
        return FAILURE;
    }

    printf("Linked list: ");

    while (current != NULL)
    {
        printf("%d ", current->data);
        current = current->next;
    }

    printf("\n");

    return SUCCESS;
}
```

Example:

```text
Linked list: 10 20 30
```

The function traverses each node until its pointer becomes NULL. It does not delete or modify the nodes.

### Q34. How do you search for a value?

**Answer:**

```c
int search(struct Node *head, int key)
{
    int position = 1;

    while (head != NULL)
    {
        if (head->data == key)
        {
            printf("Item found at position %d\n", position);
            return SUCCESS;
        }

        head = head->next;
        position++;
    }

    printf("Item not found.\n");
    return FAILURE;
}
```

Example:

```text
List: 10 -> 20 -> 30
Search: 20
Output: Item found at position 2
```

### Q35. How can you avoid memory leaks in a linked list?

**Answer:**

Use `free()` whenever a node is deleted. Also free every remaining node when the program no longer needs the list.

```c
void freeList(struct Node **head)
{
    struct Node *temp;

    if (head == NULL)
        return;

    while (*head != NULL)
    {
        temp = *head;
        *head = (*head)->next;
        free(temp);
    }
}
```

Call it before exiting:

```c
freeList(&head);
```

---

## 7. COMPLETE MENU-DRIVEN LINKED-LIST PROGRAM

### Q36. Give a complete C program for linked-list insertion, deletion, search and display.

**Answer:**

The following program uses a global head pointer and does not pass the head pointer to every operation. All operations return integer status codes. Positions are one-based.

```c
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>

#define SUCCESS 1
#define FAILURE 0

struct Node
{
    int data;
    struct Node *next;
};

struct Node *head = NULL;

int insertBeginning(void)
{
    int data;
    struct Node *newNode;

    printf("Enter data: ");

    if (scanf("%d", &data) != 1)
        return FAILURE;

    newNode = malloc(sizeof(struct Node));

    if (newNode == NULL)
        return FAILURE;

    newNode->data = data;
    newNode->next = head;
    head = newNode;

    return SUCCESS;
}

int insertEnd(void)
{
    int data;
    struct Node *newNode;
    struct Node *current;

    printf("Enter data: ");

    if (scanf("%d", &data) != 1)
        return FAILURE;

    newNode = malloc(sizeof(struct Node));

    if (newNode == NULL)
        return FAILURE;

    newNode->data = data;
    newNode->next = NULL;

    if (head == NULL)
    {
        head = newNode;
        return SUCCESS;
    }

    current = head;

    while (current->next != NULL)
        current = current->next;

    current->next = newNode;

    return SUCCESS;
}

int insertPosition(void)
{
    int position, data, i;
    struct Node *newNode;
    struct Node *current;

    printf("Enter position: ");

    if (scanf("%d", &position) != 1 || position < 1)
        return FAILURE;

    printf("Enter data: ");

    if (scanf("%d", &data) != 1)
        return FAILURE;

    if (position == 1)
        return insertAtBeginningValue(data);

    current = head;

    for (i = 1; i < position - 1 && current != NULL; i++)
        current = current->next;

    if (current == NULL)
        return FAILURE;

    newNode = malloc(sizeof(struct Node));

    if (newNode == NULL)
        return FAILURE;

    newNode->data = data;
    newNode->next = current->next;
    current->next = newNode;

    return SUCCESS;
}

int insertAtBeginningValue(int data)
{
    struct Node *newNode = malloc(sizeof(struct Node));

    if (newNode == NULL)
        return FAILURE;

    newNode->data = data;
    newNode->next = head;
    head = newNode;

    return SUCCESS;
}

int deleteBeginning(void)
{
    struct Node *temp;

    if (head == NULL)
        return FAILURE;

    temp = head;
    head = head->next;
    free(temp);

    return SUCCESS;
}

int deleteEnd(void)
{
    struct Node *current;
    struct Node *previous = NULL;

    if (head == NULL)
        return FAILURE;

    current = head;

    if (current->next == NULL)
    {
        free(current);
        head = NULL;
        return SUCCESS;
    }

    while (current->next != NULL)
    {
        previous = current;
        current = current->next;
    }

    previous->next = NULL;
    free(current);

    return SUCCESS;
}

int deletePosition(void)
{
    int position, i;
    struct Node *current;
    struct Node *temp;

    printf("Enter position to delete: ");

    if (scanf("%d", &position) != 1 || position < 1)
        return FAILURE;

    if (head == NULL)
        return FAILURE;

    if (position == 1)
        return deleteBeginning();

    current = head;

    for (i = 1; i < position - 1 && current != NULL; i++)
        current = current->next;

    if (current == NULL || current->next == NULL)
        return FAILURE;

    temp = current->next;
    current->next = temp->next;
    free(temp);

    return SUCCESS;
}

int search(void)
{
    int key, position = 1;
    struct Node *current = head;

    printf("Enter item to search: ");

    if (scanf("%d", &key) != 1)
        return FAILURE;

    while (current != NULL)
    {
        if (current->data == key)
        {
            printf("Item found at position %d\n", position);
            return SUCCESS;
        }

        current = current->next;
        position++;
    }

    printf("Item not found.\n");
    return FAILURE;
}

int display(void)
{
    struct Node *current = head;

    if (current == NULL)
    {
        printf("List is empty.\n");
        return FAILURE;
    }

    printf("Linked list: ");

    while (current != NULL)
    {
        printf("%d ", current->data);
        current = current->next;
    }

    printf("\n");
    return SUCCESS;
}

void freeList(void)
{
    struct Node *temp;

    while (head != NULL)
    {
        temp = head;
        head = head->next;
        free(temp);
    }
}

int main(void)
{
    int choice;
    int result;

    do
    {
        printf("\n1. Insert at beginning\n");
        printf("2. Insert at end\n");
        printf("3. Insert at position\n");
        printf("4. Delete beginning\n");
        printf("5. Delete end\n");
        printf("6. Delete at position\n");
        printf("7. Search\n");
        printf("8. Display\n");
        printf("9. Exit\n");
        printf("Enter choice: ");

        if (scanf("%d", &choice) != 1)
        {
            int ch;

            while ((ch = getchar()) != '\n' && ch != EOF)
                ;

            printf("Invalid input.\n");
            continue;
        }

        switch (choice)
        {
            case 1:
                result = insertBeginning();
                break;

            case 2:
                result = insertEnd();
                break;

            case 3:
                result = insertPosition();
                break;

            case 4:
                result = deleteBeginning();
                break;

            case 5:
                result = deleteEnd();
                break;

            case 6:
                result = deletePosition();
                break;

            case 7:
                search();
                continue;

            case 8:
                display();
                continue;

            case 9:
                break;

            default:
                printf("Invalid choice.\n");
                continue;
        }

        if (choice >= 1 && choice <= 6)
        {
            if (result == SUCCESS)
                printf("Operation successful.\n");
            else
                printf("Operation failed.\n");
        }

    } while (choice != 9);

    freeList();

    return 0;
}
```

**Note:** In this complete program, `insertAtBeginningValue()` is defined after `insertPosition()`. For C compilers that require a prior declaration, add this prototype before `insertPosition()`:

```c
int insertAtBeginningValue(int data);
```

The program uses a global `head` pointer, so the operations can access it directly. In larger programs, passing the head pointer or using a dedicated list structure is generally easier to maintain and test.

---

## 8. IMPORTANT PROGRAMMING RULES

### Q37. What does `->` mean?

**Answer:**

The arrow operator accesses a structure member through a pointer.

```c
newNode->data = 10;
```

It is equivalent to:

```c
(*newNode).data = 10;
```

### Q38. What is the difference between `.` and `->`?

**Answer:**

Use `.` when you have a structure object:

```c
struct Node node;
node.data = 10;
```

Use `->` when you have a pointer to a structure:

```c
struct Node *ptr = &node;
ptr->data = 20;
```

### Q39. What is the difference between `NULL` and `-1`?

**Answer:**

- `NULL` represents a null pointer.
- `-1` is an integer value often used as a sentinel for an invalid index.

Example:

```c
struct Node *head = NULL;
int front = -1;
```

Do not confuse an empty linked-list pointer with an empty queue index.

### Q40. Why must allocated memory be freed?

**Answer:**

Memory allocated using `malloc()` remains allocated until it is released with `free()` or the process terminates.

```c
struct Node *ptr = malloc(sizeof(struct Node));

if (ptr != NULL)
{
    ptr->data = 10;
    ptr->next = NULL;

    free(ptr);
    ptr = NULL;
}
```

Do not access the allocated object after freeing it. Assigning `NULL` to the local pointer helps prevent accidental reuse of that pointer.

### Q41. Why should `malloc()` use `sizeof(struct Node)` rather than `sizeof(struct Node *)`?

**Answer:**

Correct:

```c
struct Node *ptr = malloc(sizeof(struct Node));
```

Incorrect for allocating a complete node:

```c
struct Node *ptr = malloc(sizeof(struct Node *));
```

`sizeof(struct Node)` allocates enough memory for the data and the next pointer. `sizeof(struct Node *)` allocates only enough memory for a pointer, which is usually too small for the entire node.

### Q42. Why is `void main()` not recommended?

**Answer:**

Use:

```c
int main(void)
{
    return 0;
}
```

`int main(void)` is the standard form for a C program that takes no command-line arguments and returns an integer status to the environment.

### Q43. What is the difference between stack and linked-list memory allocation?

**Answer:**

An array-based stack typically uses a fixed-size array:

```c
int stack[MAX_SIZE];
```

A linked list typically allocates each node dynamically:

```c
struct Node *newNode = malloc(sizeof(struct Node));
```

The array has a fixed capacity. A linked list can grow as memory becomes available, although allocation can fail and every allocated node must eventually be freed.

---

END OF COMBINED C/C++ PROGRAMMING NOTES
Q1. Explain the given C program for a TCP server using Winsock in Windows.

Answer:

This C program implements a TCP server using the Winsock API on Windows. It listens on IP address 127.0.0.1 and port 8085, accepts a client connection, receives messages, and writes them to received_messages.txt.

1. Header Files
   - stdafx.h: Precompiled header used in some Visual Studio projects.
   - stdio.h: Provides file operations and printf().
   - stdlib.h: Provides standard utility functions.
   - winsock2.h: Provides Windows socket programming functions.

2. Macro Definitions
   - PORT 8085: Server port number.
   - BUFF_SIZE 1024: Receive buffer size.
   - IP_ADDRESS "127.0.0.1": Localhost IP address.
   - DP_ZERO 0 and DP_ONE 1: Constants for zero and one.
   - SUCCESS 0 and FAILURE 1: Return values indicating success or failure.

3. Open the File
   FILE *file = fopen("received_messages.txt", "w");

   Opens a file for writing received messages. If the file does not exist, it is created. If it exists, its previous contents are erased.

4. Initialize Winsock
   WSAStartup(MAKEWORD(2, 2), &wsaData);

   Initializes the Winsock library and requests Winsock version 2.2. It must succeed before using socket functions.

5. Create the Server Socket
   SServerSocket = socket(AF_INET, SOCK_STREAM, 0);

   - AF_INET: Uses IPv4.
   - SOCK_STREAM: Uses TCP.
   - 0: Selects the default protocol for the socket type.

   The server socket is used to listen for incoming client connections.

6. Set Socket Options
   setsockopt(SServerSocket, SOL_SOCKET, SO_REUSEADDR,
              (const char*)&iReuse, sizeof(iReuse));

   Attempts to enable address reuse. The option can help when restarting a server, although its exact behavior depends on the operating system.

7. Configure the Server Address
   SServerAddr.sin_family = AF_INET;
   SServerAddr.sin_port = htons(PORT);
   SServerAddr.sin_addr.s_addr = inet_addr(IP_ADDRESS);

   - sin_family: Specifies IPv4.
   - htons(): Converts the port number to network byte order.
   - inet_addr(): Converts the IPv4 address string into its binary representation.

8. Bind the Socket
   bind(SServerSocket, (struct sockaddr*)&SServerAddr,
        sizeof(SServerAddr));

   Associates the server socket with IP address 127.0.0.1 and port 8085.

9. Listen for Connections
   listen(SServerSocket, 5);

   Puts the server socket into listening mode. The value 5 specifies the requested backlog for pending connections.

10. Accept a Client Connection
    SClientSocket = accept(SServerSocket,
                           (struct sockaddr*)&SClientAddr,
                           &iClientAddrLen);

    Waits for a client to connect. When a connection is accepted, a new socket, SClientSocket, is used to communicate with that client.

    SServerSocket continues listening for new connections.

11. Receive Client Messages
    iReadVal = recv(SClientSocket, arrc_Buffer,
                    sizeof(arrc_Buffer), 0);

    Receives data from the connected client and stores it in arrc_Buffer.

    Return values:
    - Greater than zero: Number of bytes received.
    - Zero: The client has gracefully closed its sending connection.
    - SOCKET_ERROR: An error occurred.

12. Write Messages to the File
    fprintf(file, "Client: %s\n", arrc_Buffer);

    Writes the received message to the file with the prefix "Client:".

13. Handle the "exit" Command
    if (strcmp(arrc_Buffer, "exit") == 0)

    Checks whether the received message is exactly "exit". If it matches, the program closes the client connection and exits the main loop.

14. Cleanup
    closesocket(SClientSocket);
    closesocket(SServerSocket);
    WSACleanup();

    - closesocket(): Closes a socket.
    - WSACleanup(): Releases Winsock resources.
    - fclose(): Closes the file.

15. Return Value
    return SUCCESS;

    Returns 0 to indicate successful program termination.

Important Issues in the Original Code:

1. Buffer overflow:
   char arrc_Buffer[BUFF_SIZE] = {0};
   iReadVal = recv(SClientSocket, arrc_Buffer, sizeof(arrc_Buffer), 0);
   arrc_Buffer[iReadVal] = '\0';

   If recv() receives 1024 bytes, arrc_Buffer[1024] is outside the array bounds. Also, if recv() returns SOCKET_ERROR (-1), the code attempts an invalid array access.

   Safer approach:
   int iReadVal = recv(SClientSocket, arrc_Buffer,
                       sizeof(arrc_Buffer) - 1, 0);

   if (iReadVal > 0)
   {
       arrc_Buffer[iReadVal] = '\0';
   }

2. Incorrect error handling:
   Winsock uses SOCKET_ERROR (-1) to indicate a recv() error and returns zero when the connection is gracefully closed. Both cases should be handled separately.

3. File handling:
   The original code may close the file more than once and may attempt to write to it after an error. The file should be closed once during cleanup.

4. TCP message boundaries:
   TCP is a byte stream. One recv() call does not necessarily correspond to one complete message. A production implementation needs message framing, such as a newline delimiter or a length prefix.

5. Comparing received data with "exit":
   strcmp(arrc_Buffer, "exit") works only when the received data contains exactly that string and no additional bytes. TCP does not guarantee that an entire application message arrives in a single recv() call.

6. Missing header:
   Include <string.h> for strcmp(). Depending on the project configuration, include <ws2tcpip.h> for modern address-conversion functions.

Overall:
The program creates a TCP server, listens on localhost port 8085, accepts a client, receives its data, writes messages to a file, and closes the connection when it receives "exit". The original code needs improved receive handling, buffer-boundary checks, and centralized resource cleanup to be reliable.
# TCP CLIENT-SERVER CHAT USING FILE HANDLING IN C (WINSOCK)

## Q1. How can I write client messages received by a TCP server into a file?

**Answer:**

Open a file using `fopen()` and write the received message using `fprintf()`. Use `fflush()` to flush buffered data to the file.

```c
FILE *file = fopen("received_messages.txt", "a");

if (file == NULL)
{
    printf("Error opening file\n");
    return FAILURE;
}

/* After receiving a client message */
fprintf(file, "Client: %s\n", arrc_Buffer);
fflush(file);

/* Close the file during cleanup */
fclose(file);
```

Use `"a"` to append messages without deleting previous content. Use `"w"` to overwrite the file whenever it is opened.

---

## Q2. How can I save both client and server messages in a file?

**Answer:**

Log both received client messages and server messages before or after sending them.

```c
/* Log the message received from the client */
fprintf(file, "Client: %s\n", arrc_Buffer);
fflush(file);

/* Read the server's message */
printf("Server: ");
scanf(" %[^\n]", arrc_Message);

/* Log the server's message */
fprintf(file, "Server: %s\n", arrc_Message);
fflush(file);

/* Send the server's message to the client */
iReadVal = send(
    SClientSocket,
    arrc_Message,
    (int)strlen(arrc_Message),
    0
);
```

Both messages are written to the same file, in communication order.

---

## Q3. Give the complete TCP server code for chatting with a client and saving both messages into a file.

**Answer:**

The following server uses Winsock TCP, accepts one client at a time, exchanges messages alternately, and logs both sides of the conversation into `chat_log.txt`.

```c
#include "stdafx.h"
#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>

int main()
{
    WSADATA wsaData;
    SOCKET SServerSocket = INVALID_SOCKET;
    SOCKET SClientSocket = INVALID_SOCKET;

    struct sockaddr_in SServerAddr;
    struct sockaddr_in SClientAddr;

    int iClientAddrLen = sizeof(SClientAddr);
    int iReadVal;
    int iReuse = 1;

    char arrc_Buffer[BUFF_SIZE];
    char arrc_Message[BUFF_SIZE];

    FILE *file = fopen("chat_log.txt", "a");

    if (file == NULL)
    {
        printf("Error opening file\n");
        return FAILURE;
    }

    if (WSAStartup(MAKEWORD(2, 2), &wsaData) != 0)
    {
        printf("WSAStartup failed\n");
        fclose(file);
        return FAILURE;
    }

    SServerSocket = socket(AF_INET, SOCK_STREAM, 0);

    if (SServerSocket == INVALID_SOCKET)
    {
        printf("Socket creation failed\n");
        fclose(file);
        WSACleanup();
        return FAILURE;
    }

    if (setsockopt(
            SServerSocket,
            SOL_SOCKET,
            SO_REUSEADDR,
            (const char *)&iReuse,
            sizeof(iReuse)) == SOCKET_ERROR)
    {
        printf("setsockopt failed\n");
        closesocket(SServerSocket);
        fclose(file);
        WSACleanup();
        return FAILURE;
    }

    memset(&SServerAddr, 0, sizeof(SServerAddr));

    SServerAddr.sin_family = AF_INET;
    SServerAddr.sin_port = htons(PORT);
    SServerAddr.sin_addr.s_addr = inet_addr(IP_ADDRESS);

    if (bind(
            SServerSocket,
            (struct sockaddr *)&SServerAddr,
            sizeof(SServerAddr)) == SOCKET_ERROR)
    {
        printf("Bind failed\n");
        closesocket(SServerSocket);
        fclose(file);
        WSACleanup();
        return FAILURE;
    }

    if (listen(SServerSocket, 5) == SOCKET_ERROR)
    {
        printf("Listen failed\n");
        closesocket(SServerSocket);
        fclose(file);
        WSACleanup();
        return FAILURE;
    }

    printf("Server listening on port %d\n", PORT);

    SClientSocket = accept(
        SServerSocket,
        (struct sockaddr *)&SClientAddr,
        &iClientAddrLen
    );

    if (SClientSocket == INVALID_SOCKET)
    {
        printf("Accept failed\n");
        closesocket(SServerSocket);
        fclose(file);
        WSACleanup();
        return FAILURE;
    }

    printf("Client connected: %s\n", inet_ntoa(SClientAddr.sin_addr));

    while (1)
    {
        /* Receive client message */
        iReadVal = recv(
            SClientSocket,
            arrc_Buffer,
            sizeof(arrc_Buffer) - 1,
            0
        );

        if (iReadVal <= 0)
        {
            printf("Client disconnected or receive failed\n");
            break;
        }

        arrc_Buffer[iReadVal] = '\0';

        printf("Client: %s\n", arrc_Buffer);

        fprintf(file, "Client: %s\n", arrc_Buffer);
        fflush(file);

        if (strcmp(arrc_Buffer, "exit") == 0)
        {
            printf("Client ended the chat\n");
            break;
        }

        /* Read server message */
        printf("Server: ");

        if (fgets(arrc_Message, sizeof(arrc_Message), stdin) == NULL)
        {
            break;
        }

        arrc_Message[strcspn(arrc_Message, "\r\n")] = '\0';

        fprintf(file, "Server: %s\n", arrc_Message);
        fflush(file);

        /* Send server message */
        iReadVal = send(
            SClientSocket,
            arrc_Message,
            (int)strlen(arrc_Message),
            0
        );

        if (iReadVal == SOCKET_ERROR)
        {
            printf("Send failed\n");
            break;
        }

        if (strcmp(arrc_Message, "exit") == 0)
        {
            printf("Server ended the chat\n");
            break;
        }
    }

    closesocket(SClientSocket);
    closesocket(SServerSocket);
    fclose(file);
    WSACleanup();

    return SUCCESS;
}
```

---

## Q4. Give the complete TCP client code for chatting with the server and saving both messages into a file.

**Answer:**

The client connects to the server, sends messages, receives replies, and logs both sides into `chat_log.txt`.

```c
#include "stdafx.h"
#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>

int main()
{
    WSADATA wsaData;
    SOCKET SClientSocket = INVALID_SOCKET;

    struct sockaddr_in SServerAddr;

    int iReadVal;

    char arrc_Message[BUFF_SIZE];
    char arrc_Buffer[BUFF_SIZE];

    FILE *file = fopen("chat_log.txt", "a");

    if (file == NULL)
    {
        printf("Error opening file\n");
        return FAILURE;
    }

    if (WSAStartup(MAKEWORD(2, 2), &wsaData) != 0)
    {
        printf("WSAStartup failed\n");
        fclose(file);
        return FAILURE;
    }

    SClientSocket = socket(AF_INET, SOCK_STREAM, 0);

    if (SClientSocket == INVALID_SOCKET)
    {
        printf("Socket creation failed\n");
        fclose(file);
        WSACleanup();
        return FAILURE;
    }

    memset(&SServerAddr, 0, sizeof(SServerAddr));

    SServerAddr.sin_family = AF_INET;
    SServerAddr.sin_port = htons(PORT);
    SServerAddr.sin_addr.s_addr = inet_addr(IP_ADDRESS);

    if (connect(
            SClientSocket,
            (struct sockaddr *)&SServerAddr,
            sizeof(SServerAddr)) == SOCKET_ERROR)
    {
        printf("Connection failed\n");
        closesocket(SClientSocket);
        fclose(file);
        WSACleanup();
        return FAILURE;
    }

    printf("Connected to server\n");

    while (1)
    {
        /* Read client message */
        printf("Client: ");

        if (fgets(arrc_Message, sizeof(arrc_Message), stdin) == NULL)
        {
            break;
        }

        arrc_Message[strcspn(arrc_Message, "\r\n")] = '\0';

        /* Log client message */
        fprintf(file, "Client: %s\n", arrc_Message);
        fflush(file);

        iReadVal = send(
            SClientSocket,
            arrc_Message,
            (int)strlen(arrc_Message),
            0
        );

        if (iReadVal == SOCKET_ERROR)
        {
            printf("Send failed\n");
            break;
        }

        if (strcmp(arrc_Message, "exit") == 0)
        {
            printf("Closing connection...\n");
            break;
        }

        /* Receive server message */
        iReadVal = recv(
            SClientSocket,
            arrc_Buffer,
            sizeof(arrc_Buffer) - 1,
            0
        );

        if (iReadVal <= 0)
        {
            printf("Server disconnected or receive failed\n");
            break;
        }

        arrc_Buffer[iReadVal] = '\0';

        printf("Server: %s\n", arrc_Buffer);

        /* Log server message */
        fprintf(file, "Server: %s\n", arrc_Buffer);
        fflush(file);

        if (strcmp(arrc_Buffer, "exit") == 0)
        {
            printf("Server ended the chat\n");
            break;
        }
    }

    closesocket(SClientSocket);
    fclose(file);
    WSACleanup();

    return SUCCESS;
}
```

## Q5. How do I run the client and server?

**Answer:**

1. Define `PORT`, `IP_ADDRESS`, `BUFF_SIZE`, `DP_ZERO`, `DP_ONE`, `SUCCESS`, and `FAILURE` in your project as needed. Both programs must use matching port and IP settings.
2. On the server, use an appropriate local IP address, such as `127.0.0.1` for testing on the same computer.
3. Start the server first.
4. Start the client in another console.
5. Type a message in the client. The server receives it, logs it, and prompts for a reply.
6. Type `exit` to end the chat.

Both programs open `chat_log.txt` in append mode. For testing on the same computer, they can write to the same file, but simultaneous access from two processes is not fully synchronized. A more reliable design uses a single logger process or a shared logging mechanism.

## Q6. What are the important file-handling functions?

**Answer:**

- `fopen("chat_log.txt", "a")` — opens or creates a file and appends messages.
- `fprintf(file, "Client: %s\n", message)` — writes formatted text into the file.
- `fflush(file)` — flushes buffered output to the underlying file.
- `fclose(file)` — closes the file and flushes pending output.

**Important TCP note:** TCP is a byte stream, not a message-based protocol. A single `send()` does not guarantee that the receiver's `recv()` returns the entire message in one call. The example above is suitable as a basic learning exercise; robust applications should implement message framing, handle partial sends, and use a clearly defined protocol for message boundaries.

Q1. How to convert a binary file into CSV using programming?

Answer:
To convert a binary file into CSV, you must know the binary file's data structure, including the data types, record size, byte order (endianness), and any header information.

Steps:
1. Open the binary file in binary read mode.
2. Read the binary data.
3. Parse the data according to its structure.
4. Convert the extracted values into rows and columns.
5. Write the data into a CSV file.

Python Example:

import struct
import csv

record_format = '<iif'  # Two integers followed by one float
record_size = struct.calcsize(record_format)

with open('binary_file.dat', 'rb') as binary_file, \
     open('output.csv', 'w', newline='') as csv_file:

    csv_writer = csv.writer(csv_file)
    csv_writer.writerow(['Column1', 'Column2', 'Column3'])

    while True:
        record = binary_file.read(record_size)

        if not record:
            break

        if len(record) != record_size:
            raise ValueError("Incomplete binary record")

        values = struct.unpack(record_format, record)
        csv_writer.writerow(values)

Explanation:
- 'rb': Opens the binary file for reading.
- 'w': Opens the CSV file for writing.
- '<iif': Specifies little-endian byte order, two 32-bit integers, and one 32-bit float.
- struct.calcsize(): Calculates the size of one record.
- struct.unpack(): Converts binary bytes into Python values.
- csv.writer(): Writes the values into CSV rows.
- The incomplete-record check detects truncated data.

Important:
The format '<iif' is only an example. You must change it to match your binary file's actual structure. If the file contains a header, timestamps, arrays, or other data types, the parsing logic must account for them.

A binary file cannot always be converted directly into meaningful CSV without knowing its format.
Q1. How to send a 10 MB binary file from a C client to a server, save it as a new binary file on the server, and convert its structures and members into a CSV file?

Answer:

The process involves three steps:

1. Client: Read the binary file and send its bytes to the server using TCP sockets.
2. Server: Receive the bytes and save them into a new binary file.
3. Server: Read the received binary file, extract the structure members, and write their values into a CSV file.

CLIENT-SIDE CODE: READ A BINARY FILE

#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>

#define FILENAME "binary_file.bin"

int main(void)
{
    FILE *file = fopen(FILENAME, "rb");

    if (file == NULL)
    {
        perror("Error opening binary file");
        return 1;
    }

    fseek(file, 0, SEEK_END);
    long file_size = ftell(file);
    fseek(file, 0, SEEK_SET);

    if (file_size < 0)
    {
        perror("Error determining file size");
        fclose(file);
        return 1;
    }

    char *buffer = malloc((size_t)file_size);

    if (buffer == NULL)
    {
        perror("Memory allocation failed");
        fclose(file);
        return 1;
    }

    size_t bytesRead = fread(buffer, 1, (size_t)file_size, file);

    if (bytesRead != (size_t)file_size)
    {
        if (ferror(file))
            perror("Error reading binary file");
        else
            fprintf(stderr, "Error: Incomplete file read\n");

        free(buffer);
        fclose(file);
        return 1;
    }

    fclose(file);

    /*
       Send buffer to the server using TCP sockets.
       Implement a send loop to ensure all bytes are sent.
    */

    free(buffer);

    return 0;
}


Q2. Why do we use malloc() here?

Answer:

    char *buffer = malloc((size_t)file_size);

malloc() dynamically allocates memory based on the file size.

For example, if the file size is 10 MB, malloc() allocates approximately 10 MiB of memory when the size is 10 * 1024 * 1024 bytes.

Reasons:
1. The buffer size can be determined at runtime.
2. The buffer holds the binary file contents.
3. The buffer can be used to send the file data to the server.

Always check whether malloc() returns NULL and release the allocated memory using free().


Q3. I already know the file size is 10 MB. What should I change?

Answer:

You can use a fixed-size buffer instead of malloc().

#include <stdio.h>

#define FILENAME "binary_file.bin"
#define FILE_SIZE_BYTES (10 * 1024 * 1024)

int main(void)
{
    FILE *file = fopen(FILENAME, "rb");

    if (file == NULL)
    {
        perror("Error opening file");
        return 1;
    }

    char buffer[FILE_SIZE_BYTES];

    size_t bytesRead = fread(buffer, 1, sizeof(buffer), file);

    if (bytesRead != sizeof(buffer))
    {
        if (ferror(file))
            perror("Error reading file");
        else
            fprintf(stderr, "Error: File size is smaller than expected\n");

        fclose(file);
        return 1;
    }

    fclose(file);

    /*
       Send buffer to the server using TCP sockets.
    */

    return 0;
}

Important:
- This code assumes the file is exactly 10 MiB (10 * 1024 * 1024 bytes).
- A 10 MB file may instead mean 10,000,000 bytes.
- A large local array may exceed the available stack space on some systems.
- For a 10 MB file, reading and sending smaller chunks is generally more memory-efficient and reliable.


Q4. What does size_t bytesRead mean?

Answer:

    size_t bytesRead = fread(buffer, 1, FILE_SIZE_BYTES, file);

size_t is an unsigned integer type used to represent sizes and byte counts.

fread() returns the number of elements successfully read. Because each element in this example is one byte, bytesRead represents the number of bytes read.

Example:

If the expected file size is 10,485,760 bytes:

    bytesRead = 10485760;

means the entire expected amount was read.

    bytesRead = 5000000;

means only 5,000,000 bytes were read.

Always check the return value to detect incomplete reads.


Q5. Give the code with error handling for bytesRead.

Answer:

#include <stdio.h>

#define FILENAME "binary_file.bin"
#define FILE_SIZE_BYTES (10 * 1024 * 1024)

int main(void)
{
    FILE *file = fopen(FILENAME, "rb");

    if (file == NULL)
    {
        perror("Error opening binary file");
        return 1;
    }

    char buffer[FILE_SIZE_BYTES];

    size_t bytesRead = fread(buffer, 1, sizeof(buffer), file);

    if (bytesRead != sizeof(buffer))
    {
        if (ferror(file))
        {
            perror("Error reading binary file");
        }
        else if (feof(file))
        {
            fprintf(stderr,
                    "Error: End of file reached before reading expected bytes\n");
        }
        else
        {
            fprintf(stderr, "Error: Incomplete file read\n");
        }

        fclose(file);
        return 1;
    }

    fclose(file);

    printf("Successfully read %zu bytes\n", bytesRead);

    /*
       Send the buffer to the server using TCP sockets.
       Ensure the send loop handles partial sends.
    */

    return 0;
}

Explanation:

1. fopen() opens the file in binary-read mode.
2. fread() reads the expected number of bytes.
3. ferror() checks whether a read error occurred.
4. feof() checks whether the end of the file was reached.
5. fclose() closes the file.
6. A non-zero return value indicates failure.

Note: feof() becomes true only after a read attempts to pass the end of the file. For production code, comparing bytesRead with the expected size and checking ferror() is sufficient to identify incomplete reads and read errors.


Q6. How should the server save the received binary file and convert its structures into CSV?

Answer:

The server should:

1. Receive the file data over TCP.
2. Write all received bytes to a file opened with "wb".
3. Verify the expected file size and handle incomplete transfers.
4. Open the saved binary file in "rb" mode.
5. Parse its contents using the correct binary format.
6. Write the extracted structure members to a CSV file using fprintf().

Example structure and CSV conversion:

#include <stdio.h>

typedef struct
{
    int id;
    float value;
} Data;

int main(void)
{
    FILE *bin = fopen("received_file.bin", "rb");

    if (bin == NULL)
    {
        perror("Error opening binary file");
        return 1;
    }

    FILE *csv = fopen("structure_details.csv", "w");

    if (csv == NULL)
    {
        perror("Error opening CSV file");
        fclose(bin);
        return 1;
    }

    Data data;

    fprintf(csv, "id,value\n");

    while (fread(&data, sizeof(data), 1, bin) == 1)
    {
        fprintf(csv, "%d,%f\n", data.id, data.value);
    }

    if (ferror(bin))
    {
        perror("Error reading binary file");
        fclose(bin);
        fclose(csv);
        return 1;
    }

    fclose(bin);
    fclose(csv);

    return 0;
}

Important:
- This example works only if the binary file consists of consecutive records matching the Data structure's binary layout.
- If the file contains multiple different structures, headers, offsets, or variable-length records, the server needs the actual file format specification.
- Structure padding, alignment, endianness, and compiler differences can affect binary parsing.
- Never assume that arbitrary binary bytes can be interpreted as a C structure without validating the format.
- A TCP server must handle partial sends and receives because one send() call does not guarantee that all bytes arrive in one recv() call.
Q1. How to convert Employee and Department structures from a binary file to a CSV file in C?

Answer:

#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
#include <string.h>

typedef struct {
    int id;
    char name[50];
    float salary;
} Employee;

typedef struct {
    int dept_id;
    char dept_name[50];
} Department;

void convertToCSV(const char *inputFileName,
                  const char *outputFileName)
{
    FILE *inputFile = fopen(inputFileName, "rb");
    FILE *outputFile = fopen(outputFileName, "w");

    if (inputFile == NULL || outputFile == NULL) {
        printf("Error opening files\n");

        if (inputFile != NULL)
            fclose(inputFile);

        if (outputFile != NULL)
            fclose(outputFile);

        return;
    }

    Employee emp;

    while (fread(&emp, sizeof(Employee), 1, inputFile) == 1) {
        fprintf(outputFile, "Employee,%d,%s,%.2f\n",
                emp.id, emp.name, emp.salary);
    }

    rewind(inputFile);

    Department dept;

    while (fread(&dept, sizeof(Department), 1, inputFile) == 1) {
        fprintf(outputFile, "Department,%d,%s\n",
                dept.dept_id, dept.dept_name);
    }

    fclose(inputFile);
    fclose(outputFile);

    printf("Conversion completed\n");
}

int main(void)
{
    convertToCSV("input.bin", "output.csv");
    return 0;
}


Important:
This approach works only if the binary file contains records in the expected layout. If Employee and Department records are mixed in the same file, reading the file separately using two different structure sizes will not work reliably.

--------------------------------------------------

Q2. What if there are more than 10 Employee and Department structures?

Answer:

Use a loop to read records until fread() reaches the end of the file. Do not impose a fixed limit of 10.

Example:

Employee emp;

while (fread(&emp, sizeof(Employee), 1, inputFile) == 1) {
    fprintf(outputFile, "Employee,%d,%s,%.2f\n",
            emp.id, emp.name, emp.salary);
}

The loop processes all records that match the expected binary layout.

--------------------------------------------------

Q3. What if there are multiple types of structures, such as Employee, Department, Student, Teacher, School, Database, and College? How can all these structures be handled at the same time?

Answer:

You cannot reliably read different structure types from a binary file by simply trying each structure type in sequence. You need a way to identify each record's type and size.

A practical solution is to store a record header before each structure. The header contains a type identifier, allowing the program to determine which structure to read.

Example:

#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdint.h>
#include <string.h>

typedef enum {
    TYPE_EMPLOYEE = 1,
    TYPE_DEPARTMENT,
    TYPE_STUDENT,
    TYPE_TEACHER,
    TYPE_SCHOOL,
    TYPE_DATABASE,
    TYPE_COLLEGE
} RecordType;

typedef struct {
    int id;
    char name[50];
    float salary;
} Employee;

typedef struct {
    int dept_id;
    char dept_name[50];
} Department;

typedef struct {
    uint32_t type;
    uint32_t size;
} RecordHeader;

void convertToCSV(const char *inputFileName,
                  const char *outputFileName)
{
    FILE *in = fopen(inputFileName, "rb");
    FILE *out = fopen(outputFileName, "w");

    if (in == NULL || out == NULL) {
        printf("Error opening files\n");

        if (in != NULL)
            fclose(in);

        if (out != NULL)
            fclose(out);

        return;
    }

    RecordHeader header;

    while (fread(&header, sizeof(header), 1, in) == 1) {

        switch (header.type) {

        case TYPE_EMPLOYEE:
        {
            Employee emp;

            if (header.size != sizeof(emp) ||
                fread(&emp, sizeof(emp), 1, in) != 1) {
                printf("Invalid Employee record\n");
                goto cleanup;
            }

            fprintf(out, "Employee,%d,%s,%.2f\n",
                    emp.id, emp.name, emp.salary);
            break;
        }

        case TYPE_DEPARTMENT:
        {
            Department dept;

            if (header.size != sizeof(dept) ||
                fread(&dept, sizeof(dept), 1, in) != 1) {
                printf("Invalid Department record\n");
                goto cleanup;
            }

            fprintf(out, "Department,%d,%s\n",
                    dept.dept_id, dept.dept_name);
            break;
        }

        default:
            printf("Unknown record type: %u\n", header.type);

            /*
             * Skip unknown record payloads.
             * A production implementation should also validate
             * the size and check for file-position errors.
             */
            if (fseek(in, (long)header.size, SEEK_CUR) != 0) {
                printf("Error skipping unknown record\n");
                goto cleanup;
            }

            break;
        }
    }

cleanup:
    fclose(in);
    fclose(out);
}

int main(void)
{
    convertToCSV("input.bin", "output.csv");
    return 0;
}


Note:
This example demonstrates Employee and Department records. Add a case for each remaining structure type, with its corresponding structure definition and CSV formatting.

The binary file must have been written using the same record-header format. Existing binary files without type information cannot generally be interpreted reliably when different structure types are mixed together.

For portable binary formats, also consider field-by-field serialization, explicit byte order, versioning, and validation rather than writing raw C structures directly.
# C PROGRAMMING: TCP CLIENT-SERVER COMMUNICATION, FILE HANDLING, BINARY TRANSFER, CSV CONVERSION, AND SOCKET RECONNECTIONS

## 1. TCP Client-Server Chat Using File Handling in C

**Question:** How to implement TCP client-server communication in C using file handling?

**Answer:**

Use Winsock on Windows to create TCP sockets. The server receives messages from the client and logs them to a file using `fprintf()`. The client sends messages using `send()` and receives responses using `recv()`.

Required headers:

```c
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
#include <string.h>
#include <winsock2.h>
```

For a Windows application, link against `Ws2_32.lib` and initialize Winsock using `WSAStartup()`.

---

## 2. Server Code: TCP Communication and File Handling

**Question:** Give server code using Winsock, file handling, and the ability to accept client connections repeatedly.

**Answer:**

```c
#include "stdafx.h"
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
#include <string.h>
#include <winsock2.h>

#pragma comment(lib, "Ws2_32.lib")

#define PORT 8085
#define BUFF_SIZE 1024
#define IP_ADDRESS "127.0.0.1"
#define DP_ZERO 0
#define DP_ONE 1
#define SUCCESS 0
#define FAILURE 1

int main(void)
{
    WSADATA wsaData;
    SOCKET SServerSocket;
    SOCKET SClientSocket;
    struct sockaddr_in SServerAddr;
    struct sockaddr_in SClientAddr;
    int iClientAddrLen;
    int iReadVal;
    char arrc_Buffer[BUFF_SIZE];

    FILE *file;

    if (WSAStartup(MAKEWORD(2, 2), &wsaData) != 0)
    {
        printf("WSAStartup failed\n");
        return FAILURE;
    }

    SServerSocket = socket(AF_INET, SOCK_STREAM, 0);

    if (SServerSocket == INVALID_SOCKET)
    {
        printf("Socket creation failed\n");
        WSACleanup();
        return FAILURE;
    }

    memset(&SServerAddr, 0, sizeof(SServerAddr));

    SServerAddr.sin_family = AF_INET;
    SServerAddr.sin_port = htons(PORT);
    SServerAddr.sin_addr.s_addr = inet_addr(IP_ADDRESS);

    if (bind(SServerSocket,
             (struct sockaddr *)&SServerAddr,
             sizeof(SServerAddr)) == SOCKET_ERROR)
    {
        printf("Bind failed: %d\n", WSAGetLastError());
        closesocket(SServerSocket);
        WSACleanup();
        return FAILURE;
    }

    if (listen(SServerSocket, 5) == SOCKET_ERROR)
    {
        printf("Listen failed\n");
        closesocket(SServerSocket);
        WSACleanup();
        return FAILURE;
    }

    printf("Server listening on port %d\n", PORT);

    while (DP_ONE)
    {
        iClientAddrLen = sizeof(SClientAddr);

        SClientSocket = accept(
            SServerSocket,
            (struct sockaddr *)&SClientAddr,
            &iClientAddrLen);

        if (SClientSocket == INVALID_SOCKET)
        {
            printf("Accept failed\n");
            continue;
        }

        printf("Client connected: %s\n",
               inet_ntoa(SClientAddr.sin_addr));

        file = fopen("received_messages.txt", "a");

        if (file == NULL)
        {
            printf("File opening failed\n");
            closesocket(SClientSocket);
            continue;
        }

        while (DP_ONE)
        {
            iReadVal = recv(
                SClientSocket,
                arrc_Buffer,
                BUFF_SIZE - 1,
                0);

            if (iReadVal == 0)
            {
                printf("Client disconnected\n");
                break;
            }

            if (iReadVal == SOCKET_ERROR)
            {
                printf("Receive failed\n");
                break;
            }

            arrc_Buffer[iReadVal] = '\0';

            printf("Client: %s\n", arrc_Buffer);

            fprintf(file, "Client: %s\n", arrc_Buffer);
            fflush(file);

            if (strcmp(arrc_Buffer, "exit") == 0)
            {
                printf("Client requested exit\n");
                break;
            }

            {
                const char response[] = "Message received";
                send(SClientSocket, response,
                     (int)strlen(response), 0);
            }
        }

        fclose(file);
        closesocket(SClientSocket);

        printf("Waiting for another client...\n");
    }

    closesocket(SServerSocket);
    WSACleanup();

    return SUCCESS;
}
```

**Important:** This server handles one client at a time, but continues running after that client disconnects. The response is an acknowledgment, not an echo of the received message.

---

## 3. Client Code: TCP Communication and File Handling

**Question:** Give the corresponding C client code.

**Answer:**

```c
#include "stdafx.h"
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
#include <string.h>
#include <winsock2.h>

#pragma comment(lib, "Ws2_32.lib")

#define PORT 8085
#define BUFF_SIZE 1024
#define IP_ADDRESS "127.0.0.1"
#define SUCCESS 0
#define FAILURE 1

int main(void)
{
    WSADATA wsaData;
    SOCKET SClientSocket;
    struct sockaddr_in SServerAddr;

    char arrc_Message[BUFF_SIZE];
    char arrc_Response[BUFF_SIZE];

    int iReadVal;

    FILE *file;

    if (WSAStartup(MAKEWORD(2, 2), &wsaData) != 0)
    {
        printf("WSAStartup failed\n");
        return FAILURE;
    }

    SClientSocket = socket(AF_INET, SOCK_STREAM, 0);

    if (SClientSocket == INVALID_SOCKET)
    {
        printf("Socket creation failed\n");
        WSACleanup();
        return FAILURE;
    }

    memset(&SServerAddr, 0, sizeof(SServerAddr));

    SServerAddr.sin_family = AF_INET;
    SServerAddr.sin_port = htons(PORT);
    SServerAddr.sin_addr.s_addr = inet_addr(IP_ADDRESS);

    if (connect(SClientSocket,
                (struct sockaddr *)&SServerAddr,
                sizeof(SServerAddr)) == SOCKET_ERROR)
    {
        printf("Connection failed\n");
        closesocket(SClientSocket);
        WSACleanup();
        return FAILURE;
    }

    file = fopen("messages.txt", "a");

    if (file == NULL)
    {
        printf("File opening failed\n");
        closesocket(SClientSocket);
        WSACleanup();
        return FAILURE;
    }

    while (1)
    {
        printf("Enter message (exit to quit): ");

        if (fgets(arrc_Message, sizeof(arrc_Message), stdin) == NULL)
        {
            break;
        }

        arrc_Message[strcspn(arrc_Message, "\r\n")] = '\0';

        if (send(SClientSocket,
                 arrc_Message,
                 (int)strlen(arrc_Message),
                 0) == SOCKET_ERROR)
        {
            printf("Send failed\n");
            break;
        }

        fprintf(file, "Client: %s\n", arrc_Message);
        fflush(file);

        if (strcmp(arrc_Message, "exit") == 0)
        {
            break;
        }

        iReadVal = recv(
            SClientSocket,
            arrc_Response,
            BUFF_SIZE - 1,
            0);

        if (iReadVal <= 0)
        {
            printf("Server disconnected or receive failed\n");
            break;
        }

        arrc_Response[iReadVal] = '\0';

        printf("Server: %s\n", arrc_Response);

        fprintf(file, "Server: %s\n", arrc_Response);
        fflush(file);
    }

    fclose(file);
    closesocket(SClientSocket);
    WSACleanup();

    return SUCCESS;
}
```

**Note:** This is a basic text-chat example. TCP does not guarantee that one `send()` corresponds to one `recv()`. For arbitrary-length messages, implement a message-framing protocol.

---

## 4. Validate a Character Array With a Length Between 10 and 20

**Question:** How do I validate a character array whose string length must be between 10 and 20 characters?

**Answer:**

```c
#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>

int validateCharArray(const char *arr)
{
    size_t length;

    if (arr == NULL)
    {
        return 0;
    }

    length = strlen(arr);

    if (length >= 10 && length <= 20)
    {
        return 1;
    }

    return 0;
}

int main(void)
{
    char arr[] = "Hello123456";

    if (validateCharArray(arr))
    {
        printf("Valid string\n");
    }
    else
    {
        printf("Invalid string\n");
    }

    return 0;
}
```

**Explanation:**

- `strlen()` returns the number of characters before the terminating `'\0'`.
- Length 10 is valid.
- Length 20 is valid.
- Length less than 10 or greater than 20 is invalid.

This checks string length, not the allocated size of the array.

---

## 5. Transfer a 10 MB Binary File From Client to Server

**Question:** The client has a binary file containing many structures and their members. The file is approximately 10 MB. How can the client send it to the server, where the server saves it as a new binary file and converts its contents into CSV?

**Answer:**

The process is:

1. Client opens the binary file using `fopen(filename, "rb")`.
2. Client reads the file in chunks using `fread()`.
3. Client sends the chunks using `send()`.
4. Server receives chunks using `recv()`.
5. Server writes received bytes to a file opened using `"wb"`.
6. After the complete file is received, the server parses the binary records.
7. Server writes the extracted members to a CSV file using `fprintf()`.

Do not allocate memory for the entire 10 MB file unless necessary. A fixed-size buffer is sufficient.

### Client: Send the Binary File

```c
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
#include <winsock2.h>

#pragma comment(lib, "Ws2_32.lib")

#define PORT 8085
#define BUFF_SIZE 4096
#define SERVER_IP "127.0.0.1"
#define INPUT_FILE "input.bin"

int main(void)
{
    WSADATA wsaData;
    SOCKET clientSocket;
    struct sockaddr_in serverAddr;

    FILE *file;
    char buffer[BUFF_SIZE];

    size_t bytesRead;
    size_t totalRead = 0;
    int bytesSent;
    size_t offset;

    if (WSAStartup(MAKEWORD(2, 2), &wsaData) != 0)
    {
        printf("WSAStartup failed\n");
        return 1;
    }

    file = fopen(INPUT_FILE, "rb");

    if (file == NULL)
    {
        printf("Cannot open input file\n");
        WSACleanup();
        return 1;
    }

    clientSocket = socket(AF_INET, SOCK_STREAM, 0);

    if (clientSocket == INVALID_SOCKET)
    {
        printf("Socket creation failed\n");
        fclose(file);
        WSACleanup();
        return 1;
    }

    serverAddr.sin_family = AF_INET;
    serverAddr.sin_port = htons(PORT);
    serverAddr.sin_addr.s_addr = inet_addr(SERVER_IP);

    if (connect(clientSocket,
                (struct sockaddr *)&serverAddr,
                sizeof(serverAddr)) == SOCKET_ERROR)
    {
        printf("Connection failed\n");
        closesocket(clientSocket);
        fclose(file);
        WSACleanup();
        return 1;
    }

    while ((bytesRead = fread(buffer, 1, BUFF_SIZE, file)) > 0)
    {
        offset = 0;

        while (offset < bytesRead)
        {
            bytesSent = send(
                clientSocket,
                buffer + offset,
                (int)(bytesRead - offset),
                0);

            if (bytesSent == SOCKET_ERROR || bytesSent == 0)
            {
                printf("Send failed\n");
                fclose(file);
                closesocket(clientSocket);
                WSACleanup();
                return 1;
            }

            offset += (size_t)bytesSent;
        }

        totalRead += bytesRead;
    }

    if (ferror(file))
    {
        printf("File read failed\n");
    }
    else
    {
        printf("File data sent: %lu bytes\n",
               (unsigned long)totalRead);
    }

    /*
       A production implementation should send a file-size
       header or use a framed protocol, then signal end-of-file.
       Do not use connection closure as the only completion
       mechanism if the connection must remain open.
    */

    shutdown(clientSocket, SD_SEND);

    fclose(file);
    closesocket(clientSocket);
    WSACleanup();

    return 0;
}
```

**Important:** The example signals the end of the transfer by shutting down the client's sending direction. If you need the same connection to remain open for further commands, send the file length in a defined protocol header and receive exactly that number of bytes on the server.

### Server: Receive the Binary File

```c
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
#include <winsock2.h>

#pragma comment(lib, "Ws2_32.lib")

#define PORT 8085
#define BUFF_SIZE 4096
#define OUTPUT_FILE "received.bin"

int main(void)
{
    WSADATA wsaData;

    SOCKET serverSocket;
    SOCKET clientSocket;

    struct sockaddr_in serverAddr;
    struct sockaddr_in clientAddr;

    int clientAddrLen = sizeof(clientAddr);
    int bytesReceived;

    char buffer[BUFF_SIZE];
    FILE *file;

    if (WSAStartup(MAKEWORD(2, 2), &wsaData) != 0)
    {
        printf("WSAStartup failed\n");
        return 1;
    }

    serverSocket = socket(AF_INET, SOCK_STREAM, 0);

    if (serverSocket == INVALID_SOCKET)
    {
        printf("Socket creation failed\n");
        WSACleanup();
        return 1;
    }

    serverAddr.sin_family = AF_INET;
    serverAddr.sin_port = htons(PORT);
    serverAddr.sin_addr.s_addr = inet_addr("127.0.0.1");

    if (bind(serverSocket,
             (struct sockaddr *)&serverAddr,
             sizeof(serverAddr)) == SOCKET_ERROR)
    {
        printf("Bind failed\n");
        closesocket(serverSocket);
        WSACleanup();
        return 1;
    }

    if (listen(serverSocket, 5) == SOCKET_ERROR)
    {
        printf("Listen failed\n");
        closesocket(serverSocket);
        WSACleanup();
        return 1;
    }

    printf("Server listening on port %d\n", PORT);

    while (1)
    {
        clientAddrLen = sizeof(clientAddr);

        clientSocket = accept(
            serverSocket,
            (struct sockaddr *)&clientAddr,
            &clientAddrLen);

        if (clientSocket == INVALID_SOCKET)
        {
            printf("Accept failed\n");
            continue;
        }

        file = fopen(OUTPUT_FILE, "wb");

        if (file == NULL)
        {
            printf("Cannot create output file\n");
            closesocket(clientSocket);
            continue;
        }

        printf("Receiving binary file...\n");

        while ((bytesReceived = recv(
                    clientSocket,
                    buffer,
                    BUFF_SIZE,
                    0)) > 0)
        {
            if (fwrite(buffer, 1,
                        (size_t)bytesReceived,
                        file) != (size_t)bytesReceived)
            {
                printf("File write failed\n");
                break;
            }
        }

        if (bytesReceived == SOCKET_ERROR)
        {
            printf("Receive failed\n");
        }
        else if (bytesReceived == 0)
        {
            printf("File transfer ended\n");
        }

        fclose(file);
        closesocket(clientSocket);

        printf("Binary file saved as %s\n", OUTPUT_FILE);

        /*
           Once the complete transfer is verified,
           call the binary-to-CSV conversion function here.
        */
    }

    closesocket(serverSocket);
    WSACleanup();

    return 0;
}
```

**Key point:** `recv()` must be called repeatedly because the entire file may not arrive in one call. A 4096-byte buffer transfers a 10 MB file in manageable chunks.

The server above saves one file at a time to `received.bin`. Use a unique filename for each transfer if previous files must be preserved.

---

## 6. Why Use `fseek()`, `ftell()`, and `char *buffer`?

**Question:**

```c
fseek(file, 0, SEEK_END);
file_size = ftell(file);
fseek(file, 0, SEEK_SET);
```

Why is this required? Why use `char *buffer` instead of a character array?

**Answer:**

### `fseek()`

Moves the file position indicator.

```c
fseek(file, 0, SEEK_END);
```

Moves to the end of the file.

```c
fseek(file, 0, SEEK_SET);
```

Moves back to the beginning.

### `ftell()`

```c
file_size = ftell(file);
```

Returns the current file position. After seeking to the end, it is commonly used to determine the file size for ordinary binary files on Windows.

Check for errors before using the result.

### `char *buffer`

```c
char *buffer;
buffer = malloc(file_size);
```

A pointer can refer to dynamically allocated memory sized at runtime.

A fixed array is also valid:

```c
char buffer[4096];
```

For transferring large files, the fixed-size array is generally preferable because it avoids allocating the entire file in memory.

---

## 7. Convert Binary Structures Into CSV

**Question:** How can the server convert the received binary file into CSV?

**Answer:**

The binary file must have a known format. The server must know:

- The structure definitions.
- The order of records.
- The number of records or a reliable end-of-file condition.
- Member types and sizes.
- Structure padding and alignment.
- Byte order and compiler-specific layout, if applicable.

For illustration, assume `received.bin` contains consecutive records of this exact structure:

```c
typedef struct
{
    int id;
    char name[50];
    float salary;
} Employee;
```

Conversion code:

```c
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
#include <string.h>

typedef struct
{
    int id;
    char name[50];
    float salary;
} Employee;

int convertToCSV(const char *inputFileName,
                 const char *outputFileName)
{
    FILE *inputFile;
    FILE *outputFile;

    Employee emp;
    size_t bytesRead;

    inputFile = fopen(inputFileName, "rb");

    if (inputFile == NULL)
    {
        printf("Cannot open binary file\n");
        return 0;
    }

    outputFile = fopen(outputFileName, "w");

    if (outputFile == NULL)
    {
        printf("Cannot open CSV file\n");
        fclose(inputFile);
        return 0;
    }

    fprintf(outputFile, "ID,Name,Salary\n");

    while ((bytesRead = fread(
                &emp, 1, sizeof(emp), inputFile))
                == sizeof(emp))
    {
        emp.name[sizeof(emp.name) - 1] = '\0';

        fprintf(outputFile, "%d,%s,%.2f\n",
                emp.id, emp.name, emp.salary);
    }

    if (ferror(inputFile) || bytesRead != 0)
    {
        printf("Binary file read error or incomplete record\n");
        fclose(inputFile);
        fclose(outputFile);
        return 0;
    }

    fclose(inputFile);
    fclose(outputFile);

    printf("CSV conversion completed\n");

    return 1;
}

int main(void)
{
    if (!convertToCSV("received.bin", "output.csv"))
    {
        return 1;
    }

    return 0;
}
```

**Important:** This example only works if the file consists entirely of consecutive `Employee` records written using a compatible binary layout. It cannot automatically identify arbitrary structures.

---

## 8. What If the Binary File Contains Three Different Structures?

**Question:** If the binary file contains three structures, such as `Employee`, `Department`, and `Student`, what should be done?

**Answer:**

If the file contains different structure types, the server needs to know which structure each record represents.

For example:

```c
typedef struct
{
    int id;
    char name[50];
    float salary;
} Employee;

typedef struct
{
    int dept_id;
    char dept_name[50];
} Department;

typedef struct
{
    int student_id;
    char student_name[50];
    float marks;
} Student;
```

You cannot safely read the entire file as `Employee`, then seek to the beginning and read it as `Department`. Each structure has a different size and layout.

### Recommended solution: Add a record type

Define a record format with a type identifier and a length, for example:

```c
typedef struct
{
    unsigned int type;
    unsigned int length;
} RecordHeader;
```

Each record in the file contains:

1. A record header.
2. The record's payload.
3. The next record header, if another record follows.

The server reads the header, checks the record type, validates its length, reads the corresponding payload, and writes the appropriate fields to CSV.

For a portable binary protocol, encode header fields explicitly rather than transmitting raw C structures, since padding and byte order can vary.

An alternative is to maintain three separate binary files, one for each structure type, if the file format permits that design.

---

## 9. Why Does `recv()` Need a Loop?

**Question:** Can `recv()` read all bytes at once, or is a loop necessary?

**Answer:**

`recv()` reads up to the requested buffer size. It may return fewer bytes than expected.

For example, if the client sends 10 MB and the server buffer is 4096 bytes, the server must repeatedly call `recv()` until the complete transfer has been received.

```c
while ((bytesReceived = recv(
            clientSocket,
            buffer,
            sizeof(buffer),
            0)) > 0)
{
    fwrite(buffer, 1, bytesReceived, file);
}
```

This loop continues until the peer closes its sending direction, an error occurs, or the connection otherwise ends.

For a transfer that must leave the connection open, use a known length or a framing protocol instead of waiting for connection closure.

---

## 10. Is an `if` Condition Enough Instead of a `while` Loop?

**Question:** Can I use `if (recv(...) > 0)` instead of a loop?

**Answer:**

An `if` condition performs only one receive operation.

```c
if ((bytesReceived = recv(
         clientSocket, buffer, sizeof(buffer), 0)) > 0)
{
    fwrite(buffer, 1, bytesReceived, file);
}
```

This is insufficient for a file transfer because it may process only part of the file.

Use a loop when you need to receive multiple chunks. Use an `if` only when one receive operation is deliberately all that is required.

---

## 11. Why Does the Server Use `while (1)` Around `accept()`?

**Question:** The server should always run. Whenever a client wants to connect, it should be able to connect. Why use this loop?

**Answer:**

```c
while (1)
{
    clientSocket = accept(
        serverSocket,
        (struct sockaddr *)&clientAddr,
        &clientAddrLen);

    if (clientSocket == INVALID_SOCKET)
    {
        printf("Accept failed\n");
        continue;
    }

    /* Handle the connected client. */

    closesocket(clientSocket);
}
```

The outer `while (1)` keeps the server running so it can accept another connection after the current client disconnects.

The server and client sockets have different purposes:

- `serverSocket` remains open and listens for new connections.
- `clientSocket` represents one accepted connection.
- `closesocket(clientSocket)` closes that connection only.
- The server then returns to `accept()`.
