This design handles clients sequentially. To handle multiple clients simultaneously, use threads, asynchronous I/O, or another concurrency mechanism.

---

## 12. How Does the Echo Server Work?

**Question:** What does this code do?

```c
while ((bytesReceived = recv(
            clientSocket, buffer, BUFF_SIZE, 0)) > 0)
{
    send(clientSocket, buffer, bytesReceived, 0);
}
```

**Answer:**

It receives data and sends the received bytes back to the same client.

Example:

Client sends:

```text
Hello Server
```

Server sends back:

```text
Hello Server
```

This is called an echo server.

The loop handles repeated chunks until the client disconnects or a socket error occurs. A robust implementation must also handle partial `send()` operations because `send()` may transmit fewer bytes than requested.

---

## 13. What Happens When a Client Reconnects?

**Question:** After the client reconnects, why might the server receive only part of the data?

**Answer:**

TCP does not preserve application-message boundaries. One `send()` can be received through multiple `recv()` calls, and multiple sends can also be received together.

A reconnect creates a new TCP connection. It does not automatically resume the previous transfer or guarantee that the complete file will be sent again.

To transfer a complete file reliably:

1. Define a transfer protocol.
2. Send the expected file size or a framed header.
3. Read and write data until exactly that number of bytes has been transferred.
4. Verify the received size.
5. Optionally compare a checksum or cryptographic hash.
6. If the connection fails, discard the incomplete file or resume using a clearly defined offset-based protocol.

If the client reconnects and resends the entire file, the server must create a new file or explicitly manage the transfer so old and new bytes are not mixed.

---

## 14. Important Corrections for Reliable Binary File Transfer

**Question:** What should be corrected in a real 10 MB binary-transfer implementation?

**Answer:**

- Do not assume one `send()` transmits all requested bytes.
- Do not assume one `recv()` receives all requested bytes.
- Do not use `strlen()` on arbitrary binary data; binary files may contain null bytes.
- Do not use `arrc_Buffer[iReadVal] = '\0'` for binary data. Binary data should be written using the exact number of bytes returned by `recv()`.
- Use `"rb"` to read binary files and `"wb"` to create or overwrite binary files.
- Use `"a"` or `"ab"` only when appending is explicitly intended.
- Track the expected file size or use a defined end-of-transfer protocol.
- Check `fread()`, `fwrite()`, `send()`, and `recv()` return values.
- Close each client socket after its transfer and keep the listening socket open.
- Do not parse a binary file until the complete transfer has been verified.
- Never assume that a raw structure written by one compiler can be parsed identically by every other compiler or platform.

### Final summary

For your application, the recommended architecture is:

```text
CLIENT
  |
  | Open input.bin
  | Read chunks using fread()
  | Send all bytes using a send loop
  v
TCP CONNECTION
  |
  v
SERVER
  |
  | Receive chunks using recv()
  | Write exact byte counts using fwrite()
  | Verify complete file
  v
received.bin
  |
  | Parse according to the known binary format
  | Identify each structure or record type
  v
output.csv
```

The most important requirement is knowing the exact binary file format. C cannot automatically determine the structure definitions or member names merely by examining arbitrary binary bytes.
# C PROGRAMMING: TCP FILE TRANSFER, BINARY FILE HANDLING, AND CSV CONVERSION

## 1. How do I read a binary file containing S_EMITTER_DATA structures and convert it to CSV?

**Answer:**

Open the binary file using `"rb"` and the CSV file using `"w"`. Read one complete structure at a time using `fread()`, then write its fields using `fprintf()`.

```c
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>

#define FILE_NAME "input.bin"
#define OUTPUT_CSV "output.csv"

int main(void)
{
    FILE *f_bin = fopen(FILE_NAME, "rb");
    FILE *f_csv = fopen(OUTPUT_CSV, "w");
    S_EMITTER_DATA data;

    if (f_bin == NULL || f_csv == NULL)
    {
        fprintf(stderr, "Error opening files.\n");

        if (f_bin != NULL)
            fclose(f_bin);

        if (f_csv != NULL)
            fclose(f_csv);

        return EXIT_FAILURE;
    }

    fprintf(f_csv,
        "PulseWidth_us,Direction_Deg,DOA_Deg,"
        "Amplitude_dBm,MinAmplitude_dBm,"
        "PRI_Type,NoOfPRIs,Frequency_MHz,Bandwidth_MHz\n");

    while (fread(&data, sizeof(data), 1, f_bin) == 1)
    {
        fprintf(f_csv, "%.2f,%.2f,%.2f,%.2f,%.2f,"
                "%hu,%hu,%.2f,%.2f\n",
                data.fPulseWidth_us,
                data.fDirection_Deg,
                data.fDOA_Deg,
                data.fAmplitude_dBm,
                data.fMinAmplitude_dBm,
                data.usPRI_Type,
                data.usNoOfPRIs,
                data.dFrequency_MHz,
                data.dBandwidth_MHz);
    }

    if (ferror(f_bin))
        fprintf(stderr, "Error reading binary file.\n");

    fclose(f_bin);
    fclose(f_csv);

    return EXIT_SUCCESS;
}
```

The structure definition must be identical to the one used when the binary file was created.

---

## 2. How does the binary file access the correct data if the data is stored randomly?

**Answer:**

A binary file does not automatically identify C structures. The program reads bytes according to the structure layout.

For example:

```c
S_EMITTER_DATA data;

fread(&data, sizeof(S_EMITTER_DATA), 1, f_bin);
```

This reads `sizeof(S_EMITTER_DATA)` bytes into `data`.

If the file contains consecutive structures, the next call reads the next structure.

If the file contains a header, padding, a different structure version, or another record layout, you must account for it.

Important considerations:

- The structure layout must match the layout used to create the file.
- Compiler padding and alignment can affect `sizeof()`.
- Integer sizes and byte order must match.
- The file must contain complete records.
- If records are stored at arbitrary offsets, seek to the correct offset before reading.

---

## 3. How do I send a binary file through TCP using chunks?

**Answer:**

Read a limited number of bytes from the file, then send those bytes through the socket. Repeat until the entire file has been sent.

```c
#define CHUNK_SIZE (1024 * 1024)

char arrc_Buffer[CHUNK_SIZE];

int iFileSize;
int iBytesRead;
int iTotalBytesSent = 0;
int iBytesToSend;
int iSent;
int iOffset;

fseek(fptrFile, 0, SEEK_END);
iFileSize = ftell(fptrFile);
fseek(fptrFile, 0, SEEK_SET);

while (iTotalBytesSent < iFileSize)
{
    iBytesToSend = iFileSize - iTotalBytesSent;

    if (iBytesToSend > CHUNK_SIZE)
        iBytesToSend = CHUNK_SIZE;

    iBytesRead = (int)fread(
        arrc_Buffer, 1, iBytesToSend, fptrFile);

    if (iBytesRead <= 0)
    {
        fprintf(stderr, "Error reading file.\n");
        break;
    }

    iOffset = 0;

    while (iOffset < iBytesRead)
    {
        iSent = send(
            SClientSocket,
            arrc_Buffer + iOffset,
            iBytesRead - iOffset,
            0);

        if (iSent == SOCKET_ERROR || iSent == 0)
        {
            fprintf(stderr, "Socket send failed: %d\n",
                    WSAGetLastError());
            break;
        }

        iOffset += iSent;
    }

    if (iOffset != iBytesRead)
        break;

    iTotalBytesSent += iBytesRead;

    printf("Sent %d bytes. Total: %d\n",
           iBytesRead, iTotalBytesSent);
}
```

**Why use a chunk?**

A 10 MB file can be transferred using a 1 MB buffer instead of allocating 10 MB of memory.

Also, `send()` may send fewer bytes than requested. Therefore, a loop is required to send all bytes read from the file.

---

## 4. Why did the client print only one chunk instead of the entire file size?

**Answer:**

The chunk loop prints once for each successful iteration. If the buffer size is 1 MB, a file of approximately 10 MB should require about 11 iterations.

The correct approach is to:

1. Read the number of bytes requested.
2. Check the return value from `fread()`.
3. Send all bytes read, handling partial sends.
4. Increase the total by the actual number of bytes read.
5. Repeat until the entire file is sent.

Do not increase the total by the requested number of bytes if fewer bytes were actually read or sent.

---

## 5. How do I make the buffer size dynamic based on the file size?

**Answer:**

If the entire file must be loaded into memory:

```c
char *arrc_Buffer = NULL;

arrc_Buffer = (char *)malloc((size_t)iFileSize);

if (arrc_Buffer == NULL)
{
    fprintf(stderr, "Memory allocation failed.\n");
    return FAILURE;
}
```

However, for TCP file transfer, allocating the entire file is unnecessary. A fixed-size buffer is usually better:

```c
char arrc_Buffer[CHUNK_SIZE];
```

This avoids excessive memory allocation for large files.

**Important:** Do not use `malloc(iFileSize)` when `iFileSize` is still zero.

---

## 6. How do I receive the binary file on the TCP server in chunks?

**Answer:**

Receive each chunk and immediately write it to the binary file.

```c
#define CHUNK_SIZE (1024 * 1024)

char arrc_Buffer[CHUNK_SIZE];
int iBytesRead;
int iTotalBytes = 0;
size_t sBytesWritten;

do
{
    iBytesRead = recv(
        SClientSocket,
        arrc_Buffer,
        CHUNK_SIZE,
        0);

    if (iBytesRead > 0)
    {
        sBytesWritten = fwrite(
            arrc_Buffer, 1, (size_t)iBytesRead, fptrFile);

        if (sBytesWritten != (size_t)iBytesRead)
        {
            fprintf(stderr, "Error writing binary file.\n");
            break;
        }

        iTotalBytes += iBytesRead;
    }
    else if (iBytesRead == SOCKET_ERROR)
    {
        fprintf(stderr, "Receive failed: %d\n",
                WSAGetLastError());
        break;
    }

} while (iBytesRead > 0);

printf("Received %d bytes.\n", iTotalBytes);
```

`recv()` returns the number of bytes received, zero when the peer closes the connection, or `SOCKET_ERROR` on failure.

This example assumes the client closes its sending connection after the file transfer. If the protocol sends the file size first, receive exactly that many bytes instead of relying on connection closure.

---

## 7. Why is `sizeof(arrc_Buffer)` incorrect for a dynamically allocated buffer?

**Answer:**

Consider:

```c
char *arrc_Buffer = (char *)malloc(CHUNK_SIZE);

recv(SClientSocket, arrc_Buffer, sizeof(arrc_Buffer), 0);
```

`sizeof(arrc_Buffer)` returns the size of the pointer, typically 4 bytes on 32-bit systems or 8 bytes on 64-bit systems. It does not return the allocated buffer size.

Use:

```c
recv(SClientSocket, arrc_Buffer, CHUNK_SIZE, 0);
```

For a fixed-size array:

```c
char arrc_Buffer[CHUNK_SIZE];

recv(SClientSocket, arrc_Buffer, sizeof(arrc_Buffer), 0);
```

In the second case, `sizeof(arrc_Buffer)` correctly returns the size of the array.

---

## 8. Why did the server fail when reading the binary file after receiving it?

**Answer:**

The most important issue is that a file opened with `"wb"` is write-only.

For example:

```c
fptrFile = fopen("receive.bin", "wb");
```

You cannot use this same stream to read the binary records. Close it and reopen the file in read mode:

```c
fclose(fptrFile);

fptrFile = fopen("receive.bin", "rb");

if (fptrFile == NULL)
{
    fprintf(stderr, "Error opening binary file for reading.\n");
    return FAILURE;
}
```

Then read the records:

```c
S_EMITTER_DATA SData;

while (fread(&SData, sizeof(SData), 1, fptrFile) == 1)
{
    /* Convert SData fields to CSV here. */
}
```

If reading still fails, check that the file contains complete records and that the structure definition matches the original binary format.

---

## 9. Why does `fread()` return an error when reading `S_EMITTER_DATA`?

**Answer:**

This condition:

```c
if (fread(&SData, sizeof(S_EMITTER_DATA), 1, fptrFile) != 1)
```

means that one complete structure was not read.

Possible causes:

1. The file was opened in `"wb"` rather than `"rb"`.
2. The file position is at the end of the file.
3. The file is shorter than one complete structure.
4. The expected number of records is too large.
5. The binary structure definition differs from the original layout.
6. The received file is incomplete or corrupted.

Use this diagnostic:

```c
if (fread(&SData, sizeof(SData), 1, fptrFile) != 1)
{
    if (feof(fptrFile))
        fprintf(stderr, "End of file reached.\n");
    else if (ferror(fptrFile))
        fprintf(stderr, "File read error.\n");

    break;
}
```

Do not treat normal end-of-file as a read error when using a loop to read records until EOF.

---

## 10. Why was the server receiving the wrong file size, such as 602918315 instead of 10486904?

**Answer:**

TCP is a byte stream. It does not preserve the boundaries between separate calls to `send()` and `recv()`.

A single call to:

```c
recv(SClientSocket, (char *)&iFileSize, sizeof(iFileSize), 0);
```

may receive fewer bytes than requested.

The file size must be received completely before interpreting it as an integer.

Use a helper function:

```c
int recv_all(SOCKET sock, char *buffer, int length)
{
    int total = 0;
    int received;

    while (total < length)
    {
        received = recv(
            sock, buffer + total, length - total, 0);

        if (received <= 0)
            return 0;

        total += received;
    }

    return 1;
}
```

On the client, send the complete size field using a corresponding send-all loop. Then transfer the file data.

For a robust protocol, use a fixed-width integer such as `uint64_t` and define the byte order explicitly. Do not assume native `int` or `long` has the same size on every platform.

---

## 11. How should the client send the file size before sending file data?

**Answer:**

The client must send the file size before the file contents, and the server must receive the size before reading the data.

A simple same-platform example:

```c
int iFileSize;

/* Calculate file size before this step. */

int offset = 0;

while (offset < (int)sizeof(iFileSize))
{
    int n = send(
        SClientSocket,
        ((char *)&iFileSize) + offset,
        (int)sizeof(iFileSize) - offset,
        0);

    if (n <= 0)
    {
        fprintf(stderr, "Error sending file size.\n");
        return FAILURE;
    }

    offset += n;
}
```

The server must use `recv_all()` to receive exactly `sizeof(iFileSize)` bytes.

For large files, use a 64-bit file size and a clearly defined wire format. A simple `int` is limited and can overflow for sufficiently large files.

---

## 12. Why did the client get Winsock error 10054?

**Answer:**

Winsock error 10054 means that the connection was reset by the peer.

Common causes in this program include:

- The server exited or crashed.
- The server rejected the incoming data.
- The client and server disagree about the size-header format.
- The server attempted to read or write using invalid file handles.
- The server closed the socket before all file data arrived.

Check the server console and ensure both programs agree on the protocol:

1. Client sends the complete file-size header.
2. Server receives the complete header.
3. Client sends file data in chunks.
4. Server receives exactly the advertised number of bytes.
5. Server converts the completed binary file to CSV.

---

## 13. Why was `fwrite()` error handling incorrect?

**Answer:**

This code is incorrect:

```c
short sBytesWritten = 0;

sBytesWritten = fwrite(arrc_Buffer, iBytesRead, 1, fptrFile);

if (sBytesWritten < 0)
```

`fwrite()` returns the number of elements written, not a negative error code. With a size of `iBytesRead` and a count of `1`, success returns `1`.

Prefer:

```c
size_t sBytesWritten;

sBytesWritten = fwrite(
    arrc_Buffer, 1, (size_t)iBytesRead, fptrFile);

if (sBytesWritten != (size_t)iBytesRead)
{
    fprintf(stderr, "Error writing file.\n");
}
```

This checks the actual number of bytes written.

---

## 14. How do I skip the first 8 bytes of a binary file before reading structures?

**Answer:**

Use:

```c
if (fseek(fptrFile, 8L, SEEK_SET) != 0)
{
    fprintf(stderr, "Error seeking in binary file.\n");
    fclose(fptrFile);
    return FAILURE;
}
```

`SEEK_SET` means the beginning of the file. An offset of 8 moves the file position to byte 8, so the next read starts at the ninth byte.

If the first 8 bytes are a file header, skip them only if the binary format specifies that those bytes are not part of the first `S_EMITTER_DATA` record.

---

## 15. Why might `fread()` fail after skipping 8 bytes?

**Answer:**

Skipping 8 bytes changes the position from which records are read. The remaining file size must be sufficient for the expected records.

```c
fseek(fptrFile, 0, SEEK_END);
long fileSize = ftell(fptrFile);

if (fileSize < 8)
{
    fprintf(stderr, "File is too small.\n");
    fclose(fptrFile);
    return FAILURE;
}

if (fseek(fptrFile, 8L, SEEK_SET) != 0)
{
    fprintf(stderr, "Seek failed.\n");
    fclose(fptrFile);
    return FAILURE;
}

long remainingBytes = fileSize - 8;

if (remainingBytes % (long)sizeof(S_EMITTER_DATA) != 0)
{
    fprintf(stderr,
        "Remaining bytes are not a whole number of records.\n");
}
```

If the file contains only complete `S_EMITTER_DATA` records after the header, then:

```c
numInstances = remainingBytes / sizeof(S_EMITTER_DATA);
```

Use that count to process the records.

---

## 16. How do I convert all fields of nested structures to CSV?

**Answer:**

For each `S_EMITTER_DATA` record, write its basic fields and the nested structure fields.

Example fields:

```c
SData.m_FH_FreqHopSpots.arrfHopFreqMHz[0]
SData.m_FH_FreqHopSpots.ulFreqHopCount

SData.m_PulseAgilityHopSpots.arrfPulseWidth_us[0]
SData.m_PulseAgilityHopSpots.ulPulseAgilityHopCount

SData.m_Cal_Amplitudes.fAmplitude_1_dBm
SData.m_Cal_Amplitudes.fAmplitude_2_dBm
SData.m_Cal_Amplitudes.fAmplitude_3_dBm
SData.m_Cal_Amplitudes.fAmplitude_4_dBm

SData.m_Cal_PhaseDifference.fPhase_12_deg
SData.m_Cal_PhaseDifference.fPhase_23_deg
SData.m_Cal_PhaseDifference.fPhase_34_deg
SData.m_Cal_PhaseDifference.fPhase_14_deg
SData.m_Cal_PhaseDifference.fPhase_13_deg
```

For arrays, use a loop to write every element:

```c
int i;

for (i = 0; i < DP_ESM_FH_MAX_REPORT_COUNT; i++)
{
    fprintf(fptrCSV, "%.2f,",
        SData.m_FH_FreqHopSpots.arrfHopFreqMHz[i]);
}
```

Repeat for the pulse-agility array and other arrays as needed.

The CSV header should contain the same number of columns, in the same order, as the values written for each record.

---

## 17. What should the complete client/server file-transfer workflow look like?

**Answer:**

**Client:**

1. Initialize Winsock.
2. Create and connect the TCP socket.
3. Open the source file in `"rb"` mode.
4. Determine the file size.
5. Send the complete file-size header.
6. Read the file in chunks.
7. Send every byte, handling partial sends.
8. Close the file and socket.

**Server:**

1. Initialize Winsock.
2. Create, bind, and listen on a socket.
3. Accept the client connection.
4. Receive the complete file-size header.
5. Open the destination binary file in `"wb"` mode.
6. Receive chunks until exactly the advertised file size has arrived.
7. Write every received byte to the file.
8. Close the binary file.
9. Reopen it in `"rb"` mode.
10. Skip the header if the format requires it.
11. Read each complete structure and write the fields to CSV.
12. Close the files and sockets.

**Important:** TCP transfers bytes, not C structures. First ensure the received file matches the original binary file byte-for-byte. Only then parse its contents using the correct structure definition.

============================================================
1. READING STRUCTURE DATA FROM A BINARY FILE AND WRITING CSV
============================================================

Q1. How do I read each S_EMITTER_DATA instance from a binary file and write it to a CSV file?

Answer:

S_EMITTER_DATA data;

for (int i = 0; i < NUM_INSTANCES; i++)
{
    if (fread(&data, sizeof(S_EMITTER_DATA), 1, fptrFile) != 1)
    {
        fprintf(stderr, "Error reading binary file.\n");
        fclose(fptrFile);
        fclose(fptrCSV);
        return FAILURE;
    }

    fprintf(fptrCSV,
        "%.2f,%.2f,%.2f,%.2f,%.2f,%hu,%hu,%.2f,%.2f,"
        "%u,%u,%u,%u,%.2f,%hhu,%hhu,%hhu,%hhu,%lu,%lu\n",

        data.fPulseWidth_us,
        data.fDirection_Deg,
        data.fDOA_Deg,
        data.fAmplitude_dBm,
        data.fMinAmplitude_dBm,

        data.usPRI_Type,
        data.usNoOfPRIs,

        data.dFrequency_MHz,
        data.dBandwidth_MHz,

        data.EmitterTOFA.uiSeconds,
        data.EmitterTOFA.uiMilliSeconds,

        data.EmitterTOA.uiSeconds,
        data.EmitterTOA.uiMilliSeconds,

        data.fInstAmplitude_dBm,
        data.ucPulseAgilityFlag,

        data.arr_ucReserved[0],
        data.arr_ucReserved[1],
        data.arr_ucReserved[2],

        data.m_FH_FreqHopSpots.ulFreqHopCount,
        data.m_PulseAgilityHopSpots.ulPulseAgilityHopCount
    );
}


Q2. Why does NUM_INSTANCES produce an undeclared identifier error?

Answer:

NUM_INSTANCES must be declared or defined before it is used.

For example:

#define NUM_INSTANCES 100

Use the actual number of records in your binary file. Do not assume that the file contains 100 records.

Alternatively, calculate the number of complete records from the file size:

long lFileSize;
long lRecordCount;

fseek(fptrFile, 0, SEEK_END);
lFileSize = ftell(fptrFile);

lRecordCount = (lFileSize - 8) / sizeof(S_EMITTER_DATA);

fseek(fptrFile, 8, SEEK_SET);

This calculation assumes the file has an 8-byte header and contains only complete S_EMITTER_DATA records after it.

Verify that the remaining file size is divisible by sizeof(S_EMITTER_DATA).


Q3. Why do errors such as these occur?

EmitterTOFA_Seconds is not a member of S_EMITTER_DATA.
freqHopCount is not a member of S_EMITTER_DATA.

Answer:

The member names used in the code do not match the structure definition.

Use the actual members declared in structure.h:

data.EmitterTOFA.uiSeconds
data.EmitterTOFA.uiMilliSeconds
data.EmitterTOA.uiSeconds
data.EmitterTOA.uiMilliSeconds

data.m_FH_FreqHopSpots.ulFreqHopCount
data.m_PulseAgilityHopSpots.ulPulseAgilityHopCount

Always check structure.h for the exact member names and data types.


============================================================
2. BINARY FILE HEADER: FIRST 8 BYTES
============================================================

Q4. The first 8 bytes of the binary file contain the file header. How do I start reading records after those bytes?

Answer:

if (fseek(fptrFile, 8L, SEEK_SET) != 0)
{
    fprintf(stderr, "Error seeking file position.\n");
    fclose(fptrFile);
    return FAILURE;
}

This moves the file position to byte offset 8.

File layout:

Bytes 0-7: Header
Bytes 8 onward: S_EMITTER_DATA records

Then read the records using fread().

S_EMITTER_DATA data;

if (fread(&data, sizeof(S_EMITTER_DATA), 1, fptrFile) != 1)
{
    fprintf(stderr, "Error reading record.\n");
    return FAILURE;
}

Important: fread() reads the structure using the compiler's structure layout. The sender and receiver must agree on the structure layout, field sizes, packing, and byte order.


============================================================
3. READING AND WRITING INDIVIDUAL STRUCTURE MEMBERS
============================================================

Q5. How do I print every S_EMITTER_DATA member separately?

Answer:

printf("Pulse Width       : %f\n", data.fPulseWidth_us);
printf("Direction         : %f\n", data.fDirection_Deg);
printf("DOA               : %f\n", data.fDOA_Deg);
printf("Amplitude         : %f\n", data.fAmplitude_dBm);
printf("Minimum Amplitude : %f\n", data.fMinAmplitude_dBm);

printf("PRI Type          : %hu\n", data.usPRI_Type);
printf("Number of PRIs    : %hu\n", data.usNoOfPRIs);

printf("Frequency         : %.10f\n", data.dFrequency_MHz);
printf("Bandwidth         : %.10f\n", data.dBandwidth_MHz);

printf("TOFA Seconds      : %u\n",
       data.EmitterTOFA.uiSeconds);

printf("TOFA Milliseconds : %u\n",
       data.EmitterTOFA.uiMilliSeconds);

printf("TOA Seconds       : %u\n",
       data.EmitterTOA.uiSeconds);

printf("TOA Milliseconds  : %u\n",
       data.EmitterTOA.uiMilliSeconds);

printf("Instant Amplitude : %f\n", data.fInstAmplitude_dBm);

printf("Pulse Agility Flag: %u\n", data.ucPulseAgilityFlag);

printf("Reserved 1        : %u\n", data.arr_ucReserved[0]);
printf("Reserved 2        : %u\n", data.arr_ucReserved[1]);
printf("Reserved 3        : %u\n", data.arr_ucReserved[2]);

printf("Frequency Hop Count: %lu\n",
       data.m_FH_FreqHopSpots.ulFreqHopCount);

printf("Pulse Agility Hop Count: %lu\n",
       data.m_PulseAgilityHopSpots.ulPulseAgilityHopCount);

Note:
The format specifier must match the member's actual type. For older Visual C++ versions, use the appropriate format for the declared type.


Q6. How do I write the binary file into a TXT file?

Answer:

FILE *fptrTXT = fopen("output.txt", "w");

if (fptrTXT == NULL)
{
    fprintf(stderr, "Error opening TXT file.\n");
    return FAILURE;
}

S_EMITTER_DATA data;

while (fread(&data, sizeof(S_EMITTER_DATA), 1, fptrFile) == 1)
{
    fprintf(fptrTXT, "Pulse Width: %f\n", data.fPulseWidth_us);
    fprintf(fptrTXT, "Direction: %f\n", data.fDirection_Deg);
    fprintf(fptrTXT, "DOA: %f\n", data.fDOA_Deg);
    fprintf(fptrTXT, "Frequency: %.10f\n", data.dFrequency_MHz);
    fprintf(fptrTXT, "Bandwidth: %.10f\n", data.dBandwidth_MHz);
    fprintf(fptrTXT, "--------------------------------\n");
}

fclose(fptrTXT);

This produces a human-readable text file. It does not preserve the binary representation; it prints interpreted field values.


============================================================
4. TCP CLIENT/SERVER FILE TRANSFER
============================================================

Q7. How do I send a binary file from a TCP client to a server?

Answer:

The general sequence is:

1. Initialize Winsock using WSAStartup().
2. Create a socket.
3. Connect to the server.
4. Open the binary file using fopen(FILE_NAME, "rb").
5. Determine the file size using fseek() and ftell().
6. Send the file size to the server.
7. Read the file in chunks using fread().
8. Send each chunk using send().
9. Close the file and socket.

Example of reading a file in chunks:

while (iTotalBytesSent < iFileSize)
{
    iBytesToSend = min(CHUNK_SIZE,
                       iFileSize - iTotalBytesSent);

    iBytesRead = fread(arrc_Buffer, 1, iBytesToSend, fptrFile);

    if (iBytesRead <= 0)
    {
        fprintf(stderr, "Error reading file.\n");
        break;
    }

    /* send() may send fewer bytes than requested. */
    {
        int iOffset = 0;

        while (iOffset < iBytesRead)
        {
            int iSent = send(
                SClientSocket,
                arrc_Buffer + iOffset,
                iBytesRead - iOffset,
                0
            );

            if (iSent == SOCKET_ERROR || iSent == 0)
            {
                fprintf(stderr, "Error sending file.\n");
                break;
            }

            iOffset += iSent;
            iTotalBytesSent += iSent;
        }

        if (iOffset < iBytesRead)
            break;
    }
}

Important:
- fread() returns the number of items read. With a size of 1, it returns bytes read.
- send() returns bytes sent, not necessarily the full requested amount.
- Reset byte counters for every new file transfer.
- Do not allocate the entire file if you only need a fixed-size transfer buffer.
- Check all socket errors and close resources on every error path.


Q8. Why does the client print "Connected to the server" repeatedly and send the same file repeatedly?

Answer:

Because the connection and file-transfer logic is inside an infinite loop.

After sending the file, the code sets the connection flag back to zero. This causes another connection and another file transfer.

If you want to transfer the file once per successful connection, use this structure:

while (1)
{
    SClientSocket = socket(AF_INET, SOCK_STREAM, 0);

    if (SClientSocket == INVALID_SOCKET)
    {
        Sleep(3000);
        continue;
    }

    if (connect(SClientSocket,
                (struct sockaddr *)&SServerAddr,
                sizeof(SServerAddr)) == SOCKET_ERROR)
    {
        printf("Waiting to connect...\n");
        closesocket(SClientSocket);
        Sleep(3000);
        continue;
    }

    printf("Connected to the server.\n");

    /* Open and transfer the file once here. */

    closesocket(SClientSocket);

    /* Wait before the next connection attempt if needed. */
    Sleep(3000);
}

Reset the file position and byte counters for each transfer. A continuously running client must deliberately decide whether it should resend the same file on every connection or only send newly available data.


Q9. How can the server remain running and accept new clients?

Answer:

Place accept() inside an outer loop:

while (1)
{
    int iClientAddrLen = sizeof(SClientAddr);

    SClientSocket = accept(
        SServerSocket,
        (struct sockaddr *)&SClientAddr,
        &iClientAddrLen
    );

    if (SClientSocket == INVALID_SOCKET)
    {
        printf("Accept failed: %d\n", WSAGetLastError());
        continue;
    }

    printf("Client connected: %s\n",
           inet_ntoa(SClientAddr.sin_addr));

    /*
       Receive and process this client's file.
       Close its file handles.
       Close SClientSocket.
    */

    closesocket(SClientSocket);
}

Keep the listening socket open throughout the loop.

This handles clients sequentially. To serve multiple clients simultaneously, use threads or another suitable concurrency design.


Q10. How do I receive the file size from the client?

Answer:

Do not assume one recv() call will receive all four bytes of an int.

Use a helper that receives exactly the requested number of bytes:

int RecvAll(SOCKET s, char *buffer, int length)
{
    int total = 0;

    while (total < length)
    {
        int received = recv(
            s,
            buffer + total,
            length - total,
            0
        );

        if (received <= 0)
            return 0;

        total += received;
    }

    return 1;
}

Usage:

if (!RecvAll(SClientSocket,
             (char *)&iFileSize,
             sizeof(iFileSize)))
{
    fprintf(stderr, "Error receiving file size.\n");
    return FAILURE;
}

The client must use a matching protocol for the file-size field. For portable protocols, specify the integer width and byte order rather than sending a native int directly.


Q11. How do I receive the complete binary file?

Answer:

Use the announced file size and continue receiving until that number of bytes arrives.

int iTotalBytes = 0;

while (iTotalBytes < iFileSize)
{
    int iRemaining = iFileSize - iTotalBytes;

    int iBytesRead = recv(
        SClientSocket,
        arrc_Buffer,
        min(CHUNK_SIZE, iRemaining),
        0
    );

    if (iBytesRead <= 0)
    {
        fprintf(stderr, "Connection closed or receive error.\n");
        break;
    }

    if (fwrite(arrc_Buffer, 1, iBytesRead, fptrFile)
        != (size_t)iBytesRead)
    {
        fprintf(stderr, "Error writing binary file.\n");
        break;
    }

    iTotalBytes += iBytesRead;
}

Only process the file if iTotalBytes == iFileSize.

Important fixes:
- fopen("receive.bin", "wb") opens the file for writing.
- After receiving, close the writing handle before reopening the file with "rb".
- Check file-open results before calling fclose().
- Use fwrite(buffer, 1, bytesRead, file) to make byte counts straightforward.
- recv() returning zero means the peer closed the connection.
- Validate the received file size before allocating memory or writing data.


============================================================
5. ENDIAN BASICS
============================================================

Q12. What is endianness?

Answer:

Endianness describes the order in which the bytes of a multi-byte value are stored in memory.

Two common formats are:

Little-endian:
Least significant byte (LSB) is stored at the lowest memory address.

Big-endian:
Most significant byte (MSB) is stored at the lowest memory address.


Q13. Give an example of little-endian and big-endian.

Answer:

Consider a 32-bit integer:

unsigned int value = 0x12345678;

On a little-endian system, its bytes in increasing memory-address order are:

Address     Byte
0x1000      0x78
0x1001      0x56
0x1002      0x34
0x1003      0x12

On a big-endian system:

Address     Byte
0x1000      0x12
0x1001      0x34
0x1002      0x56
0x1003      0x78

The numeric value is the same when interpreted correctly. Only the byte order differs.


Q14. How do I check whether a machine is little-endian or big-endian?

Answer:

unsigned int value = 1;

if (*(unsigned char *)&value == 1)
{
    printf("Little-endian\n");
}
else
{
    printf("Big-endian\n");
}

Explanation:

The integer value 1 has the representation 0x00000001 on a typical 32-bit unsigned int.

If the first byte in memory is 0x01, the system is little-endian.

If the first byte in memory is 0x00, the system is big-endian.

This check assumes the integer has more than one byte and the system uses the usual binary integer representation.


Q15. What does if (*(char *)&num == 1) mean?

Answer:

It examines the first byte in memory occupied by num.

Example:

int num = 1;

if (*(char *)&num == 1)
{
    printf("Little-endian\n");
}

(char *)&num converts the address of num to a byte pointer.

*(char *)&num reads the first byte.

The expression checks the byte order, not the numeric value of the integer itself.


Q16. How can I check the byte order of a double?

Answer:

double value = 12.45;
unsigned char *bytes = (unsigned char *)&value;
int i;

printf("Double bytes in memory:\n");

for (i = 0; i < sizeof(value); i++)
{
    printf("%02X ", bytes[i]);
}

printf("\n");

This displays the actual bytes of the double in memory.

It is more reliable than trying to determine endianness by looking at the printed floating-point value.

For a typical IEEE-754 double on a little-endian machine, 12.45 is stored with bytes in reverse order compared with its big-endian representation. The exact representation depends on the floating-point format and architecture.


============================================================
6. WHY ENDIAN CONVERSION IS NEEDED
============================================================

Q17. Why do we need endian conversion?

Answer:

Endian conversion is needed when data is stored or transmitted in one byte order but interpreted on a system that expects another.

Example:

A little-endian processor stores a 32-bit integer as:

78 56 34 12

A protocol requires big-endian bytes:

12 34 56 78

The sender or receiver must arrange the bytes according to the protocol.

If both sides already agree on the same byte order, no conversion is needed.


Q18. Does DoubleEndianSwap convert little-endian to big-endian or big-endian to little-endian?

Answer:

Your function uses htonll() on Windows and htobe64() on other platforms.

These are intended to convert a 64-bit value from host byte order to big-endian byte order.

Therefore:

- On a little-endian host, the bytes are reversed to produce big-endian order.
- On a big-endian host, no byte reversal is necessary.

However, the implementation must be correct for the target platform.

Also, the function as written changes the bit pattern of the double and then interprets the changed bits as another double. The resulting floating-point number will usually be meaningless.

For serialization, preserve the bit pattern as bytes. When decoding, reverse the conversion before interpreting those bytes as a double.


Q19. Why is little-endian common in microcontrollers and embedded systems?

Answer:

Many common embedded processor families, including ARM Cortex-M, support or commonly use little-endian operation. Other architectures may use different or configurable byte orders.

Little-endian is not inherently faster for every operation. Processor architecture, instruction set, memory system, and compiler determine performance.

Example:

A 32-bit sensor value is:

0x12345678

On a little-endian microcontroller, memory contains:

78 56 34 12

The program can read the entire 32-bit value with a normal 32-bit load when the memory is correctly aligned and the hardware supports it.

If the value must be sent using a big-endian protocol, the software must arrange the bytes accordingly.


Q20. Can memory be read in bits or bytes?

Answer:

Software can inspect individual bits using bitwise operations and access bytes using byte-sized types.

Example of checking a bit:

unsigned char flags = 0x05;

if (flags & 0x01)
{
    printf("Bit 0 is set\n");
}

Example of accessing individual bytes:

unsigned int value = 0x12345678;
unsigned char *bytes = (unsigned char *)&value;

printf("%02X\n", bytes[0]);

The byte order displayed depends on the machine.

Processors generally access memory using supported byte, halfword, word, or larger loads and stores. Individual bits are usually extracted or modified using masks and shifts rather than ordinary C/C++ memory loads.


Q21. Why doesn't endian conversion apply to a one-byte value?

Answer:

A single byte has no internal byte order to reverse.

For example:

unsigned char value = 0xAB;

The byte is simply:

AB

There is no second byte to exchange it with.

Endian conversion matters when the representation contains multiple bytes, such as 16-bit integers, 32-bit integers, 64-bit integers, floats, and doubles.

This does not mean every multi-byte type needs conversion. Conversion is required only when the source and destination byte-order requirements differ.


============================================================
7. FLOAT AND DOUBLE ENDIAN CONVERSION
============================================================

Q22. How do I convert a 32-bit integer to network byte order?

Answer:

unsigned long value = 0x12345678;
unsigned long networkValue = htonl(value);

On Windows, include the appropriate Winsock header and link Ws2_32.lib.

For 16-bit integers, use htons().

For incoming network values, use ntohl() and ntohs().

These functions are for integer values. For floating-point values, preserve the float's bit representation and convert the bytes explicitly.


Q23. How do I convert a 64-bit double's byte order safely in old Visual C++?

Answer:

For an IEEE-754 double stored in eight bytes, a byte-reversal helper can be used when the protocol explicitly requires reversed byte order.

#include <string.h>

void ReverseDoubleBytes(double input, double *output)
{
    unsigned char inputBytes[8];
    unsigned char outputBytes[8];
    int i;

    if (output == NULL)
        return;

    if (sizeof(double) != 8)
        return;

    memcpy(inputBytes, &input, 8);

    for (i = 0; i < 8; i++)
    {
        outputBytes[i] = inputBytes[7 - i];
    }

    memcpy(output, outputBytes, 8);
}

This reverses the eight bytes. It is appropriate only when the source and destination formats require opposite byte order and the double representations are compatible.

For a real communication protocol, preferably encode the floating-point value into a byte buffer and explicitly define the wire format.


Q24. Why did the earlier DoubleEndianSwap code print an extremely large number?

Answer:

The code changed the double's 64-bit representation and then interpreted the swapped bits as a new double.

For example, 12.45 has a particular IEEE-754 representation. Reversing those bytes can produce a bit pattern representing a completely different number, possibly an extremely large value, a tiny value, infinity, or NaN.

Endian conversion changes the byte representation, not the mathematical value.

The correct workflow is:

1. Obtain the original floating-point bit pattern.
2. Convert its bytes for transmission or storage.
3. On the receiving side, convert the bytes back to the host's expected order.
4. Interpret the restored bit pattern as a floating-point value.

Do not print a byte-swapped double and expect it to display the original mathematical number.


Q25. Why did the earlier float code print 0.000000?

Answer:

The code used:

*(unsigned long *)&fScanRate_rpm =
    htonl(*(unsigned long *)&fScanRate_rpm);

This assumes unsigned long has the same size as float and accesses a float through an incompatible pointer type.

Those assumptions are not portable and can cause incorrect results.

For a 32-bit float, use a 32-bit integer type with the same size and memcpy() to preserve the bit pattern, or reverse the bytes in a byte buffer.

Example for reversing four bytes:

void ReverseFloatBytes(float input, float *output)
{
    unsigned char inBytes[4];
    unsigned char outBytes[4];
    int i;

    if (output == NULL || sizeof(float) != 4)
        return;

    memcpy(inBytes, &input, 4);

    for (i = 0; i < 4; i++)
        outBytes[i] = inBytes[3 - i];

    memcpy(output, outBytes, 4);
}

Again, reverse bytes only if the source and destination byte orders require it.


Q26. Why did uint64_t and htonll produce compilation errors in older Visual C++?

Answer:

Older Visual C++ versions may not provide stdint.h or a built-in htonll() function.

For older Windows compilers, __int64 is a supported 64-bit integer type.

A Windows-specific 64-bit host-to-network helper can be defined as follows:

#include <Winsock2.h>

__int64 htonll(__int64 value)
{
    unsigned __int64 u = (unsigned __int64)value;

    return (__int64)(
        (((unsigned __int64)htonl((unsigned long)(u & 0xFFFFFFFF)))
          << 32)
        |
        htonl((unsigned long)(u >> 32))
    );
}

This implementation assumes a little-endian Windows host, as is typical for the target environment. If portability to big-endian systems is needed, detect host byte order and convert conditionally.

Include Winsock headers in the correct order and link Ws2_32.lib.

The key point: htonll() is not a standard Winsock function available automatically on every Windows compiler. You must define or use a suitable implementation.


============================================================
8. CHECKING FREQUENCY AND OTHER STRUCTURE MEMBERS
============================================================

Q27. The Frequency_MHz values in the CSV look incorrect. How do I check whether endian conversion is required?

Answer:

First, inspect the raw bytes in the binary file.

S_EMITTER_DATA data;
unsigned char *bytes;
int i;

if (fread(&data, sizeof(data), 1, fptrFile) == 1)
{
    bytes = (unsigned char *)&data.dFrequency_MHz;

    printf("Frequency bytes: ");

    for (i = 0; i < sizeof(data.dFrequency_MHz); i++)
    {
        printf("%02X ", bytes[i]);
    }

    printf("\n");

    printf("Frequency interpreted as double: %.10f\n",
           data.dFrequency_MHz);
}

If the interpreted value is wrong, possible causes include:

- Wrong byte order.
- Incorrect file offset or record size.
- A structure-layout or packing mismatch.
- Incorrect field type.
- Data written using a different floating-point representation.
- Corrupted or incomplete file transfer.

Do not apply endian conversion to every field blindly. Determine the actual file format and byte order first.


Q28. How do I check all fields in S_EMITTER_DATA for possible endian issues?

Answer:

Check the fields by type and size:

- float members: usually 4 bytes.
- double members: usually 8 bytes.
- unsigned short members: usually 2 bytes.
- unsigned integer members: commonly 4 bytes, but verify.
- unsigned long members: compiler and platform dependent.
- unsigned char members: 1 byte.

For example:

printf("Pulse Width: %f\n", data.fPulseWidth_us);
printf("Direction: %f\n", data.fDirection_Deg);
printf("DOA: %f\n", data.fDOA_Deg);
printf("Amplitude: %f\n", data.fAmplitude_dBm);
printf("Frequency: %.10f\n", data.dFrequency_MHz);
printf("Bandwidth: %.10f\n", data.dBandwidth_MHz);
printf("PRI Type: %hu\n", data.usPRI_Type);
printf("Number of PRIs: %hu\n", data.usNoOfPRIs);
printf("TOFA Seconds: %u\n", data.EmitterTOFA.uiSeconds);
printf("TOA Seconds: %u\n", data.EmitterTOA.uiSeconds);

Check the actual types in structure.h before choosing format specifiers or conversion functions.

For a portable file format, convert every multi-byte field according to the format specification instead of dumping the native structure directly.


Q29. Why were the binary values and CSV values different?

Answer:

The binary file stores raw bytes. The CSV file stores textual representations of interpreted values.

Differences can result from:

1. Endian mismatch.
2. Incorrect structure definition.
3. Reading the wrong byte offset.
4. Incorrect NUM_INSTANCES.
5. Floating-point rounding from formatting such as %.2f.
6. Reading a file before all bytes have arrived.
7. Applying an unnecessary endian conversion.
8. Incorrect field order or format specifiers.

For example, %.2f prints only two digits after the decimal point. Use %.10f for more precision while debugging.

First verify the raw bytes, file size, header, and structure layout. Apply endian conversion only when the actual binary format requires it.


============================================================
9. IMPORTANT SOCKET AND FILE-HANDLING FIXES
============================================================

Q30. Why is sizeof(arrc_Buffer) wrong for a dynamically allocated buffer?

Answer:

If arrc_Buffer is declared as:

char *arrc_Buffer;

then:

sizeof(arrc_Buffer)

returns the size of the pointer, usually 4 bytes in a 32-bit program or 8 bytes in a 64-bit program.

It does not return the size of the allocated memory.

Use an explicit buffer length:

recv(SClientSocket, arrc_Buffer, CHUNK_SIZE, 0);

The requested length must not exceed the actual allocated buffer size.


Q31. Why is this incorrect?

if (iBytesRead < 0)

after fread().

Answer:

fread() returns the number of items successfully read. It does not return a negative number on error.

For a single structure:

if (fread(&data, sizeof(data), 1, fptrFile) != 1)
{
    fprintf(stderr, "Error reading structure.\n");
}

For byte-oriented reads:

size_t bytesRead = fread(buffer, 1, bufferSize, fptrFile);

if (bytesRead < bufferSize)
{
    if (ferror(fptrFile))
        fprintf(stderr, "File read error.\n");
    else if (feof(fptrFile))
        printf("End of file reached.\n");
}


Q32. Why is this incorrect?

if (sBytesWritten < 0)

after fwrite().

Answer:

fwrite() returns the number of items written, not a negative error code.

Use:

if (fwrite(buffer, 1, bytesToWrite, fptrFile)
    != (size_t)bytesToWrite)
{
    fprintf(stderr, "Error writing file.\n");
}


Q33. Why should I not call fclose() on a NULL file pointer?

Answer:

If fopen() fails, the pointer is NULL. Do not call fclose() on it.

Use:

fptrFile = fopen("receive.bin", "wb");

if (fptrFile == NULL)
{
    fprintf(stderr, "Cannot open receive.bin.\n");
    return FAILURE;
}

Only close file handles that were successfully opened.


Q34. What is the correct general sequence for the server?

Answer:

1. Call WSAStartup().
2. Create the listening socket.
3. Configure the server address.
4. Call bind().
5. Call listen().
6. Enter an accept() loop.
7. Receive the file size.
8. Receive exactly that many bytes.
9. Save the binary file.
10. Close the binary output file.
11. Reopen it for reading.
12. Skip the 8-byte header.
13. Read the records and write the CSV.
14. Close the files and client socket.
15. Return to accept() for the next client.
16. Call closesocket() on the listening socket and WSACleanup() when shutting down.


============================================================
10. KEY TAKEAWAYS
============================================================

Q35. What should I remember about endian conversion?

Answer:

- Endianness describes the order of bytes in a multi-byte value.
- Little-endian stores the least significant byte at the lowest memory address.
- Big-endian stores the most significant byte at the lowest memory address.
- htons() converts a 16-bit integer to network byte order.
- htonl() converts a 32-bit integer to network byte order.
- htonll() is not universally provided by Windows; an appropriate helper may be required.
- A single-byte value has no byte order to reverse.
- Float and double values require preservation of their bit patterns during serialization.
- Reversing a double's bytes and immediately printing it as a double generally produces a different numerical value.
- Never convert all fields without checking the source format.
- TCP is a byte stream. A single send() or recv() call does not guarantee that the entire requested amount is transferred.
- The sender and receiver must agree on file headers, record counts, field order, field sizes, packing, and byte order.
- When possible, define an explicit binary protocol instead of transmitting a native C/C++ structure directly.

C/C++ PROGRAMMING NOTES: ENDIAN CONVERSION, STRUCTURES, TCP SOCKETS, BINARY FILES AND CSV

============================================================
1. htons() — WHAT IS IT?
============================================================

Q: What is htons()?

A: htons() means Host TO Network Short. It converts a 16-bit integer from host byte order to network byte order.

Declaration:

u_short htons(u_short hostshort);

Example:

unsigned short usValue = 20;
unsigned short usConverted = htons(usValue);

printf("%hu\n", usConverted);

On a little-endian Windows machine, 20 is stored as:

Host byte order:
14 00

Network byte order:
00 14

After htons(), the returned value is interpreted using the host's byte order, so printing the converted value numerically may produce a different number, such as 5120.

Important: htons() returns the converted value. It does not modify the original variable unless the return value is assigned.

Correct:

usValue = htons(usValue);

This does not modify usValue:

htons(usValue);


============================================================
2. htonl() — 32-BIT INTEGER CONVERSION
============================================================

Q: What is htonl()?

A: htonl() means Host TO Network Long. It converts a 32-bit integer to network byte order.

Example:

unsigned long ulValue = 256;
ulValue = htonl(ulValue);

On a little-endian machine, the 32-bit value 256 is represented in host memory as:

00 01 00 00

Its network byte order is:

00 00 01 00

For a Windows Winsock program, use htonl() for 32-bit integer fields. Do not assume that the C/C++ type long is always 32 bits on every platform.

Q: What about ordinary int?

A: On typical Windows systems, int is 32 bits, so htonl() can be used for an int after appropriate type handling. Prefer explicitly sized integer types such as uint32_t when defining binary protocols.


============================================================
3. HTONF() — FLOAT ENDIAN CONVERSION
============================================================

Q: How can a float be converted to network byte order?

A: Winsock does not provide a standard htonf() function. A macro can reinterpret the four bytes of a float as a 32-bit integer and swap them using htonl().

Example macro:

#define htonf(fData) \
    (*(uint32_t *)&(fData) = htonl(*(uint32_t *)&(fData)))

Example usage:

float fValue = 500.33f;
htonf(fValue);

However, this macro uses pointer type-punning, which can cause alignment or aliasing problems in some C/C++ implementations. A safer implementation for IEEE-754 32-bit floats is:

#include <stdint.h>
#include <string.h>
#include <winsock2.h>

static void FloatToNetworkBytes(float value, unsigned char output[4])
{
    uint32_t bits;

    memcpy(&bits, &value, sizeof(bits));
    bits = htonl(bits);
    memcpy(output, &bits, sizeof(bits));
}

This produces the network-order bytes without modifying the original float variable.

Important: Network byte order is a byte representation. Do not convert a float to network order and then print it as if it were an ordinary float. The bytes need to be converted back before using %.2f.


============================================================
4. WHY HTONF() AND ASSIGNMENT CAN GIVE DIFFERENT RESULTS
============================================================

Q: What is the difference between these statements?

htonf(pSEmtr->fPulseWidth_us);

pSEmtr->fPulseWidth_us = htonf(pSEmtr->fPulseWidth_us);

A: With the macro:

#define htonf(fData) \
    (*(unsigned int *)&fData = htonl(*(unsigned int *)&fData))

The macro is an assignment expression and produces the assigned integer value. It is not a proper float-returning function.

The first statement modifies the float's bytes in place.

The second statement additionally assigns the macro's integer result to the float variable. This can corrupt the value because the integer result is converted numerically to float.

Use the first form if you retain the macro, but preferably replace it with a byte-conversion function that operates on a separate output buffer.

Q: Does pSEmtr->fDOA_Deg pass an address or a value?

A: The expression pSEmtr->fDOA_Deg accesses the member's value. The macro can take its address internally using &fData.

Q: Can htons() or htonl() accept a pointer?

A: No. Their normal declarations accept integer values, not pointers.

Correct:

pSEmtr->usPRI_Type = htons(pSEmtr->usPRI_Type);

Incorrect:

htons(&pSEmtr->usPRI_Type);


============================================================
5. HTONLL() — 64-BIT INTEGER CONVERSION
============================================================

Q: How can a 64-bit integer be converted?

A: Winsock does not provide a universally available standard htonll() function. A common Windows implementation is:

#ifdef WIN32

__int64 htonll(__int64 n)
{
#if defined(_M_IX86) || defined(_M_X64)
    return (((__int64)htonl((unsigned long)n)) << 32)
           | htonl((unsigned long)(n >> 32));
#else
    return n;
#endif
}

#endif

Note: Verify the architecture and compiler conditions before using this implementation. For portable code, convert the 64-bit integer into eight explicitly ordered bytes.

For double-precision floating-point data, convert its 64-bit representation rather than applying an integer conversion directly to the numerical double value.


============================================================
6. DOUBLE ENDIAN CONVERSION
============================================================

Q: How can a double be converted?

A: A double is commonly 64 bits on Windows and Linux. Its bit representation can be copied into a uint64_t, converted, and copied back.

Example:

#include <stdint.h>
#include <string.h>

static void DoubleToNetworkBytes(double value, unsigned char output[8])
{
    uint64_t bits;
    int i;

    memcpy(&bits, &value, sizeof(bits));

    for (i = 0; i < 8; ++i)
    {
        output[i] = (unsigned char)(bits >> (56 - 8 * i));
    }
}

This explicitly generates the eight network-order bytes for the usual IEEE-754 double representation on a little-endian host.

For binary file protocols, a more reliable design is to serialize each field explicitly instead of writing an entire native structure directly.


============================================================
7. WHY NESTED STRUCTURE MEMBERS WORK WITH HTONS()
============================================================

Q: Can htons() be used with a nested structure member?

A: Yes. Access the member through the nested structure and assign the return value.

Example:

pSEmtr->EmitterParameters.usRFCarrier_Freq_Type =
    htons(pSEmtr->EmitterParameters.usRFCarrier_Freq_Type);

For another nested member:

pSEmtr->m_Cal_Amplitudes.fAmplitude_1_dBm

The member can be accessed using the pointer and dot operators as appropriate.

Example:

htonf(pSEmtr->m_Cal_Amplitudes.fAmplitude_1_dBm);

This works with the earlier macro because the macro takes the member's address internally. However, use a safe float-byte conversion routine for production code.


============================================================
8. STRUCTURE VARIABLE VS STRUCTURE POINTER
============================================================

Q: What is the difference?

Structure variable:

S_EMITTER_DATA SData;

Access:

SData.usPRI_Type = 10;

Structure pointer:

S_EMITTER_DATA *pSEmtr = &SData;

Access:

pSEmtr->usPRI_Type = 10;

The arrow operator is equivalent to:

(*pSEmtr).usPRI_Type = 10;

Q: Which changes reflect in the original structure?

A: If pSEmtr points to SData, modifying pSEmtr->usPRI_Type modifies SData.usPRI_Type.

Q: What happens if a structure is passed by value to a function?

A: The function receives a copy. Changes to that copy do not change the caller's structure.

Q: What happens if a pointer to the structure is passed?

A: The function can modify the original structure through the pointer.


============================================================
9. WINSOCK HTONS DECLARATIONS
============================================================

Q: What does this declaration mean?

#if INCL_WINSOCK_API_PROTOTYPES
WINSOCK_API_LINKAGE
u_short
WSAAPI
htons(u_short hostshort);
#endif

A: This declares the actual htons() function prototype, allowing the compiler to check calls to the function.

Q: What does this declaration mean?

#if INCL_WINSOCK_API_TYPEDEFS
typedef
u_short
(WSAAPI * LPFN_HTONS)(u_short hostshort);
#endif

A: This defines LPFN_HTONS as a function-pointer type. A variable of this type can hold a pointer to a compatible function.

Example:

LPFN_HTONS pfnHtons = htons;

unsigned short result = pfnHtons(20);

Normally, simply calling htons(20) is sufficient. You do not need a function pointer unless you specifically want to call the function through one.


============================================================
10. ENDIAN CONVERSION EXAMPLES
============================================================

Q: What is the byte representation of 20 as a 16-bit integer?

Decimal: 20
Hexadecimal: 0x0014

Little-endian bytes:
14 00

Network-order bytes:
00 14

Q: What is the byte representation of 256?

Decimal: 256
Hexadecimal: 0x0100

Little-endian bytes:
00 01

Network-order bytes:
01 00

Q: What is the byte representation of 257?

Decimal: 257
Hexadecimal: 0x0101

Little-endian bytes:
01 01

Network-order bytes:
01 01

The bytes are identical because both bytes are 0x01.

Q: What is 25314 in hexadecimal?

25314 decimal = 0x62E2

Little-endian bytes:
E2 62

Network-order bytes:
62 E2

Q: What is 0x44D19447 in decimal?

0x44D19447 = 1,155,211,335 as an unsigned 32-bit integer.

Important: If these bits represent a float, interpret them as a floating-point bit pattern instead of as an ordinary integer.

Q: What is the network-order representation of float 500.33?

A: The IEEE-754 single-precision representation is approximately 0x43FA2A3D (subject to the exact float rounding). The corresponding network-order bytes are:

43 FA 2A 3D

To send a float, send these bytes; to recover the float, reverse the byte-to-bit conversion on the receiving machine.


============================================================
11. FILE TRANSFER USING TCP SOCKETS
============================================================

Q: How does the client send a file to the server?

A: The client opens the file in binary mode, calculates its size, sends the size, reads the file in chunks, and sends those chunks over the TCP connection.

Example core logic:

FILE *fptrFile = NULL;
char filename[260];
long fileSize;

printf("Enter the file name: ");

if (fgets(filename, sizeof(filename), stdin) == NULL)
{
    printf("Unable to read file name.\n");
    return FAILURE;
}

/* Remove the newline. */
filename[strcspn(filename, "\r\n")] = '\0';

if (filename[0] == '\0')
{
    printf("File name cannot be empty.\n");
    return FAILURE;
}

fptrFile = fopen(filename, "rb");

if (fptrFile == NULL)
{
    printf("Unable to open file.\n");
    return FAILURE;
}

if (fseek(fptrFile, 0, SEEK_END) != 0)
{
    fclose(fptrFile);
    return FAILURE;
}

fileSize = ftell(fptrFile);

if (fileSize < 0)
{
    fclose(fptrFile);
    return FAILURE;
}

if (fseek(fptrFile, 0, SEEK_SET) != 0)
{
    fclose(fptrFile);
    return FAILURE;
}

printf("File size: %ld bytes\n", fileSize);

/* Continue by sending the size and file data. */

fclose(fptrFile);

Note: This is the core file-validation logic, not a complete socket transfer function.


============================================================
12. WHY THE CLIENT MAY NOT READ THE FILE CORRECTLY
============================================================

Q: The client reports that it sent 192 bytes. Why might the server receive garbage?

A: Several bugs in the original transfer logic can cause this.

1. send() may send fewer bytes than requested. Loop until every byte has been sent.
2. recv() may receive fewer bytes than requested. Loop until the expected amount is received.
3. The file size must be communicated consistently by client and server.
4. A zero-byte file must not trigger a receive loop that waits for data.
5. Check fread() for zero and ferror(), not only for negative values.
6. Do not increment the total bytes sent by the requested amount unless that many bytes were actually sent.
7. Check the file format and expected record size before converting binary data into a structure.
8. Ensure the server and client agree on the byte order used for the file size.

Example: correct send-all helper for Winsock.

int SendAll(SOCKET s, const char *buffer, int length)
{
    int total = 0;

    while (total < length)
    {
        int n = send(s, buffer + total, length - total, 0);

        if (n == SOCKET_ERROR || n == 0)
        {
            return FAILURE;
        }

        total += n;
    }

    return SUCCESS;
}

Example: correct receive-all helper.

int RecvAll(SOCKET s, char *buffer, int length)
{
    int total = 0;

    while (total < length)
    {
        int n = recv(s, buffer + total, length - total, 0);

        if (n == SOCKET_ERROR || n == 0)
        {
            return FAILURE;
        }

        total += n;
    }

    return SUCCESS;
}

For a robust protocol, transmit the file size in a defined byte order and convert it on the receiving side.


============================================================
13. WHY A SMALL FILE MAY PRODUCE GARBAGE IN CSV
============================================================

Q: The file size is 1 KB, but there is no meaningful data. Why does the CSV contain garbage?

A: A non-zero file size does not guarantee that the file contains a valid binary record.

The server's CSV code expects the file to contain complete S_EMITTER_DATA records. If it reads arbitrary bytes or an incomplete record into the structure, its fields may contain meaningless values.

For example:

if (fread(pSEmtr, sizeof(S_EMITTER_DATA), 1, fptrFile) != 1)
{
    fprintf(stderr, "Incomplete or invalid binary record.\n");
    free(pSEmtr);
    return FAILURE;
}

However, this check only ensures that a complete record was read. It does not prove that the bytes represent a valid record.

Check the file format, header, record count, record size, and any protocol version before processing it.


============================================================
14. FILE SIZE VALIDATION
============================================================

Q: How can the server validate the binary file size?

A: Determine the file size and verify that the data after the header contains an exact number of complete records.

Example:

long fileSize;
long headerSize = 8;
long payloadSize;

if (fseek(fptrFile, 0, SEEK_END) != 0)
{
    return FAILURE;
}

fileSize = ftell(fptrFile);

if (fileSize < 0)
{
    return FAILURE;
}

if (fileSize < headerSize)
{
    fprintf(stderr, "Invalid file: header is incomplete.\n");
    return FAILURE;
}

payloadSize = fileSize - headerSize;

if (payloadSize == 0)
{
    printf("File has a header but no records.\n");
    return SUCCESS;
}

if (payloadSize % sizeof(S_EMITTER_DATA) != 0)
{
    fprintf(stderr, "Invalid file: incomplete binary record.\n");
    return FAILURE;
}

if (fseek(fptrFile, headerSize, SEEK_SET) != 0)
{
    return FAILURE;
}

The header size and record size must match the actual binary file format. Native structure sizes may differ between compiler settings, so a portable format should define its field sizes and layout explicitly.


============================================================
15. SERVER-SIDE EMPTY FILE HANDLING
============================================================

Q: How can the server avoid writing garbage into CSV for an empty file?

A: Validate the received file size before attempting to parse any records.

Example:

if (iFileSize < 0)
{
    fprintf(stderr, "Invalid file size.\n");
    return FAILURE;
}

if (iFileSize == 0)
{
    printf("Received an empty file.\n");

    /* Create an empty output CSV, or write only its header
       if that is the required application behavior. */

    return SUCCESS;
}

If the received file contains only a header and no records, skip the record-reading loop and do not write record values.

Important: A file that contains 1 KB of zeros is not an empty file. It contains 1 KB of bytes, but those bytes may not represent valid records.


============================================================
16. BUFFER SIZE — 1 KB FILE AND 10 MB BUFFER
============================================================

Q: If the buffer size is 10 MB, can it handle a 1 KB file?

A: Yes. A buffer larger than the file can handle the file, provided the code reads only the actual remaining number of bytes.

Example:

#define BUFFER_SIZE (10 * 1024 * 1024)

char *buffer = (char *)malloc(BUFFER_SIZE);

if (buffer == NULL)
{
    return FAILURE;
}

size_t bytesRead = fread(buffer, 1, BUFFER_SIZE, fptrFile);

A 1 KB file will normally produce a bytesRead value of 1024.

The buffer size does not make invalid binary data valid. It also does not determine how many structure records are present.


============================================================
17. FILE EXTENSION VALIDATION — .BIN AND .TXT
============================================================

Q: How can the client allow only .bin and .txt files?

A: Validate the extension and then check whether the file can be opened.

Example:

#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>
#include <ctype.h>

int IsValidExtension(const char *filename)
{
    const char *dot = strrchr(filename, '.');

    if (dot == NULL)
    {
        return 0;
    }

    if (_stricmp(dot, ".bin") == 0 ||
        _stricmp(dot, ".txt") == 0)
    {
        return 1;
    }

    return 0;
}

int main(void)
{
    char filename[260];
    FILE *file;

    printf("Enter file name: ");

    if (fgets(filename, sizeof(filename), stdin) == NULL)
    {
        return 1;
    }

    filename[strcspn(filename, "\r\n")] = '\0';

    if (filename[0] == '\0')
    {
        printf("File name cannot be empty.\n");
        return 1;
    }

    if (!IsValidExtension(filename))
    {
        printf("Only .bin and .txt files are allowed.\n");
        return 1;
    }

    file = fopen(filename, "rb");

    if (file == NULL)
    {
        printf("File does not exist or cannot be opened.\n");
        return 1;
    }

    printf("File is accessible.\n");

    fclose(file);
    return 0;
}

Note: _stricmp() is available in Microsoft Visual C++. On other platforms, use the appropriate case-insensitive comparison function.

Extension validation does not verify the actual content or file format. A file named data.bin may still contain invalid data.


============================================================
18. FILE HANDLING ERRORS IN THE ORIGINAL CODE
============================================================

Q: Why is fclose(fptrFile) incorrect when fopen() has failed?

A: If fopen() returns NULL, there is no valid file stream to close.

Incorrect:

fptrFile = fopen(filename, "rb");

if (fptrFile == NULL)
{
    fclose(fptrFile);
    return FAILURE;
}

Correct:

fptrFile = fopen(filename, "rb");

if (fptrFile == NULL)
{
    fprintf(stderr, "Unable to open file.\n");
    return FAILURE;
}

Q: Why should the file not be opened again without closing the previous stream?

A: Opening the same file again overwrites the pointer if the previous stream is not closed. This can leak the original stream.

Correct sequence:

1. Open receive.bin using "wb".
2. Receive and write the bytes.
3. Close the write stream.
4. Reopen receive.bin using "rb".
5. Seek to the correct header offset.
6. Read and validate records.
7. Write CSV data.
8. Close the read stream and CSV stream.


============================================================
19. FREAD() AND FWRITE() CORRECT USAGE
============================================================

Q: How should fread() be checked?

A:

size_t count = fread(pSEmtr, sizeof(*pSEmtr), 1, fptrFile);

if (count != 1)
{
    if (ferror(fptrFile))
    {
        fprintf(stderr, "File read error.\n");
    }
    else
    {
        fprintf(stderr, "End of file or incomplete record.\n");
    }

    return FAILURE;
}

Q: How should fwrite() be checked?

A:

size_t count = fwrite(buffer, 1, bytesRead, fptrFile);

if (count != bytesRead)
{
    fprintf(stderr, "File write error.\n");
    return FAILURE;
}

When using fwrite(buffer, bytesRead, 1, file), its return value is the number of complete elements written, not the number of bytes. For this reason, comparing it directly with bytesRead is incorrect.


============================================================
20. IMPORTANT CSV CONVERSION RULE
============================================================

Q: Should endian conversion be performed before writing values with fprintf()?

A: No, not if the purpose is to print the original numeric values in human-readable CSV format.

If the binary file stores fields in network byte order, first convert each field back into host byte order. Then pass the resulting numeric values to fprintf().

For example:

pSEmtr->usPRI_Type = ntohs(pSEmtr->usPRI_Type);
pSEmtr->m_FH_FreqHopSpots.ulFreqHopCount =
    ntohl(pSEmtr->m_FH_FreqHopSpots.ulFreqHopCount);

For a float or double, reverse the byte conversion into a separate byte buffer and reconstruct the original floating-point value.

Then print:

fprintf(fptrCSV, "%hu,%.2f,%lu\n",
        pSEmtr->usPRI_Type,
        pSEmtr->fPulseWidth_us,
        pSEmtr->m_FH_FreqHopSpots.ulFreqHopCount);

Use the correct format specifiers for the exact types in the structure. For example, %hu is for unsigned short after integer promotion, and %.2f is used for a double argument (a float is promoted to double in a variadic call).


============================================================
21. SUMMARY OF THE MOST IMPORTANT POINTS
============================================================

1. htons() converts a 16-bit integer value to network byte order.
2. htonl() converts a 32-bit integer value to network byte order.
3. Winsock does not provide a standard htonf() function.
4. A nested structure member can be accessed using -> and . operators.
5. A pointer lets a function modify the original structure.
6. send() and recv() may process fewer bytes than requested.
7. Always loop until all expected bytes have been sent or received.
8. Validate the received file size before parsing the binary file.
9. A non-zero file size does not guarantee valid records.
10. Check that the payload contains complete records before converting to CSV.
11. A 10 MB buffer can handle a 1 KB file, but it cannot fix invalid data.
12. Validate the extension and check fopen() for NULL.
13. Never call fclose() on a NULL file pointer.
14. Check fread() and fwrite() return values correctly.
15. Convert network-order fields back to host order before printing them as numeric CSV values.
16. Avoid blindly reading an entire C/C++ structure from a binary file unless the file format explicitly guarantees the same layout, field sizes, padding, and byte order.
17. Define and validate the binary header, payload length, record count, and protocol version to prevent garbage values.
# C PROGRAMMING: FILE VALIDATION, BINARY FILE HANDLING, TCP WINSOCK CLIENT-SERVER, AND CSV CONVERSION

## 1. How to validate a filename extension in C?

**Question:** How can we validate whether a filename has a `.bin` or `.txt` extension?

**Answer:**

```c
#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>

int isValidFileExtension(const char *filename)
{
    const char *dot = strrchr(filename, '.');

    if (dot != NULL && dot != filename)
    {
        const char *extension = dot + 1;

        if (strcmp(extension, "bin") == 0 ||
            strcmp(extension, "txt") == 0)
        {
            return 1;
        }
    }

    return 0;
}

int main(void)
{
    char filename[100];

    printf("Enter the filename: ");

    if (scanf("%99s", filename) != 1)
        return 1;

    if (!isValidFileExtension(filename))
    {
        printf("Invalid extension. Use .bin or .txt.\n");
        return 1;
    }

    FILE *file = fopen(filename, "rb");

    if (file == NULL)
    {
        printf("File not found or cannot be opened.\n");
        return 1;
    }

    printf("File opened successfully.\n");
    fclose(file);

    return 0;
}
```

- `strrchr()` finds the last occurrence of a character.
- `dot + 1` points to the extension after the dot.
- `strcmp()` compares strings.
- `fopen(filename, "rb")` opens a file in binary read mode.

## 2. Why are pointers used in this program?

**Question:** Why are pointers required?

**Answer:**

1. `const char *dot`: Stores the address of the last dot returned by `strrchr()`.
2. `const char *extension`: Points to the extension within the original filename without copying it.
3. `FILE *file`: Represents the file stream returned by `fopen()`.
4. `S_EMITTER_DATA *pSEmtr`: Points to dynamically allocated memory containing an emitter-data structure.
5. `char *buffer`: Points to dynamically allocated memory used for transferring file data.

Pointers allow functions to access existing data and work with dynamically allocated memory.

## 3. Which fread() loop condition is correct?

**Question:**

```c
while (fread(&SData, sizeof(S_EMITTER_DATA), 1, binaryFile) > 0)
```

or

```c
while (fread(&SData, sizeof(S_EMITTER_DATA), 1, binaryFile) >= 0)
```

**Answer:** The first condition is correct.

```c
while (fread(&SData, sizeof(S_EMITTER_DATA), 1, binaryFile) == 1)
{
    /* Process one complete structure */
}
```

`fread()` returns the number of complete elements successfully read.

When `size = sizeof(S_EMITTER_DATA)` and `count = 1`:

- Return value `1`: One complete structure was read.
- Return value `0`: No complete structure was read, usually because of EOF or a read error.

The `>= 0` condition is incorrect because `fread()` returns an unsigned-size type, so its result is never negative. It would also remain true when the result is zero, potentially causing an infinite loop.

To distinguish EOF from an error:

```c
if (ferror(binaryFile))
    printf("File read error.\n");
else if (feof(binaryFile))
    printf("End of file reached.\n");
```

## 4. How does htonll() work?

**Question:** Explain this function.

```c
#ifdef WIN32

__int64 htonll(__int64 n)
{
#if defined(_M_IX86) || defined(_M_IA64)
    return (((__int64)htonl((unsigned long)n)) << 32)
           + htonl((unsigned long)(n >> 32));
#else
    return n;
#endif
}

#endif
```

**Answer:**

`htonll()` converts a 64-bit integer from host byte order to network byte order.

- `htonl()` converts a 32-bit value from host to network byte order.
- The lower and upper 32-bit portions are rearranged on the relevant Windows architectures.
- On the other branch, the function returns the value unchanged, assuming the host already uses the required byte order.

Network byte order is big-endian.

Important: The implementation must be matched to the actual target architecture. A portable implementation should explicitly detect host endianness rather than relying only on architecture macros.

## 5. How does DoubleEndianSwap() work?

**Question:** Explain the double-precision endian-conversion function.

**Answer:**

```c
void DoubleEndianSwap(double input, double *output)
{
    uint64_t bits;

    if (output == NULL)
        return;

    memcpy(&bits, &input, sizeof(bits));

    /* Apply a suitable 64-bit byte-order conversion here. */
    bits = host_to_big_endian_64(bits);

    memcpy(output, &bits, sizeof(bits));
}
```

`host_to_big_endian_64()` above represents a platform-specific conversion function that must be implemented or replaced with the appropriate platform API.

A `double` normally occupies 64 bits. The function copies its bit representation into a 64-bit integer, converts the byte order, and copies the converted bits back.

Using `memcpy()` avoids strict-aliasing problems associated with directly casting a `double *` to an integer pointer.
