- Heap: commonly supports dynamic allocation
- Stack: commonly supports function calls and automatic locals

Q2. Where is this stored?

    int global_initialized = 10;

Answer: Commonly in .data, which is usually mapped to RAM in embedded systems.

Q3. Where is this stored?

    int global_zero;

Answer: Commonly in .bss, which is usually mapped to RAM.

Q4. Where is this stored?

    void function(void) { }

Answer: Its machine code is commonly in .text, often mapped to flash or executable memory.

Q5. Where is this stored?

    void function(void)
    {
        int local = 10;
    }

Answer: local has automatic storage duration. It may be held in a register or stack slot; its location is not guaranteed by C.

Q6. Where is this stored?

    int *p = malloc(sizeof *p);

Answer: The pointer p has its own storage according to its declaration. The allocated object is dynamically allocated.

Q7. What is the typical embedded startup sequence?

Answer:
1. Reset handler begins execution.
2. Startup code initializes the runtime environment.
3. Initialized writable data is copied from its load image to RAM if required.
4. BSS is zero-initialized.
5. Runtime and hardware initialization occur.
6. main() is called.

Q8. Is every const object stored in flash?

Answer: No. const does not guarantee a particular physical memory section. Placement depends on the implementation and linker configuration.

Q9. What is the difference between RAM and flash?

Answer:
- RAM is volatile working memory.
- Flash is nonvolatile storage and commonly holds program code and initial data images in embedded systems.

============================================================
12. STACK MEMORY — COMPLETE STUDY CHECKLIST
============================================================

Study these topics in order:

1. Function calls and returns
2. Call stack
3. Stack frames
4. Stack pointer (SP)
5. Frame pointer (FP)
6. Return addresses
7. Saved registers
8. Local variables and parameters
9. Calling conventions
10. Function prologue and epilogue
11. Recursion
12. Stack growth direction on the target
13. Stack overflow
14. Interrupt stack usage
15. Nested interrupts
16. Task-specific stacks in an RTOS
17. Stack high-water marks
18. Stack corruption
19. Buffer overflow
20. Dangling pointers to expired local objects
21. Assembly inspection
22. Debugging with a debugger

Q1. What is a stack frame?

Answer: The storage and machine state associated with a function invocation.

Q2. Does every local variable necessarily live on the stack?

Answer: No. A compiler may keep variables in registers or optimize them away.

Q3. What causes stack overflow?

Answer: Excessive recursion, large local objects, deep call chains, interrupt nesting or insufficient task-stack sizing.

Q4. Why is stack overflow dangerous in embedded systems?

Answer: It can corrupt adjacent memory, another task's stack or critical control data.

============================================================
13. HEAP MEMORY — COMPLETE STUDY CHECKLIST
============================================================

1. malloc()
2. calloc()
3. realloc()
4. free()
5. Allocation failure
6. Allocation metadata
7. Free lists and allocator strategies
8. Internal fragmentation
9. External fragmentation
10. Memory leaks
11. Double free
12. Invalid free
13. Use-after-free
14. Ownership
15. Lifetime
16. Alignment
17. Allocation overhead
18. Thread safety of allocators
19. Deterministic allocation
20. Fixed-size memory pools
21. Heap corruption debugging
22. Embedded allocation policies

Q1. What is the difference between malloc() and calloc()?

Answer:
- malloc() allocates storage without initializing its bytes.
- calloc() allocates storage and initializes all bits to zero.

Q2. What does realloc() do?

Answer: It attempts to resize an allocation and may move it. If it fails for a nonzero requested size, the original allocation remains valid.

Safer pattern:

    int *tmp = realloc(p, new_count * sizeof *p);

    if (tmp != NULL) {
        p = tmp;
    }

Q3. Why must allocation sizes be checked?

Answer: Incorrect size calculations can cause integer overflow or allocate less storage than required.

============================================================
14. POINTERS AND MEMORY RELATIONSHIPS
============================================================

Study:
1. Address-of operator (&)
2. Dereference operator (*)
3. Pointer initialization
4. Null pointers
5. Pointer arithmetic
6. Pointer to pointer
7. Function pointers
8. Void pointers
9. Array decay
10. Pointer versus array
11. const int *p
12. int * const p
13. int * const *p
14. Pointer aliasing
15. restrict
16. Object lifetime
17. Pointer provenance and valid access
18. Alignment
19. Out-of-bounds access
20. Dangling pointers
21. Wild pointers
22. Pointer comparisons
23. Function-pointer callbacks
24. Struct pointers
25. Dynamic arrays

Q1. What is the difference between const int *p and int * const p?

Answer:
- const int *p: the pointed-to int cannot be modified through p.
- int * const p: p cannot be reassigned to another address.

Q2. What does restrict mean in C?

Answer: It is a promise about access to an object through restricted pointer expressions, enabling certain compiler optimizations. Violating the required aliasing conditions can cause undefined behavior.

============================================================
15. STRUCTURE ALIGNMENT AND PADDING
============================================================

Study:
1. Alignment requirements
2. Member padding
3. Tail padding
4. Structure size
5. offsetof()
6. Nested structures
7. Arrays of structures
8. Structure packing
9. Bit-fields
10. ABI layout
11. Cache locality
12. Unaligned accesses
13. Binary protocol layout
14. Hardware register layout
15. Portable serialization

Example:

    struct Example {
        char c;
        int i;
        char d;
    };

Q1. Why may sizeof(struct Example) be larger than the sum of its member sizes?

Answer: The implementation may insert padding between members and at the end to satisfy alignment.

Q2. How do you inspect a member offset?

Answer:

    printf("%zu\n", offsetof(struct Example, i));

Include <stddef.h>.

============================================================
16. UNION MEMORY
============================================================

Study:
1. Shared storage
2. Union size and alignment
3. Member access
4. Object representations
5. Tagged unions
6. Union versus structure
7. Endianness experiments
8. Strict aliasing considerations
9. Hardware register unions
10. Portability

Example:

    union Value {
        uint32_t u32;
        unsigned char bytes[4];
    };

Q1. Do the union members have separate storage?

Answer: No. They overlap in storage.

Q2. Is a union a portable serialization format?

Answer: No. Endianness, padding, representation and alignment may differ across platforms.

============================================================
17. EMBEDDED C — LOW-LEVEL TOPICS
============================================================

Study:
1. volatile
2. const volatile
3. Memory-mapped I/O
4. Register access
5. Bit masks
6. Interrupt service routines
7. Callback functions
8. UART
9. SPI
10. I2C
11. CAN
12. DMA
13. Cache coherency
14. Interrupt-safe data structures
15. Circular buffers
16. State machines
17. Watchdog timers
18. Bootloaders
19. Linker scripts
20. Startup code
21. Fixed-size memory pools
22. Real-time constraints
23. Race conditions
24. Atomic operations
25. RTOS queues and task stacks

Q1. What does volatile do?

Answer: It tells the compiler that accesses to the volatile object are observable side effects and must follow the implementation's volatile-access rules. It does not make operations atomic or provide thread synchronization.

Q2. What is const volatile used for?

Answer: A value that software should not modify through that lvalue but whose value can change externally, such as a hardware status register.

Q3. What is memory-mapped I/O?

Answer: Hardware registers are accessed through addresses in the processor's address space, using platform-defined access widths and semantics.

Q4. Why is volatile not enough for thread synchronization?

Answer: It does not establish the required atomicity or happens-before relationships. Use C atomics or suitable synchronization primitives.

============================================================
18. C++ OOP CONCEPTS WITH EXAMPLES
============================================================

1. CLASS AND OBJECT

    class Motor {
    public:
        int speed = 0;

        void start() {
            // Start motor.
        }
    };

    int main() {
        Motor m;
        m.speed = 100;
        m.start();
    }

Answer: Motor is a class; m is an object.

2. ENCAPSULATION

    class Motor {
    private:
        int speed = 0;

    public:
        void setSpeed(int s) {
            speed = s;
        }

        int getSpeed() const {
            return speed;
        }
    };

Answer: Private data is accessed through the public interface.

3. ABSTRACTION

    class UART {
    public:
        void sendData() {
            initialize();
            // Send data.
        }

    private:
        void initialize() {
            // Hardware initialization.
        }
    };

Answer: The public interface hides internal implementation details.

4. INHERITANCE

    class Vehicle {
    public:
        void start() {}
    };

    class Car : public Vehicle {
    public:
        void drive() {}
    };

Answer: Car inherits the public interface of Vehicle.

5. MULTIPLE INHERITANCE

    class Camera {
    public:
        void capture() {}
    };

    class GPS {
    public:
        void locate() {}
    };

    class Robot : public Camera, public GPS {};

Answer: Robot inherits from both Camera and GPS.

6. POLYMORPHISM

    class Sensor {
    public:
        virtual void read() = 0;
        virtual ~Sensor() = default;
    };

    class TemperatureSensor : public Sensor {
    public:
        void read() override {}
    };

Answer: Different derived types can provide different implementations of the same virtual interface.

7. FUNCTION OVERLOADING

    int add(int a, int b) {
        return a + b;
    }

    float add(float a, float b) {
        return a + b;
    }

Answer: The compiler selects an overload using the argument types.

8. OPERATOR OVERLOADING

    class Number {
    public:
        int value;

        Number(int v) : value(v) {}

        Number operator+(const Number& other) const {
            return Number(value + other.value);
        }
    };

Answer: The + operator is defined for Number objects.

9. CONSTRUCTOR

    class Test {
    public:
        Test() {}
    };

Answer: A constructor initializes an object when it is created.

10. COPY CONSTRUCTOR

    class Test {
    public:
        int x;

        Test(int v) : x(v) {}
        Test(const Test& other) : x(other.x) {}
    };

Answer: The copy constructor initializes a new object from an existing object.

11. DESTRUCTOR

    class Test {
    public:
        ~Test() {}
    };

Answer: The destructor performs cleanup when the object's lifetime ends.

12. THIS POINTER

    class Test {
    public:
        int x;

        void set(int x) {
            this->x = x;
        }
    };

Answer: this refers to the current object in a non-static member function.

13. STATIC MEMBER

    class Test {
    public:
        inline static int count = 0;
    };

Answer: A static data member belongs to the class rather than to each individual object. The inline variable form requires C++17 or later.

14. FRIEND FUNCTION

    class Test {
        int x = 10;
        friend int getValue(const Test&);
    };

    int getValue(const Test& t) {
        return t.x;
    }

Answer: A friend function can access private and protected members.

15. VIRTUAL FUNCTION

    class Base {
    public:
        virtual void show() {}
        virtual ~Base() = default;
    };

    class Derived : public Base {
    public:
        void show() override {}
    };

Answer: A virtual call through an appropriate base pointer or reference can dispatch to the derived override.

16. PURE VIRTUAL FUNCTION

    class Sensor {
    public:
        virtual void read() = 0;
        virtual ~Sensor() = default;
    };

Answer: A pure virtual function makes Sensor abstract unless the class has other language-specific circumstances affecting abstractness.

17. OBJECT SLICING

    Derived d;
    Base b = d;

Answer: Copying a derived object into a base object by value discards the derived-specific portion.

18. RTTI

Answer: Runtime type information supports operations such as typeid and dynamic_cast for suitable polymorphic class hierarchies.

19. VIRTUAL DESTRUCTOR

Answer: A polymorphic base class generally needs a virtual destructor when objects may be deleted through base-class pointers.

20. TEMPLATES

    template <typename T>
    T add(T a, T b) {
        return a + b;
    }

Answer: Templates enable generic functions and classes.

21. EXCEPTION HANDLING

    try {
        throw 10;
    } catch (int x) {
        // Handle x.
    }

Answer: Exceptions transfer control to a matching handler. Some embedded projects disable exceptions by policy.

22. NAMESPACE

    namespace Device {
        int status = 0;
    }

Answer: A namespace groups names and helps prevent naming conflicts.

23. SMART POINTER

    #include <memory>

    auto p = std::make_unique<int>(10);

Answer: unique_ptr manages exclusive ownership and releases the object automatically.

============================================================
19. C++ OBJECT MEMORY LAYOUT
============================================================

Study:
1. Non-static data members
2. Padding and alignment
3. Empty classes
4. Base-class subobjects
5. Multiple inheritance
6. Virtual inheritance
7. vptr and vtable implementation models
8. Virtual function dispatch
9. RTTI
10. Object slicing
11. Constructor order
12. Destructor order
13. Copy constructor
14. Move constructor
15. Rule of three, five and zero
16. new and delete
17. Placement new
18. RAII
19. Smart-pointer control blocks
20. ABI and name mangling

Q1. Where is a vptr stored?

Answer: Many C++ implementations place a hidden virtual-table pointer in polymorphic objects, but the C++ standard does not mandate a particular vptr or vtable layout.

Q2. What is a vtable?

Answer: A common compiler implementation is a table of virtual-dispatch-related entries used to support virtual function calls.

Q3. What is RAII?

Answer: Resource Acquisition Is Initialization. A resource's lifetime is tied to an object's lifetime, so cleanup happens through destruction.

Q4. What is the difference between new and malloc?

Answer:
- new allocates storage and initializes or constructs an object.
- malloc allocates raw storage and returns void*.
- new throws an exception on failure by default; malloc returns a null pointer on failure.
- Use matching allocation and deallocation mechanisms.

Q5. What is the difference between unique_ptr and shared_ptr?

Answer:
- unique_ptr provides exclusive ownership.
- shared_ptr supports shared ownership through reference counting.
- weak_ptr observes a shared object without keeping it alive.

============================================================
20. C++ MULTITHREADING AND PERFORMANCE
============================================================

Study:
1. std::thread
2. Mutex
3. Lock guard
4. Unique lock
5. Condition variable
6. Atomic operations
7. Memory ordering
8. Race conditions
9. Deadlocks
10. Producer-consumer
11. False sharing
12. Cache locality
13. Move semantics
14. Copy elision
15. STL containers
16. Iterator invalidation
17. vector growth
18. map versus unordered_map
19. Allocators
20. Profiling

Q1. What is the difference between atomics and mutexes?

Answer:
- Atomics support indivisible operations on specific objects.
- Mutexes protect critical sections and can guard multiple related variables.
- Choose based on correctness, contention and complexity.

Q2. What is a data race in C++?

Answer: Conflicting unsynchronized accesses from different threads, at least one of which modifies the same memory location, can cause undefined behavior.

Q3. Why is cache locality important?

Answer: Accessing nearby data can reduce cache misses and improve performance.

Q4. What is false sharing?

Answer: Different threads modify separate variables that occupy the same cache line, causing unnecessary cache-coherence traffic.

============================================================
22. CIRCULAR BUFFER — EMBEDDED CODING
============================================================

Q1. Why use a circular buffer for UART?

Answer: It reuses a fixed-size array to store incoming or outgoing bytes without shifting the remaining data after every operation.

Q2. What is wrap-around?

Answer: When the index reaches the end of the buffer, it returns to the beginning.

Example for a buffer whose size is a power of two:

    next = (index + 1) & (SIZE - 1);

Answer: This works only when SIZE is a nonzero power of two and the index is valid.

Q3. What must be defined for a circular buffer?

Answer:
- Buffer storage and capacity
- Head and tail indices
- Full/empty convention
- Overflow policy
- Synchronization between producer and consumer
- Whether it is used in an ISR or multiple tasks

============================================================
23. COMPILER AND BUILD PROCESS
============================================================

1. Preprocessing
2. Compilation
3. Assembly
4. Linking
5. Object files
6. Symbol tables
7. Static and dynamic libraries

10. Debug symbols
11. Optimization levels
12. Undefined behavior
13. Compiler warnings
14. ABI
15. Cross-compilation
16. Map files

Q1. What are the major build stages?

Answer:
Source code -> preprocessing -> compilation -> assembly -> linking -> executable or firmware image.

Q2. What does a linker script do in embedded development?

Answer: It defines memory regions and controls how sections are placed into the target memory map.

24. DEBUGGING AND INTERVIEW PRACTICE

Study these failure patterns:

1. Null-pointer dereference
2. Out-of-bounds array access
3. Use-after-free
4. Double free
5. Memory leak
6. Stack overflow
7. Incorrect sizeof usage
8. Signed/unsigned conversion
9. Invalid pointer arithmetic
10. Missing string terminator
11. Buffer overflow
12. Incorrect structure alignment assumptions
13. Data race
14. Deadlock
15. ISR/task race
16. DMA/cache-coherency problems
17. Wrong register access width
18. Incorrect interrupt handling

20. Incorrect linker memory placement

Useful tools:
- GCC or Clang warnings
- AddressSanitizer where supported
- UndefinedBehaviorSanitizer where supported
- Valgrind on supported host systems
- GDB
- Disassembly and compiler-generated assembly
- Embedded debugger and hardware watchpoints
- Linker map files

============================================================
25. FINAL MEMORY MASTERY CHECKLIST
============================================================

Before claiming mastery, you should be able to answer all of these without notes:

1. What is a memory address?
2. What are RAM, ROM and flash?
3. What are .text, .rodata, .data and .bss?
4. What is static storage duration?
5. What is automatic storage duration?
6. What is dynamic allocation?
7. Is stack allocation static or runtime?
8. What is a stack frame?
9. What is a stack pointer?
10. What is a frame pointer?
11. What causes stack overflow?
12. How does recursion consume stack?
13. What are malloc, calloc, realloc and free?
14. What is heap fragmentation?
15. What is a memory leak?
16. What is a dangling pointer?
17. What is a wild pointer?
18. What is use-after-free?
19. How does pointer arithmetic work?
20. What is array decay?
21. How are two-dimensional arrays laid out?
22. What is endianness?
23. What is alignment?
24. What is structure padding?
25. What is a union?
26. What does volatile guarantee?
27. What is memory-mapped I/O?
28. What are DMA and cache coherency?
29. How does a C++ object differ from a C structure?
30. How do virtual functions commonly work?
31. What are vptr and vtable?
32. What is object slicing?
33. What is RAII?
34. What are smart pointers?
35. What is the difference between atomic and mutex?
36. What is cache locality?
37. What is false sharing?
38. How does startup code initialize RAM?
39. How does a linker script place program sections?
40. How do you debug memory corruption?

FINAL STUDY RULE:

For every topic, write a small C or C++ program, draw its memory layout, reason about object lifetime and ownership, test boundary conditions, compile with warnings, and inspect the behavior using a debugger. Distinguish what the language standard guarantees from what a particular compiler, operating system or MCU implements.
